  • Copying the slide's geometric inverse \(\lceil \ln u/\ln(1-p) \rceil\) into a Q-Q plot. It returns the \(1-u\) quantile, so your points come out in reverse order. Use \(\ln(1-u)\).
  • Mixing up the exponential's mean \(a\) (slides) and rate \(\lambda = 1/a\) (study guide). \(-a\ln(1-u)\) and \(-\ln(1-u)/\lambda\) are the same thing.
  • Small samples wiggle off the line even when the model is right. Judge the overall pattern, especially in the tails where points are sparse.

Reporting variability

The idea

The slides' summary is "avoid means if possible" because a mean loses information (Summ p.32). The reporting variability handout turns that into practice. The number that describes how much the measurements varied is the one that tells a reader how far to trust the headline number, and it's the one most often left out.

The handout's main rule is to report a spread whose meaning matches the claim. If you're claiming something about a typical run, report the spread of the data itself, like the standard deviation, IQR or percentiles. If you're claiming something about how precisely you estimated a mean, report a confidence interval for that mean. A figure labelled only "error bars" tells the reader neither.

What to know

MeasureAnswersFails when
Mean ± SDHow far individual runs sit from the meanSkewed or heavy-tailed data, where mean − SD can go below possible values
Standard error \(s/\sqrt{n}\)How precisely the mean is estimatedPresented as if it described the data, since it shrinks with \(\sqrt{n}\) and the data's spread doesn't
Confidence intervalWhich values of the summary fit the dataRead as containing 95% of the data
Percentiles (p95, p99)What the tail looks likeToo few samples (a p99.9 needs thousands)
IQR, quartilesWhere the middle half sits, robust to outliersThe tail is the actual finding
Min, max, rangeWorst case seenUsed as a stability claim, since the range grows with sample size
COVSpread relative to size, across different scalesMean near zero, or heavy tails
Median absolute deviationTypical deviation, robust to contaminationRarely fails, mostly just underused
Interquartile meanA center that ignores both tails, good with few runs and wild outliersThe tails are what you're studying
Box, violin, raincloud plots, CDF or CCDFThe whole shape, more than any single numberBox plots with unstated whisker rules, CCDFs without log axes
Number of runsHow much evidence there isOmitted, which makes every other measure uninterpretable

Hoefler and Belli's rules quoted in the handout. Rule 5 says to report whether values are deterministic and give confidence intervals when they aren't. Rule 6 says not to assume normality without a diagnostic check, which is what the HW3 Q-Q plots are. Rule 7 says to compare nondeterministic data soundly, e.g. with non-overlapping confidence intervals or ANOVA. Rule 8 says to ask whether a mean or median is even the right summary, because a worst-case latency claim needs a high percentile.

Good examples from the handout include Parendi, which prints the maximum next to its 2.8× geomean and gives the full per-benchmark table, and the Aurora paper, which uses a harmonic mean for a rate (TEPS) and an arithmetic mean for times, stating the number of repetitions each time.

Paper in the handoutWhat it shows you how to report
Agarwal et al., deep RL (NeurIPS 2021)Means over 3 to 5 seeds can reverse rankings. Use the interquartile mean, performance profiles and stratified bootstrap CIs, which hold up with few runs
Georges, Buytaert and Eeckhout, Java (OOPSLA 2007)JIT, GC and runtime noise make a single best or mean time indefensible. Use CIs for comparisons and violin plots to show bimodality and skew
Serverless variability (2023)Variability is the subject. Show the empirical CDF first, then time series with CIs, then seasonal decomposition
Post-quantum timing side channels (2025)Pair pictures (histograms with KDE, ECDFs, violins) with numbers (Welch t, K-S distance, Cliff's delta)
Miller, error bars for LLM evals (2024)Standard errors from the CLT, clustered SEs when questions come in related groups, and power analysis for sample size
Arcuri and Briand, randomized SE tools (ICSE 2011)Many independent runs, a non-parametric test, and the Vargha-Delaney \(\hat{A}_{12}\) effect size, the chance one tool's run beats the other's

When a paper gives you only a geomean speedup, the study guide (F2) says to ask for three things. Ask for the per-benchmark raw times, so you can recompute any summary including total time. Ask for a spread (range, COV or a box plot), since 1.3× spanning 0.4× to 9× is a different result from 1.3× spanning 1.2× to 1.4×. Ask how benchmarks were chosen and whether any were dropped. Two systems with the same mean can still differ in spread (SD or COV), in the tail (p90, max, box plot) or in shape, such as unimodal against bimodal (histogram or Q-Q plot), which is study guide F3.

Worked example

You time a request 25 times and get \(\bar{x} = 40\) ms and \(s = 10\) ms.

Standard error \(= s/\sqrt{n} = 10/\sqrt{25} = 10/5 = 2\) ms. If you ran 100 times instead and \(s\) stayed near 10, the SE would be \(10/10 = 1\) ms. The SE halved because you know the mean better, but individual requests are just as spread out. Writing "40 ± 2 ms" without saying it's an SE makes the system look four times steadier than it is.

Now a latency set with mean 4 ms and \(s = 6\) ms. Mean − SD \(= 4 - 6 = -2\) ms, an impossible latency. That tells you the data are right-skewed, so "mean ± SD" is the wrong summary. Report the median with the IQR or SIQR, and the p95 or p99 if the claim is about the tail. This matches the slide rule on p.49 for unbounded, skewed data.

Easy to get wrong

  • Unlabelled error bars. Always say SD, SE or CI, and give \(n\).
  • Reporting a bare geomean speedup with no spread. Give the max or min beside it, or the per-benchmark table.
  • Using the range to argue a system is stable. More runs make the range grow on their own.

Check yourself

1. A researcher adds two features, each built on the previous one. The first improves quality by 20% and the second by 87.5%. Which mean gives the improvement per feature, and what is it?

Geometric, because each gain applies on top of the last, so the factors multiply (Summ p.30). Product \(= 1.2 \times 1.875 = 2.25\), and \(\sqrt{2.25} = 1.5\), so 50% per feature. Check that \(1.5 \times 1.5 = 2.25\). The arithmetic mean of 53.75% would overstate it. Same setup as HW3 1(a).

2. Five compiler runs on similar-sized inputs take 100, 140, 120, 160 and 180 ms. Which mean, and what value?

Arithmetic, because these are times and their sum is the total time, so the mean should be directly proportional to it (Summ p.25, p.27). \((100+140+120+160+180)/5 = 700/5 = 140\) ms. Same setup as HW3 1(b).

3. A database runs the same workload three times and measures 40, 60 and 120 transactions per second. Which mean, and what value?

Harmonic, because it's a rate and the mean should be inversely proportional to total time (Summ p.28). With common denominator 120, \(1/40 + 1/60 + 1/120 = 3/120 + 2/120 + 1/120 = 6/120 = 1/20\). So \(x_H = 3/(1/20) = 60\) tps. The arithmetic mean, 73.3, would overstate it. Same setup as HW3 1(c).

4. A program spends 60 s in each of two phases. An optimization makes phase 1 take 20 s and leaves phase 2 alone. Find the arithmetic, geometric and harmonic means of the two speedups and the true overall speedup.

Speedups are \(60/20 = 3\) and \(60/60 = 1\). AM \(= 2\). GM \(= \sqrt{3} \approx 1.73\). HM \(= 2/(1/3 + 1) = 2/(4/3) = 1.5\). The true speedup is \(120/80 = 1.5\). The harmonic mean matches because the phases had equal baseline time (study guide B2, Eeckhout's equal-time speedup in the means handout). The geometric mean still overstates it.

5. For 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, compute the sample variance, standard deviation and COV.

Mean \(= 35/5 = 7\). Deviations are −4, −2, 0, 2, 4 and their squares sum to \(16+4+0+4+16 = 40\). \(s^2 = 40/4 = 10\), \(s = \sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\), COV \(\approx 3.16/7 \approx 0.45\). Divide by \(n-1\), not \(n\) (Summ p.39-41).

6. Sorted data 4, 7, 8, 10, 12, 15, 16, 18, 21, 30. Find Q1, the median, Q3, the IQR and the SIQR with the course convention.

\(n = 10\), position \(r = 10p + 0.5\). Q1 has \(r = 3\), so \(Q_1 = x_3 = 8\). The median has \(r = 5.5\), so \((12+15)/2 = 13.5\). Q3 has \(r = 8\), so \(Q_3 = x_8 = 18\). IQR \(= 10\), SIQR \(= 5\) (Summ p.45, p.59). Excel's PERCENTILE would give Q1 at position \(1 + 9(0.25) = 3.25\), which is 8.5, so don't copy the method from p.43.

7. How many histogram bins does Sturges' rule give for 64 points? For 1000?

\(1 + \log_2 64 = 1 + 6 = 7\). For 1000, \(1 + \log_2 1000 \approx 10.97\), which is 10 with the floor written on Summ p.54 and 11 with the rounding HW3 and the study guide used. Say which you're doing. Bin width matters because the same data can look different with different cells (p.56).

8. Data set 1 is 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and data set 2 is 7, 9, 11, 13, 15. What does their Q-Q plot look like, and do they have the same distribution shape?

Each quantile of set 2 is \(2x + 5\) of the matching quantile of set 1, so the points lie exactly on a line with slope 2 and intercept 5. Linear quantile functions mean equal shape (Summ p.61). The slope shows twice the scale and the intercept shows a shift of 5.

9. On a normal probability plot (data against z), the lowest points fall below the line and the highest points rise above it. What does that mean? What if the curve bows the same way at both ends?

Points falling below at the low end and rising above at the high end mean the data reach further out than a normal in both directions, so longer tails than normal (Summ p.71). If both ends sit on the same side of the line and the middle on the other, the plot is bowed, which means asymmetry (p.73). Bowed upward like HW3's not-normal.txt means right skew.

10. Pick an index of dispersion for (i) a cache hit ratio over many intervals, (ii) response times on a loaded server, (iii) repeated timings that look symmetric and unimodal.

(i) Hit ratios are bounded in [0, 1], so the range. (ii) Response times are unbounded and right-skewed, so percentiles or SIQR. (iii) Unbounded, unimodal and symmetric, so COV. This is the rule on Summ p.49.

11. A paper reports latency as "40 ms ± 2 ms" from 25 runs with no other detail. What two things would you ask, and why?

First, whether the 2 ms is an SD, an SE or a CI half-width, since those answer different questions. If it's an SE, the SD is \(2 \times \sqrt{25} = 10\) ms, so individual runs vary five times more than the figure suggests. Second, the distribution or high percentiles, because latency is usually right-skewed and a mean ± SD can describe a distribution no user sees (reporting variability handout, Hoefler and Belli rule 8).

12. Why does the HW3 Q-Q plot rescale each z with \(m + s z\), and what would the line look like without that step?

Rescaling puts the theoretical quantiles in the data's units, so normal data land on the 45° line \(y = x\) (Summ p.69). Without it you plot data against standard z, and a normal sample falls on a line whose intercept is about the mean and slope about the standard deviation (p.70). Either way the test is straightness.

13. A box plot's five-number summary is 4.26, 6.08, 7.35, 8.33, 12.74. Find the IQR, SIQR and midhinge, and say whether the maximum is an outlier by Tukey's rule.

IQR \(= 8.33 - 6.08 = 2.25\), SIQR \(= 1.125\), midhinge \(= (6.08 + 8.33)/2 = 7.205\). The upper fence is \(8.33 + 1.5(2.25) = 11.705\), and 12.74 is above it, so yes. This is the Summ p.50 data (study guide D1). The midhinge is below the median 7.35, which hints at left skew in the middle half.

14. A theoretical Q-Q plot against Exp(\(\lambda = 0.5\)), with theory on \(x\) and data on \(y\), is a clean straight line through the origin with slope 2. What do you conclude?

The straight line means the exponential shape fits (Summ p.63). The slope of 2 means the data are twice as spread as Exp(0.5), whose mean is 2. So the data look exponential with mean about 4, that is \(\lambda \approx 0.25\). A wrong parameter changes the slope, not the straightness (study guide E1).

15. For a geometric distribution with \(p = 0.5\), what is the 0.75 quantile, and what goes wrong if you use the slide's inverse?

Solve \(1 - 0.5^x \ge 0.75\), so \(0.5^x \le 0.25\) and \(x = \lceil \ln 0.25/\ln 0.5 \rceil = 2\). Check that \(F(2) = 1 - 0.25 = 0.75\). The slide's \(\lceil \ln u/\ln(1-p) \rceil\) on Summ p.64 gives \(\lceil \ln 0.75/\ln 0.5 \rceil = \lceil 0.415 \rceil = 1\), which is the 0.25 quantile. That form is fine for generating random values but reverses a Q-Q plot.

16. Hit ratios at three cache levels are 0.90, 0.80 and 0.50. Which mean, what is it, and what does the product mean?

Geometric, because effects across levels multiply (Summ p.30). The product \(0.90 \times 0.80 \times 0.50 = 0.36\) is the compounded pass-through fraction, and \(0.36^{1/3} \approx 0.711\). Check by cubing, \(0.71^3 = 0.358\), just under 0.36 (study guide B4).

Confidence intervals

Slides: CI (Lecture 6, Confidence Intervals), plus the confidence-intervals study guide, the Confidence Intervals in the Wild handout and the Monte Carlo handout (Law and Kelton p.90-91). Homework: HW4 (Problems 1 and 2 are the mechanics, Problem 3 is the paper audit).

Errors, accuracy, precision and resolution

The idea

Every measurement you take is the true value plus some error. A confidence interval exists to put a number on that error, but it can only see one kind of it. So before any formula, you need to know which errors repetition can fix and which it can't.

Systematic errors push every measurement the same way, like a clock that drifts with temperature or forgetting to clear the cache before each timing run. Random errors go up or down with equal chance and come from the tool's limits, the person reading it, or random processes inside the system. Repeating the experiment averages random error away and leaves systematic error exactly where it was.

The study guide's one-line summary is worth memorising. A narrow interval is a claim about precision only, so you can be very precise about a wrong number.

What to know

Systematic error comes from an experimental mistake, gives a constant or slowly varying bias, and is controlled by the experimenter's skill (CI p.7). Random error is unpredictable and unbiased, usually can't be controlled, and is what statistics characterises (p.8).

Timer quantization is the standard example of random error. The same event can be reported as 13 or 14 clock ticks depending on where it starts relative to the tick, so a measurement is really \(X \pm \Delta\) (p.9-10).

The error model (p.11-15). Suppose each of \(n\) independent error sources adds \(+E\) or \(-E\) with probability \(\tfrac12\) each. The chance of a given measured value equals the number of paths to it divided by \(2^n\), which is a binomial distribution. As \(n\) grows the binomial turns into the normal, which is why measurements form a bell curve and why the rest of the lecture assumes normal random errors (p.21).

TermCaused byMeans
AccuracySystematic errorsHow close the mean of the measurements is to the true value
PrecisionRandom errorsHow repeatable the measurements are
ResolutionThe toolSmallest increment between measured values

That table is (p.16-17). Accuracy is hard to know because you need an outside standard, resolution depends on the tool, and precision is the one you quantify with statistics (p.18). That last point is the whole reason for the rest of this section.

Why statistics at all (p.3-6). Statistics is the branch of maths that deals with collecting, analysing, interpreting and presenting masses of numerical data, and this course cares about the analysis and interpretation part (p.3). A statistic is a quantity computed from a sample, and it serves as an estimate of a population parameter. Your measurements are a sample of the process you're measuring, so you use statistics for two jobs. One is to infer the true value of the metric. The other is to quantify how much imprecision the random errors left in that estimate.

Turning systematic error into random error (study guide Q17). You can't average away a bias, but you can sometimes spread it out so it acts like noise. Randomize the order of the configurations instead of running all of A and then all of B, so any drift hits both equally. Interleave the runs of each configuration across hours and days, so time-of-day effects on a shared machine get averaged instead of confounded with the comparison. Stabilizer does the same thing to code and heap layout (Wild handout, entry 5). None of this fixes a bias that hits every configuration equally, like a clock source that always under-reports, because randomizing only turns differences between conditions into noise.

Warm-up is also systematic. The first iterations of a JIT or a simulation run are slow for a reason you know, so you delete them before computing anything (study guide Q18, Wild handout entries 4 and 15).

Worked example

Three independent error sources, each \(\pm E\) with probability \(\tfrac12\). What measured values can you get and how likely is each?

There are \(2^3 = 8\) equally likely paths. To land on \(x - 3E\) all three errors must be \(-E\), which is 1 path. To land on \(x - E\) you need two minus and one plus, and the plus can be any of the 3 sources, so 3 paths. By symmetry \(x + E\) has 3 paths and \(x + 3E\) has 1.

\[ \Pr(x-3E) = \tfrac18,\quad \Pr(x-E) = \tfrac38,\quad \Pr(x+E) = \tfrac38,\quad \Pr(x+3E) = \tfrac18 \]

Check that they add to \(\tfrac{1+3+3+1}{8} = 1\). The counts 1, 3, 3, 1 are row 3 of Pascal's triangle, so the shape is binomial, and it already peaks in the middle. With two sources you get the \(\tfrac14, \tfrac12, \tfrac14\) bars on (p.12-13).

Easy to get wrong

  • Calling a narrow interval "accurate". Narrow means precise. A biased setup gives a tight interval around the wrong value, and more runs only make it tighter (study guide, and the Mytkowicz entry in the Wild handout).
  • Mixing up resolution and precision. Resolution is a property of the tool. Precision is how much repeated readings scatter.
  • Thinking quantization is systematic. On (p.9) it is listed as random error because the start of the event relative to the tick is unpredictable.
  • Thinking randomization removes bias. It converts differences between conditions into random error, but an offset shared by every condition stays exactly where it was.

Sampling distribution, estimators and the central limit theorem

The idea

Your measurements are a sample, and the true value you want is a population parameter. A statistic like \(\bar x\) is your estimate of it (CI p.4-5). If you could repeat the whole experiment many times, each repeat would give a different \(\bar x\). Those values have their own distribution, called the sampling distribution of the mean, and it's much narrower than the spread of single measurements.

The central limit theorem says what that distribution looks like. For large samples of independent values it's normal, centred on \(\mu\), with standard deviation \(\sigma/\sqrt n\). Every interval in this lecture is built from that fact.

What to know

Point versus interval estimate (CI p.22-23). A point estimate is a single best guess, like \(\bar x\). An interval estimate \((a, b)\) comes with a stated confidence, the probability that the procedure correctly captures the parameter.

Point estimator properties (p.23). Unbiased means the average of the statistic over all possible samples of size \(n\) equals the parameter. Efficient means precise as an estimator. Consistent means it gets closer to the parameter as \(n\) grows.

The sample mean is unbiased (p.24) because \[ E[\bar X] = \frac{E\left[\sum_{i=1}^n X_i\right]}{n} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n E[X_i]}{n} = \frac{n\mu}{n} = \mu. \]

The tables on (p.25-26) draw samples from the same skewed population HW4 Problem 2 uses (mean 0.2083, variance 0.0440). At \(n = 15\) the three sample means average to 0.2643, an error of 26.9%. At \(n = 87\) they average to 0.2206, an error of 5.9%. That's consistency in action. The sample variance row shrinks the same way. The three variances average 0.0639 at \(n = 15\), which is 45.3% off the population's 0.0440, and 0.0459 at \(n = 87\), which is only 4.3% off. The row labelled "efficiency" there is really the relative error \(|\bar x - \mu|/\mu\) of each sample.

Central limit theorem (p.29). If the observations are independent and come from one population with mean \(\mu\) and standard deviation \(\sigma\), then for large \(n\) \[ \bar x \sim N\!\left(\mu,\ \sigma/\sqrt n\right) \] where the slide writes the second argument as a standard deviation. The standard deviation of the sample mean, \(\sigma/\sqrt n\), is the standard error.

On (p.30) the slide cuts \(\alpha/2\) off each tail of that curve. With \(z_{\alpha/2}\) the critical value of \(N(0,1)\) for an upper-tail area of \(\alpha/2\), \[ c_1 = \mu - \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} z_{\alpha/2}, \qquad c_2 = \mu + \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt n} z_{\alpha/2}. \] So \(\bar x\) lands in \((c_1, c_2)\) with probability \(1-\alpha\). Flip it around and you get the interval for \(\mu\).

How large is large? A later slide says the sum of a "large number" of values is normal, typically assumed to be more than about 6 or 7 values (p.64). That's a rule about how fast averages become normal for well-behaved data. It is a different rule from the \(n > 30\) cutoff for using \(z\) instead of \(t\) in the next topic.

Monte Carlo estimates are sample means too (Monte Carlo handout, Law and Kelton p.90-91). To estimate \(I = \int_a^b g(x)\,dx\), draw \(X_1, \dots, X_n\) independent and uniform on \([a,b]\) and set \(Y_i = (b-a)g(X_i)\). Then \(E(Y) = I\), so \[ \bar Y(n) = (b-a)\frac{\sum_{i=1}^n g(X_i)}{n} \] is an unbiased estimator of \(I\), and \(\mathrm{Var}[\bar Y(n)] = \mathrm{Var}(Y)/n\), so it's also consistent. The handout's table estimates \(\int_0^\pi \sin x\,dx = 2\) and gets 2.213 at \(n = 10\) but 1.993 at \(n = 160\). Since \(\bar Y(n)\) is just a mean of independent values, the usual mean interval gives its precision, and the sample-size formula tells you how many draws you need. HW4 Problem 2c part 5 is the same idea pointed at a method, because checking an interval's coverage means simulating many samples and counting hits.

Worked example

Response times have \(\sigma = 12\) ms. You take \(n = 36\) independent measurements. Where will \(\bar x\) fall 95% of the time?

Standard error is \(\sigma/\sqrt n = 12/\sqrt{36} = 12/6 = 2\) ms. For 95%, \(\alpha = 0.05\) and \(z_{0.025} = 1.96\). So \(\bar x\) lands within \(1.96 \times 2 = 3.92\) ms of \(\mu\) with probability 0.95.

Now take \(n = 144\). The standard error becomes \(12/\sqrt{144} = 12/12 = 1\) ms and the band shrinks to \(\pm 1.96\) ms. Four times the data halved the band, because \(\sqrt 4 = 2\).

Easy to get wrong

  • Confusing \(s\) with \(s/\sqrt n\). The standard deviation describes single measurements. The standard error describes the mean.
  • Forgetting the CLT needs independence (p.29, p.65). Consecutive latencies from one run are not independent, and more of them doesn't fix it (p.66).
  • Reading \(N(\mu, \sigma/\sqrt n)\) on (p.29) as a variance. Here the second argument is a standard deviation. On (p.36) the slide writes \(N(\mu, \sigma^2)\) with a variance, so check which one each slide means.

Interval for the mean, z for large n and t for small n

The idea

The interval is the point estimate plus or minus a multiple of the standard error. The only decision is which multiple. If \(\sigma\) is known, or \(n\) is large enough that \(s\) is a good stand-in for \(\sigma\), you use a normal critical value. If \(n\) is small you also have to pay for having estimated \(\sigma\) from the same few values, and the \(t\) distribution's heavier tails are that payment.

This is exactly HW4 Problem 1. You used \(z\) for the sample of 50 and \(t\) with 15 degrees of freedom for the sample of 16.

What to know

Which distribution (CI p.27). With \(\sigma\) known, use the normal. With \(\sigma\) unknown and a large sample, \(s\) is a good estimate of \(\sigma\), so the normal is still fine. With \(\sigma\) unknown, a small sample, and normally distributed data, use \(t\) with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom.

Large samples, \(n > 30\) (p.31). The \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval is \[ \left(\bar x - z_{\alpha/2}\frac{s}{\sqrt n},\ \ \bar x + z_{\alpha/2}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}\right) \] where \(z_{\alpha/2}\) is the \((1-\alpha/2)\)-quantile of \(N(0,1)\).

Small samples from a normal population (p.37). \[ \left(\bar x - t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\frac{s}{\sqrt n},\ \ \bar x + t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\frac{s}{\sqrt n}\right) \] where \(t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\) is the critical value of \(t\) with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom for an upper-tail area of \(\alpha/2\).

Why \(t\) (p.35-36). For normal data \((\bar X-\mu)/(\sigma/\sqrt n)\) is \(N(0,1)\) and \((n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\) is chi-square with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom, so \((\bar X - \mu)/\sqrt{s^2/n}\) has a \(t\) distribution with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. Its pdf looks like \(N(0,1)\), and for \(v > 30\) degrees of freedom you can approximate it by \(N(0,1)\). That is where the \(n > 30\) rule comes from. The slide's definition is \[ t(v) \sim \frac{N(0,1)}{\sqrt{\chi^2(v)/v}} \] where \(\chi^2(v)\) is the sum of squares of \(v\) independent unit normals (p.35). The study guide puts a number on the rule. At 29 df the 95% value is 2.045 against 1.960, under 5% apart.

Confidence\(\alpha\)\(z_{\alpha/2}\)\(t\), df 3df 8df 9df 15df 24
90%0.101.6452.3531.8601.8331.7531.711
95%0.051.9603.1822.3062.2622.1312.064
99%0.012.5765.8413.3553.2502.9472.797

On (p.38) the slide gets 2.145 for \(n = 15\), which is the df 14 value at 95%. Remember df is \(n-1\), never \(n\). The same slide reruns the three \(n = 15\) samples from (p.25) with \(t\), and all three intervals contain the population mean 0.2083. For \(n = 87\) the table on (p.32) uses \(z\), and its interval sizes 0.0750, 0.0894 and 0.1175 at 90%, 95% and 99% show the width growing with confidence.

Worked example

Small sample. You time a request 16 times and get \(\bar x = 50\) ms and \(s = 8\) ms. Give the 90%, 95% and 99% intervals for the mean.

Here \(n = 16\) is small and \(\sigma\) is unknown, so use \(t\) with \(16 - 1 = 15\) degrees of freedom. The standard error is \(s/\sqrt n = 8/\sqrt{16} = 8/4 = 2\) ms.

Level\(t_{[\alpha/2;\,15]}\)Half-width \(= t \times 2\)Interval
90%1.7533.506\((46.494,\ 53.506)\)
95%2.1314.262\((45.738,\ 54.262)\)
99%2.9475.894\((44.106,\ 55.894)\)

Each row is just \(50 \pm\) half-width. For 95%, \(50 - 4.262 = 45.738\) and \(50 + 4.262 = 54.262\).

Large sample. Same mean and \(s\), but \(n = 64\). Now \(n > 30\), so use \(z\). The standard error is \(8/\sqrt{64} = 8/8 = 1\) ms. At 95% the half-width is \(1.96 \times 1 = 1.96\), giving \((48.04,\ 51.96)\) ms.

If the exam hands you raw data, compute \(\bar x\) and \(s\) first with the \(n-1\) divisor (HW4 did this in Python, but on the exam it's by hand). Check question 2 below walks through one.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(n\) instead of \(n-1\) degrees of freedom, as in reading 2.145 off the df 14 row when \(n = 14\).
  • Using the one-tailed column. The interval needs \(\alpha/2\) in each tail, so 95% means the 0.025 upper-tail value, 1.960 for \(z\).
  • Dividing by \(n\) instead of \(\sqrt n\). With \(n = 16\) you divide \(s\) by 4, not 16.
  • Using \(t\) for small skewed samples without comment. The \(t\) interval assumes the data are normal (p.37, p.67). For skewed data at small \(n\) see the last topic.
  • For a population file like HW4's, the population variance divides by \(N\). The sample \(s\) still divides by \(n-1\).

What the confidence level means and what sets the width

The idea

The study guide calls the meaning of "95% confident" the single most misstated idea in the lecture. The 95% describes the procedure. If you repeated the whole experiment many times and built an interval each time, about 95% of those intervals would contain \(\mu\). Your one interval either contains \(\mu\) or it doesn't, since both are fixed numbers once you've computed it.

Width is the other half of reading an interval. You control it through \(n\) and the confidence level, the data control it through \(s\), and the critical value changes with \(n\) when you use \(t\).

What to know

The picture on (CI p.33) is 100 samples, each with its own interval, and about \(100(1-\alpha)\) of them include \(\mu\). The summary slide words it as \(\Pr(\)population parameter within computed interval\()\) (p.74). Read that as the success rate of the method, not as a probability about one interval you already have.

The half-width of a mean interval is \[ h = (\text{critical value}) \times \frac{s}{\sqrt n}. \]

ChangeEffect on widthWhy
Larger \(n\)Narrower, like \(1/\sqrt n\)Standard error shrinks (p.39), and \(t\) drops toward \(z\) (p.35)
Larger \(s\)Wider, in proportionMore scatter in the data
Higher confidenceWiderBigger critical value (p.32)

To halve the width you need four times the data, and one more decimal place costs a hundred times the data. In general \(n_2 = n_1 (h_1/h_2)^2\) (study guide Q15). That question also prices it in time. Going from \(\pm 1.4\) to \(\pm 0.5\) ms takes 196 runs, and at 4 minutes a run that's 784 minutes, about 13 hours, against 100 minutes for the original 25.

A narrower interval at the same \(n\) is bought with confidence, not knowledge. A 90% interval is narrower than a 99% one only because you've accepted that 1 in 10 of them will miss \(\mu\) instead of 1 in 100 (study guide Q3).

To compare two systems, build one interval on the difference. The study guide's formula sheet lists the paired version \(\bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,n-1]}\, s_d/\sqrt n\) and the unpaired one, and Q5 and Q8 work them. Those belong to the comparing-alternatives section, which covers them in full.

Comparing two systems with intervals. If two 95% intervals don't overlap, the means differ at that level. If they do overlap, you've learned nothing, because the difference can still be significant (Wild handout, pitfall 1). The right object is an interval on the difference.

Error bars. At \(n = 5\) the standard error is \(s/\sqrt5 \approx s/2.24\) and the 95% half-width is \(2.776 \times s/2.24 \approx 1.24\,s\). So from narrowest to widest it's SE, then SD, then the CI. A caption that just says "mean of 5 runs" leaves a factor of nearly 3 unexplained (Wild handout, pitfall 6). The CI multiplier on \(s\) is \(t/\sqrt n\), which is about 1.05 at \(n = 6\) and about 0.92 at \(n = 7\), so from 7 runs on the CI is narrower than the SD. Careful with study guide Q21. Its first sentence orders them SE, CI, SD, which contradicts its own arithmetic two lines later. Trust the arithmetic.

Worked example

This mirrors HW4 Problem 1 part 4. Take \(s = 8\). Compare the 95% half-width at \(n = 16\) and at \(n = 64\).

At \(n = 16\), \(h = 2.131 \times 8/4 = 2.131 \times 2 = 4.262\). At \(n = 64\), \(h = 1.96 \times 8/8 = 1.96\). The ratio is \(4.262/1.96 \approx 2.17\).

Split the ratio into its two causes. The sample size gives \(\sqrt{64/16} = \sqrt4 = 2\). The critical value gives \(2.131/1.96 \approx 1.087\). Multiply and \(2 \times 1.087 \approx 2.17\). In your HW4 answer the 16-sample interval was about 2.3 times wider than the 50-sample one, with \(\sqrt{50/16} = 1.77\) from \(n\), the rest from \(t\) versus \(z\), and some from that sample happening to have a larger \(s\).

Now the reverse question. Your interval is \(\pm 1.4\) ms from 25 runs and you want \(\pm 0.5\) ms. Then \(n_2 = 25 \times (1.4/0.5)^2 = 25 \times 2.8^2 = 25 \times 7.84 = 196\) runs.

Easy to get wrong

  • Saying "there's a 95% probability \(\mu\) is in this interval". That's a Bayesian credible-interval statement and needs a Bayesian analysis (study guide). Even (p.53) slips into this phrasing with "95% certain".
  • Thinking more data narrows the interval around the true value. It narrows it around \(\bar x\), which only converges to \(\mu\) if there's no systematic error.
  • Treating overlap as "not significant". Non-overlap implies significance, overlap implies nothing.
  • Using one sample's hit or miss to judge a method. Coverage is a long-run rate, so you'd need many samples to check it, which is HW4 Problem 2c part 5.
  • Expecting 10 times the runs to give 10 times narrower. It gives \(\sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\) times narrower.

Interval for a proportion

The idea

Sometimes each observation is a category instead of a number, like whether a file is html, a request failed, or the PC was inside the OS when a timer interrupt fired. You want the fraction of the population in one category. A proportion is just the mean of a 0/1 variable, so the same machinery works with a standard error built from \(p(1-p)\).

What to know

If \(n_1\) of \(n\) observations are in the category, the sample proportion is \(p = n_1/n\) and the population proportion is \(\pi\) (CI p.46). The sampling distribution of \(p\) tends to a normal with mean \(\pi\) and standard error \(\sigma_p = \sqrt{\pi(1-\pi)/n}\) (p.47). This is a normal approximation to the binomial, so the slide requires \(n\pi \ge 10\). The study guide asks for both \(np\) and \(n(1-p)\) to be at least about 10.

The interval (p.48) is \[ \left(p - z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}},\ \ p + z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}\right). \]

The slide examples. A Web log with 650 gif files out of 1000 gives (0.625, 0.675) at 90% and (0.620, 0.680) at 95% (p.50). The OS-time example has 658 of 6000 interrupts in the OS, so \(p = 0.1097\) and the 95% interval is (0.1018, 0.1176), or 10.2% to 11.8% of the time (p.51-53).

The caveat (p.49). This is the Wald interval and it's known to be problematic. Use Wilson for \(n < 40\) and Agresti-Coull for \(n > 40\). The study guide adds that it breaks down when \(p\) is near 0 or 1 or \(n\) is small, and the symptom is an interval running below 0 or above 1. For 99 of 100 the Wilson interval is (0.946, 0.998), which is lopsided and stays inside [0, 1] (study guide Q7).

That failure zone is exactly where CS proportions live, like error rates, failure rates, coverage and benchmark accuracy near the ceiling (Wild handout, pitfall 4). A model scoring 98% on 200 questions has only \(200 \times 0.02 = 4\) misses, well under 10, so its Wald interval can't be trusted.

Worked example

80 of 400 requests hit a slow path. Give the 90% and 95% intervals for the true fraction.

First \(p = 80/400 = 0.2\). Check the condition with \(np = 80\) and \(n(1-p) = 320\), both well over 10.

Standard error is \(\sqrt{0.2 \times 0.8 / 400} = \sqrt{0.16/400} = \sqrt{0.16}/\sqrt{400} = 0.4/20 = 0.02\).

At 95%, \(1.96 \times 0.02 = 0.0392\), so the interval is \((0.2 - 0.0392,\ 0.2 + 0.0392) = (0.1608,\ 0.2392)\).

At 90%, \(1.645 \times 0.02 = 0.0329\), so the interval is \((0.1671,\ 0.2329)\).

Splitting the square root into \(\sqrt{\text{top}}/\sqrt{\text{bottom}}\) is the trick that makes these hand-friendly when \(n\) is a perfect square.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(t\). Proportion intervals use \(z\) because they rest on the normal approximation to the binomial.
  • Skipping the \(np \ge 10\) check. With 99 of 100 correct the 95% Wald interval is \((0.9705, 1.0095)\), which goes past 1 (study guide Q7).
  • Forgetting the square root, or putting \(n\) outside it.
  • Comparing two models by whether their proportion intervals overlap. Build an interval on the difference instead.

How many measurements you need

The idea

Run the interval formula backwards. Pick the half-width you can live with, then solve for \(n\). The catch is that the formula needs \(s\) (or \(p\)) before you've collected the data. The fix is a small pilot run used only to estimate \(s\), which then feeds the formula (CI p.41).

The study guide's cultural point is that "we ran it three times" isn't a sample size. A justified \(n\) names the pilot, the target half-width and the confidence level.

What to know

Mean, relative error (p.40). Ask for \((c_1, c_2) = (1 \mp e)\bar x\), so \(z_{\alpha/2}\, s/\sqrt n = \bar x e\), which gives \[ n = \left(\frac{z_{\alpha/2}\, s}{\bar x e}\right)^2. \] The slide example (p.42-44) has \(\bar x = 7.94\) s, \(s = 2.14\) s, 90% confidence and "within 7%", which the slide reads as a total width of 7%, so \(e = 0.035\). Then \(n = (1.645 \times 2.14 / (0.035 \times 7.94))^2 = 160.5\), rounded up to 161.

Mean, absolute half-width \(e\) (study guide formula sheet). \(n = (z_{\alpha/2}\, s / e)^2\). Same idea with \(e\) in data units.

Proportion, relative error (p.54). Setting \((1-e)\bar p = \bar p - z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{\bar p(1-\bar p)/n}\) gives \[ n = \frac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, \bar p(1-\bar p)}{(e\bar p)^2}. \] The OS example wants 95% and \(\pm 0.5\%\) with \(\bar p = 0.1097\), so \(e = 0.005\) is relative to \(\bar p\). That needs 1,247,102 interrupts, which at one per 10 ms is 3.46 hours (p.55-57).

Proportion, absolute half-width \(e\) (study guide). \(n = z_{\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)/e^2\). With no estimate of \(p\), use \(p = 0.5\) because it makes \(p(1-p)\) as large as it can be.

Always round up, because rounding down misses the target.

Worked example

Mean. A pilot of 10 runs gives \(\bar x = 20\) ms and \(s = 5\) ms. How many runs for a 95% interval of \(\pm 2\%\)?

Here \(e = 0.02\), so \(\bar x e = 20 \times 0.02 = 0.4\) ms. The top is \(1.96 \times 5 = 9.8\). Then \(9.8/0.4 = 24.5\) and \(n = 24.5^2 = 600.25\), so 601 runs. For the square, \(24.5^2 = 24^2 + 2(24)(0.5) + 0.25 = 576 + 24 + 0.25 = 600.25\).

Proportion. A pilot says about 20% of requests hit the slow path. You want 95% confidence and \(\pm 10\%\) relative error, so \(e\bar p = 0.1 \times 0.2 = 0.02\).

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.2 \times 0.8}{0.02^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.16}{0.0004} = \frac{0.614656}{0.0004} = 1536.64 \]

Round up to 1537. For the division, dividing by 0.0004 is the same as multiplying by 2500, and \(0.614656 \times 2500 = 1536.64\).

Proportion, absolute, no pilot (study guide Q13). You want a failure rate within \(\pm 3\) percentage points at 95% and know nothing about it, so take the worst case \(p = 0.5\), where \(p(1-p) = 0.25\).

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.25}{0.03^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.25}{0.0009} = \frac{0.9604}{0.0009} = 1067.1 \]

Round up to 1068 trials. If a pilot says the rate is about 5% (Q14), \(p(1-p) = 0.05 \times 0.95 = 0.0475\) and \(n = 3.8416 \times 0.0475/0.0009 = 0.182476/0.0009 = 202.75\), so 203 trials. The catch is that \(\pm 3\) points around 5% is a relative error of \(0.03/0.05 = 60\%\), which is almost never what you wanted. For rare events, state the target as relative error, which is the slides' formula.

Easy to get wrong

  • Mixing up relative and absolute error. The slides' proportion formula divides by \((e\bar p)^2\) because their \(e\) is a fraction of \(\bar p\). The study guide's formula divides by \(e^2\) with \(e\) in percentage points. Read which one the question means.
  • Using the full width as \(e\). "Within 7%" on (p.42) became \(e = 0.035\), half of 7%.
  • Rounding 160.5 down to 160. Always round up.
  • Using \(t\). You don't know \(n\) yet, so you can't know the degrees of freedom. The slides use \(z\).
  • Forgetting to square. \(n\) grows with the square of \(z s/(\bar x e)\).
  • Trusting a low pilot estimate of \(p\). If the true rate is closer to 0.5 than the pilot said, \(p(1-p)\) is bigger and your interval comes out wider than planned, so round the pilot estimate toward 0.5 (study guide Q14).
  • Writing "we ran it three times" as a sample size. A justified \(n\) reports the pilot, the target half-width and the confidence level so a reviewer can check it (study guide Q16).

Interval for a variance

The idea

Sometimes the claim is about variability itself, like jitter or tail predictability. If the data are normal, \((n-1)s^2/\sigma^2\) follows a chi-square distribution, and you can invert that just like you inverted the \(t\) statistic. The chi-square isn't symmetric, so the interval isn't symmetric around \(s^2\) either.

What to know

The \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval for \(\sigma^2\) (CI p.59) is \[ \frac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_U} \le \sigma^2 \le \frac{(n-1)s^2}{\chi^2_L} \] where \(\chi^2_L\) is the lower critical value (the \(\alpha/2\) quantile) and \(\chi^2_U\) is the upper one (the \(1-\alpha/2\) quantile), both with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. The big critical value gives the lower limit, and the two limits sit at different distances from \(s^2\) because the chi-square is not symmetric (p.59-60).

Slide example (p.61). For \(n = 100\) from \(N(3,2)\), \(s^2 = 4.71435\), the critical values with 99 df are 73.361 and 128.422, and the interval is \((3.6343, 6.362)\), which contains the true variance 4.

Only trust it for normal data. For non-normal populations, especially small samples, it isn't very accurate (p.62). For an interval on \(\sigma\), take square roots of both ends.

Worked example

Fifteen runs give \(s^2 = 4.0\) ms². Give the 95% interval for \(\sigma^2\) and \(\sigma\). With 14 df the table gives \(\chi^2_L = 5.629\) and \(\chi^2_U = 26.119\) (study guide Q6).

Numerator \((n-1)s^2 = 14 \times 4.0 = 56\).

Lower limit \(56/26.119\). Since \(26.119 \times 2 = 52.238\), the remainder is \(56 - 52.238 = 3.762\), and \(3.762/26.119 \approx 0.144\). So about 2.144.

Upper limit \(56/5.629\). Since \(5.629 \times 10 = 56.29\) is just over 56, the answer is just under 10, about 9.95.

So \(\sigma^2 \in (2.14,\ 9.95)\) ms², and taking roots, \(\sigma \in (1.46,\ 3.15)\) ms because \(1.46^2 \approx 2.13\) and \(3.15^2 \approx 9.92\). Notice how wide this is. Fifteen runs pin down a mean much better than a variance.

Easy to get wrong

  • Putting the small critical value under the lower limit. That gives limits in the wrong order.
  • Using \(n\) degrees of freedom or forgetting to multiply \(s^2\) by \(n-1\).
  • Writing the interval as \(s^2 \pm\) something. It's not symmetric.
  • Applying it to skewed data. It needs a normal population (p.59, p.62).

When normality or independence fails, batching, transformation and the bootstrap

The idea

Everything so far assumed normal errors and independent measurements (CI p.63, p.65). The CLT rescues normality for large \(n\), but not for small skewed samples, and nothing rescues you from dependence by adding more measurements from the same run (p.66-67). The deck gives three responses, and they are not interchangeable. HW4 Problem 2 had you run two of them on the skewed data.

What to know

The damage (p.67). With \(n = 16\) from a right-skewed population (skew 1.8), a nominal 95% \(t\) interval contains \(\mu\) only about 91% of the time. The error is systematic, since the interval is consistently too narrow.

Batching (p.68). Average groups of four or more observations and build the \(t\) interval from the batch means, which is the CLT applied by hand. With \(m\) batches the interval is \(\bar b \pm t_{[\alpha/2;\,m-1]}\, s_b/\sqrt m\). The cost is degrees of freedom. 16 values become 4 batch means, so \(t\) goes from 2.13 to 3.18, about 50% wider, and you no longer have an interval for individual values. It's the only response that handles correlated observations (p.70). Batch in the order the data were collected, never after sorting.

Why batching works on dependence (study guide, Wild handout entry 15). Averaging within a batch breaks most of the correlation between batch means, so the batch means act like an independent sample. The standard error of the mean barely changes, since the mean of the batch means is the grand mean. What you lose is degrees of freedom. Batch size is a tradeoff. Batches too small leave correlation and the interval stays too narrow, and batches too large leave too few batches and the \(t\) multiplier explodes. Delete the warm-up before batching, because that's a systematic error.

Transformation (p.68). Apply log, square root or Box-Cox, then use the \(t\) interval. Choose the transform from the data. Back-transforming the endpoints gives an interval for the median, not the mean, and the centre \(e^{\text{mean}(\ln x)}\) is the geometric mean.

Bootstrap (p.69). Treat your sample as a stand-in for the population. Draw \(n\) values from it with replacement, compute the statistic, repeat \(B\) times (\(B = 10{,}000\) takes seconds), and read off the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles for a 95% interval. With replacement is essential so resamples differ like independent samples would. These percentile intervals under-cover at small \(n\), meaning the real coverage is below the promised level, so prefer BCa, SciPy's default. BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) reads off a shifted, lopsided pair of percentiles using a bias correction, from the fraction of resamples below the observed estimate, and an acceleration, from the jackknife (study guide). It works for medians, quantiles, ratios, correlations and differences of medians, which have no formula. Use a large \(B\) because it costs CPU, not data. With \(B = 40\) the tails can only move in steps of \(1/40 = 2.5\%\) and the endpoints jump around from run to run (study guide Q11).

SituationUse
Observations correlated (simulation output, consecutive requests)Batching
You want the median, or the process is multiplicativeTransformation
You want the mean and observations are independentBootstrap, the modern default and narrowest at equal coverage

That's (p.70). None of the three creates information, so at \(n = 16\) they all stay approximate and more data is the real fix. If your 16 values missed the tail, every resample misses it too. The slide ends by asking whether, with strongly skewed data, the mean is even the summary you want.

A geometric-mean speedup is the log transform done silently, since the log of the geometric mean is the mean of the logs (Wild handout, entry 16). So a paper that reports one has already moved to the median, whether it says so or not.

Worked example

Sixteen latencies in the order measured, in batches of four.

BatchValuesSumMean \(b_j\)
17, 9, 14, 104010
215, 12, 13, 165614
311, 13, 10, 144812
418, 14, 17, 156416

The mean of the batch means is \((10+14+12+16)/4 = 52/4 = 13\), which equals the grand mean \(208/16 = 13\), as it always must.

Deviations from 13 are \(-3, 1, -1, 3\) and their squares are \(9, 1, 1, 9\), summing to 20. So \(s_b^2 = 20/(4-1) = 6.667\) and \(s_b \approx 2.582\) (check \(2.58^2 = 6.656\)).

Standard error \(s_b/\sqrt m = 2.582/\sqrt4 = 2.582/2 = 1.291\). With \(m - 1 = 3\) df, \(t_{[0.025;\,3]} = 3.182\), so the half-width is \(3.182 \times 1.291 \approx 4.108\). The 95% interval is \((13 - 4.108,\ 13 + 4.108) = (8.892,\ 17.108)\).

In your HW4 run the batch means were 0.4212, 0.2368, 0.0904 and 0.2139 with \(s_b = 0.1365\). The 95% batched interval was \(0.2406 \pm 3.182 \times 0.1365/2 = 0.2406 \pm 0.2172\), which is (0.0234, 0.4578) by hand and (0.0233, 0.4578) in your table from the unrounded values. The 99% one used 5.841 and went below zero, which is impossible for that data. That's the price of only 3 degrees of freedom.

Your HW4 bootstrap on the same 16 values gave (0.1493, 0.3407) at 95%, narrower than the raw \(t\) interval (0.1319, 0.3493), which fits (p.70) calling it the narrowest. All nine HW4 intervals contained 0.2083, but one sample can't show coverage.

Log transform (study guide Q10). Sixteen skewed latencies have mean 18.394 ms, but in logs mean\((\ln x) = 2.7392\) and \(s = 0.5335\). The standard error on the log scale is \(0.5335/4 = 0.1334\) and the half-width is \(2.131 \times 0.1334 \approx 0.2842\). The log-scale interval is \((2.7392 - 0.2842,\ 2.7392 + 0.2842) = (2.4550,\ 3.0234)\). Exponentiating both ends (the exam would give you these) gives about \((11.65,\ 20.56)\) ms, centred on \(e^{2.7392} \approx 15.5\) ms. That's an interval for the median, centred on the geometric mean, which sits below the 18.4 ms arithmetic mean because the two big values pull the arithmetic mean up. It's symmetric by ratio, \(15.5/11.65 \approx 1.33\) and \(20.56/15.5 \approx 1.33\), not by milliseconds.

Bootstrap bookkeeping. With \(B = 10{,}000\) sorted resample means, the 95% percentile interval is roughly the 251st and 9750th values and the 90% is the 501st and 9500th (study guide Q11). Conventions differ by one index.

Easy to get wrong

  • Expecting batching to narrow the interval. It almost always widens it because \(t\) jumps with fewer degrees of freedom. In HW4 it came out about twice as wide as the raw \(t\) interval at 95%.
  • Sorting before batching. Then the batches aren't draws from the same distribution (HW4 2a).
  • Believing the bootstrap "makes no assumptions". It still needs independent draws, and resampling an autocorrelated trace gives the same too-narrow interval (Wild handout, pitfall 7).
  • Reporting a back-transformed log interval as an interval for the mean. It's for the median.
  • Bootstrapping without replacement. Every resample would then be your original sample.

What an interval doesn't cover, reading intervals in papers

The idea

The whole lecture is machinery for one kind of uncertainty, the error that shrinks when you repeat a measurement. Bias, confounding, a sample that isn't representative and the wrong summary statistic all sit outside the interval, and they're usually the bigger problem (study guide, closing section). The Wild handout and HW4 Problem 3 ask you to audit a real paper with this in mind, and the study guide's interpretation questions (Q19 to Q26) are where people lose marks.

What to know

Sampling error only (Wild handout, pitfall 3). A user-initiated speed-test dataset is a selection effect, since people run tests when the connection feels slow. Ten times the tests narrows the interval and leaves the bias exactly where it was, so more data makes a biased estimate more confidently wrong. The fix is a design that breaks the link between outcome and inclusion, like scheduled tests from fixed vantage points (study guide Q23, Wild handout entry 12).

Right statistic (pitfall 5, study guide Q22). Latency is right-skewed and SLOs are written on the 99th percentile. A tight interval on the mean answers a question nobody asked, because a 42 ms mean fits a 400 ms p99. An interval on a quantile needs order statistics or the bootstrap, since the lecture's formulas cover means, proportions and variances. Raasveldt et al. report medians with quantile-based intervals for this reason (Wild handout, entry 14).

Experimental unit (pitfall 7, study guide Q19). Two hundred iterations inside one JVM share JIT state, heap layout and CPU frequency history, so they aren't 200 independent draws. Treating them as replicates is pseudo-replication and gives a far too narrow interval. Either make the invocation the unit (many JVM runs, one number each, then resample those) or delete warm-up and use batch means.

Randomized algorithms (study guide Q24). Intervals still apply. The population is the distribution of outcomes over random seeds on that input and machine, so the claim covers that input only. Generalizing to other inputs is a second, usually larger, source of variance.

Best of 3 (study guide Q25). The maximum of \(n\) runs is biased, since it grows with \(n\), and a reader who runs the method once lands near the mean, not your best. Comparing your best against a baseline's mean is rigged before any statistics. Report all runs, or a robust statistic with a bootstrap interval as Agarwal et al. do, and call the result preliminary if you can't support an interval.

Huge \(n\) (Wild handout, entry 9). With millions of users every difference is statistically detectable, so the question becomes whether the effect is big enough to matter.

The audit (Wild handout Part III, HW4 Problem 3). Find the interval, name the estimator, find \(n\) and whether it's justified, check the normality assumption and which response was used, check what the independent unit is, name one error source the interval leaves out, and check whether the conclusion quietly needs a credible interval.

Worked example

Your HW4 Problem 3 audit of the Puffer paper is the model. The stall ratio is total stall time over total watch time, a ratio with no lecture formula, so the authors bootstrap it (CI p.69). They randomized by session but resampled streams, and streams in one session share a connection, so the unit doesn't match the randomization and the intervals are probably too narrow. Resampling whole sessions would fix that. The intervals also say nothing about viewers who never found Puffer, which is error outside the interval. Finally, they compare schemes by whether error bars overlap, which isn't a significance test.

A quick number to keep the p99 point concrete. In 10,000 requests only \(0.01 \times 10{,}000 = 100\) values lie above the p99, so a tail estimate rests on about 100 points even when the mean rests on 10,000. And if all 10,000 came from one run, the interval also says nothing about run-to-run variation (study guide Q22).

Easy to get wrong

  • Reading a narrow interval as a trustworthy result. Every measurement in a biased or confounded setup can carry a respectable interval (Wild handout, entry 14).
  • Counting measurements instead of units. Ask what was independently replicated.
  • Saying intervals don't apply to randomized algorithms. They do, with the seed distribution as the population.
  • Accepting undefined error bars. Check the caption for SD, SE or CI (pitfall 6).

Check yourself

1. True or false. A computed 95% interval of (45.7, 54.3) ms has a 95% probability of containing the true mean.

False. Once computed, the interval and \(\mu\) are both fixed, so it either contains \(\mu\) or it doesn't. The 95% is the long-run rate at which the procedure produces intervals that contain \(\mu\), which is the 100-samples picture on (CI p.33). A probability statement about this one interval would need a Bayesian credible interval.

2. Nine runs give 4, 6, 5, 7, 3, 5, 6, 4, 5 seconds. Find the 95% interval for the mean.

The sum is 45, so \(\bar x = 45/9 = 5\). Deviations are \(-1, 1, 0, 2, -2, 0, 1, -1, 0\), squares \(1, 1, 0, 4, 4, 0, 1, 1, 0\), summing to 12. So \(s^2 = 12/8 = 1.5\) and \(s \approx 1.225\) (since \(1.225^2 \approx 1.5\)). Standard error \(1.225/\sqrt9 = 1.225/3 \approx 0.408\). With \(n = 9\) use \(t\) with 8 df, \(t_{[0.025;\,8]} = 2.306\), so the half-width is \(2.306 \times 0.408 \approx 0.941\). The interval is \((4.06,\ 5.94)\) s (CI p.37).

3. In HW4 Problem 1 you used z for the sample of 50 and t for the sample of 16. Why, and why was the 16-sample interval so much wider?

The slides use \(z\) for \(n > 30\) because \(s\) is then a good estimate of \(\sigma\) and \(t\) with more than 30 df is close to \(N(0,1)\) (CI p.31, p.35). With 16 values you also have to account for estimating \(\sigma\), so you use \(t\) with 15 df (p.37). The width grew because the standard error has \(\sqrt n\) underneath (\(\sqrt{50/16} \approx 1.77\)) and because 2.131 is bigger than 1.960. Your sample of 16 also happened to have a larger \(s\).

4. 90 of 900 test cases fail. Give the 95% interval for the failure rate and check the condition.

\(p = 90/900 = 0.1\). The condition holds since \(np = 90\) and \(n(1-p) = 810\). Standard error \(\sqrt{0.1 \times 0.9/900} = \sqrt{0.09}/\sqrt{900} = 0.3/30 = 0.01\). Half-width \(1.96 \times 0.01 = 0.0196\). Interval \((0.0804,\ 0.1196)\) (CI p.48).

5. 2 of 50 runs crash. Compute the 95% Wald interval. What's wrong with it and what would you use instead?

\(p = 0.04\). Standard error \(\sqrt{0.04 \times 0.96/50} = \sqrt{0.000768} \approx 0.0277\), half-width \(1.96 \times 0.0277 \approx 0.0543\), giving \((-0.014,\ 0.094)\). A negative crash rate is impossible. The normal approximation fails because \(np = 2\) is far below 10 (CI p.47). The slide's alternatives are Wilson for \(n < 40\) and Agresti-Coull for \(n > 40\), so with \(n = 50\) you'd use Agresti-Coull (p.49).

6. A pilot gives a mean of 40 ms and s = 8 ms. How many runs for a 90% interval within ±5% of the mean?

\(\bar x e = 40 \times 0.05 = 2\). Top \(1.645 \times 8 = 13.16\). Ratio \(13.16/2 = 6.58\). Then \(n = 6.58^2 = 43.30\), so 44 runs (CI p.40). Round up, never down.

7. Your 95% interval from 20 runs is ±3 ms. Your advisor wants ±1 ms. Roughly how many runs?

Half-width scales like \(1/\sqrt n\), so \(n_2 = 20 \times (3/1)^2 = 20 \times 9 = 180\) runs. A three-times-narrower interval costs nine times the runs (CI p.39). It's approximate because \(s\) and the critical value will shift a little.

8. Fifteen runs have s = 3 ms. Give the 95% interval for σ² using χ² values 5.629 and 26.119 (14 df).

\((n-1)s^2 = 14 \times 9 = 126\). Lower limit uses the big value, \(126/26.119 \approx 4.82\) (since \(26.119 \times 4.8 \approx 125.4\)). Upper limit \(126/5.629 \approx 22.38\) (since \(5.629 \times 22 \approx 123.8\)). So \(\sigma^2 \in (4.82,\ 22.38)\) ms² and \(\sigma \in (2.20,\ 4.73)\) ms. It assumes normal data (CI p.59, p.62).

9. In HW4 the batched interval was the widest. Why does a "correction" for non-normality make the interval wider, and what did you give up?

Batching turns 16 observations into 4 batch means, so the degrees of freedom drop from 15 to 3 and \(t\) at 95% jumps from 2.131 to 3.182 (CI p.68). The standard error stays about the same, so the width grows with the multiplier. You gave up degrees of freedom and any statement about individual values. At 99% the multiplier is 5.841, and your interval went below zero, a sign that 4 batches is too few at that level.

10. Which response would you use for (a) waiting times of 100,000 consecutive customers in one simulation run, (b) speedups on 12 benchmarks, (c) 40 independent skewed job times when you want the mean?

(a) Batching, because consecutive waits are correlated and it's the only response that handles dependence. (b) Transformation, because speedups are multiplicative and the log scale gives the geometric mean, with the median caveat. (c) Bootstrap, because the data are independent and you want the mean, and it's the narrowest at equal coverage (CI p.70). BCa is better than plain percentiles here.

11. A colleague's interval is ±0.2 ms after 5,000 runs, so they call the result accurate. What's your reply?

A narrow interval shows precision, which comes from random error. Accuracy depends on systematic error, which repetition doesn't touch (CI p.17). A miscalibrated timer or a warm cache would give a tight interval around the wrong number. If the 5,000 values are consecutive requests from one run they also aren't independent, so the interval is too narrow anyway (p.66).

12. Two systems have 95% intervals (10, 14) and (13, 17). Can you conclude their means don't differ significantly?

No. Overlap tells you nothing. Non-overlap would imply a significant difference, but overlapping intervals are compatible with a significant one. Build an interval on the difference and see whether it contains 0 (Wild handout, pitfall 1, and study guide Q20).

13. A benchmark runs 10 times with mean 7.2 s and s = 0.8 s. Give the 95% interval. Then redo it for 100 runs with the same mean and s, and explain why the half-width doesn't shrink by a factor of 10.

With \(n = 10\) use \(t\) with 9 df, 2.262. Standard error \(0.8/\sqrt{10} \approx 0.8/3.162 \approx 0.253\), half-width \(2.262 \times 0.253 \approx 0.572\), interval \((6.63,\ 7.77)\) s (CI p.37). With \(n = 100\) use \(z = 1.96\). Standard error \(0.8/10 = 0.08\), half-width \(1.96 \times 0.08 \approx 0.157\), interval \((7.04,\ 7.36)\) s (p.31). The ratio is \(0.572/0.157 \approx 3.6\), which is \(\sqrt{10} \approx 3.16\) from \(n\) times \(2.262/1.96 \approx 1.15\) from dropping \(t\). It isn't 10 because width goes like \(1/\sqrt n\) (p.39). For the \(n = 10\) data at other levels (study guide Q3), 90% uses \(t_{[0.05;\,9]} = 1.833\), so the half-width is \(1.833 \times 0.253 \approx 0.464\) and the interval is \((6.74,\ 7.66)\). 99% uses \(t_{[0.005;\,9]} = 3.250\), so the half-width is \(3.250 \times 0.253 \approx 0.822\) and the interval is \((6.38,\ 8.02)\). The 90% one is narrower only because you accept that 1 in 10 such intervals misses \(\mu\) instead of 1 in 100. This is study guide Q1 to Q3.

14. You want a failure rate within ±3 percentage points at 95% and have no estimate of it. How many trials? What if a pilot says about 5%, and what's the catch?

With no estimate use \(p = 0.5\) because it maximizes \(p(1-p)\). Then \(n = 1.96^2 \times 0.25/0.03^2 = 0.9604/0.0009 = 1067.1\), so 1068 trials. With \(p = 0.05\), \(n = 3.8416 \times 0.0475/0.0009 = 202.75\), so 203. The catch is that \(\pm 3\) points on a 5% rate is \(\pm 60\%\) relative, too loose to be useful, so state rare-event targets as relative error, which is the slides' form \(n = z_{\alpha/2}^2\,\bar p(1-\bar p)/(e\bar p)^2\) (CI p.54). This is study guide Q13 and Q14.

15. You estimate the integral of sin x from 0 to π by Monte Carlo. From 100 draws, Y = π sin X has mean 1.99 and s ≈ 0.97. Give a 95% interval for the integral and say why the method is legitimate.

\(\bar Y(n)\) is a mean of independent values with \(E(Y) = I\), so it's unbiased and its variance is \(\mathrm{Var}(Y)/n\) (Monte Carlo handout, Law and Kelton p.90-91). With \(n = 100 > 30\) use \(z\). Standard error \(0.97/\sqrt{100} = 0.097\), half-width \(1.96 \times 0.097 \approx 0.190\), interval \((1.80,\ 2.18)\), which contains the true value 2 (CI p.31). To halve the width you'd need 400 draws.

16. You simulate 1000 samples of size 16 from a skewed population and 910 of the nominal 95% t intervals contain μ. Is the method's coverage really below 95%?

The coverage estimate is a proportion, so use the proportion interval (CI p.48). \(p = 0.91\), standard error \(\sqrt{0.91 \times 0.09/1000} = \sqrt{0.0000819} \approx 0.00905\), half-width \(1.96 \times 0.00905 \approx 0.0177\), interval \((0.892,\ 0.928)\). It excludes 0.95, so yes, the method under-covers, which matches the 91% figure on (p.67). A single sample, as in HW4 Problem 2c part 5, could never show this.

17. A paper bootstraps an interval on mean throughput from 200 iterations inside one JVM run. What's wrong and how would you fix it?

The iterations share JIT state, heap layout and CPU frequency history, so they aren't independent draws. Treating them as replicates is pseudo-replication, and the bootstrap assumes independent draws, so the interval comes out far too narrow (CI p.69, Wild handout pitfall 7). Fix it by running many JVM invocations, reducing each to one number, and resampling invocations, or by deleting warm-up and using batch means, the only response that handles dependence (p.70). This is study guide Q19.

18. A model answers 348 of 400 benchmark questions correctly. Give the 95% Wald interval for its accuracy and check the condition.

\(p = 348/400 = 0.87\). The condition holds because \(np = 348\) and \(n(1-p) = 52\) are both above 10 (CI p.47). The standard error is \(\sqrt{0.87 \times 0.13/400} = \sqrt{0.1131/400} = \sqrt{0.00028275} \approx 0.0168\), so the half-width is \(1.96 \times 0.0168 \approx 0.033\) and the interval is \((0.837,\ 0.903)\), or 83.7% to 90.3% (p.48). This is study guide Q4. Its Q5, a second model at 89% on the same questions, needs an interval on the difference, which the comparing-alternatives section works through.

19. A new allocator is run against a baseline on 7 benchmarks. The differences (new minus baseline, in seconds) are −0.8, −1.2, +0.3, −0.5, −1.5, −0.2, −0.9. Is the improvement significant at 95%?

Both systems ran the same benchmarks, so the data are paired and you build one interval on the differences. The sum is \(-4.8\), so \(\bar d = -4.8/7 = -0.686\). The squared deviations from \(-0.686\) add up to about 2.229, so \(s_d^2 = 2.229/6 = 0.371\) and \(s_d \approx 0.609\). The standard error is \(0.609/\sqrt7 = 0.609/2.646 \approx 0.230\). With 6 df, \(t_{[0.025;\,6]} = 2.447\), so the half-width is \(2.447 \times 0.230 \approx 0.564\) and the interval is \((-1.25,\ -0.12)\) s. It excludes 0, so the improvement is significant, but it could be anywhere from 0.12 to 1.25 s (CI p.37, study guide Q8 and formula sheet).

20. Sixteen latencies in measurement order (ms) are 11.2, 13.8, 9.6, 45.1 | 12.4, 10.9, 18.7, 11.8 | 14.6, 10.2, 62.3, 13.1 | 9.8, 16.4, 12.9, 21.5, with \(\bar x = 18.394\) and \(s = 14.501\). Give the 95% t interval and the 95% batch-means interval for the four marked batches, and explain the difference in width.

The t interval uses \(s/\sqrt{16} = 14.501/4 = 3.625\) and \(t_{[0.025;\,15]} = 2.131\), so the half-width is \(2.131 \times 3.625 \approx 7.73\) and the interval is \((10.67,\ 26.12)\) ms. The batch means are \(79.7/4 = 19.925\), \(53.8/4 = 13.450\), \(100.2/4 = 25.050\) and \(60.6/4 = 15.150\), and they average to 18.394, the grand mean. Their squared deviations from 18.394 add up to about 81.61, so \(s_b^2 = 81.61/3 = 27.20\) and \(s_b \approx 5.216\). The standard error is \(5.216/2 = 2.608\), and with 3 df the half-width is \(3.182 \times 2.608 \approx 8.30\), giving \((10.09,\ 26.69)\) ms. The batched interval is only about 7% wider here even though the multiplier grew by \(3.182/2.131 \approx 1.49\), because this sample's batch standard error happened to come out smaller (2.608 against 3.625). That's luck. Batching keeps the standard error the same on average and costs degrees of freedom, so expect close to 50% wider in general (CI p.68, study guide Q9).

Hypothesis testing

Slides: HT (Lecture 9, Hypothesis Testing), with the hypothesis-testing study guide, the "Hypothesis Testing in the Wild" handout and the chi-square goodness-of-fit handout. Homework: HW5 (Problems 1 and 2, and Problem 3 on Spang et al., IMC 2021).

The two hypotheses and which side you test

The idea

A hypothesis test is an argument by contradiction. You assume a specific claim about the population, the null hypothesis \(H_0\), and then ask whether the data you got would be surprising if that claim were true. If they would be, you drop \(H_0\) and accept the alternative \(H_1\). If they wouldn't, you keep \(H_0\) for now, but you haven't shown it's true (HT p.4). So you're never asking "is this true?", only "is my data consistent with it?" (p.3).

That asymmetry decides how you set things up. A test can give you evidence for \(H_1\) by rejecting \(H_0\). It can never give you evidence for \(H_0\). So whatever you're trying to establish goes in \(H_1\), and its negation becomes \(H_0\), the thing you're hoping to knock down (p.4). If you put your claim in \(H_0\), a small noisy experiment would "support" it just by failing to reject, which is support through low power and not through evidence.

The second choice is the direction. If any difference counts, the test is two-sided. If only one direction supports the claim, it's one-sided. The research question decides this, and it has to be decided before you see the data (p.5). The slide's two examples are "Is A faster than B?", which is one-sided, and "Does the patch change latency?", which is two-sided.

What to know

PieceWhat it is
\(H_0\)Always contains the equality, \(\mu = \mu_0\). It's held until the data force you off it (p.4).
\(H_1\)\(\mu \ne \mu_0\) (two-sided), or \(\mu > \mu_0\) or \(\mu < \mu_0\) (one-sided) (p.4-5).
Two-sided ruleReject if \(|Z| > z[\alpha/2]\). The \(\alpha\) is split between two tails (p.5, p.9).
One-sided ruleReject if \(Z > z[\alpha]\) for \(H_1: \mu > \mu_0\), or \(Z < -z[\alpha]\) for \(H_1: \mu < \mu_0\). All of \(\alpha\) sits in one tail (p.5, p.9).

At \(\alpha = 0.05\) the two-sided cutoff is \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) and the one-sided cutoff is \(z[0.05] = 1.645\) (p.9). The one-sided test has the smaller cutoff, so it has more power in the direction you named and no power at all in the other one.

The trap is switching to one-sided after seeing which way the data went. You'd have picked the other tail if the data had gone the other way, so the real false-positive rate of that procedure is 0.10, not 0.05 (p.5).

Worked example

Setting up the hypotheses (HW5 Problem 2.1). A group suspects the mean time to the first sensor fault is less than 740 hours and wants to confirm it. The claim is "\(\mu < 740\)", so it goes in \(H_1\).

\[H_0: \mu = 740 \text{ (or } \mu \ge 740\text{)}, \qquad H_1: \mu < 740.\]

It's one-sided because only a mean below 740 supports the claim. A mean above 740 would just be "no support".

Same data, two directions. Take HW5 Problem 1, where \(n = 36\), \(\sigma = 0.8\), \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) and \(\bar{x} = 2.74\). The statistic is \(Z = 1.80\) (worked out in the next topic).

  • Two-sided, \(H_1: \mu \ne 2.5\). Compare \(|1.80|\) with 1.96. It's smaller, so you fail to reject.
  • One-sided, \(H_1: \mu > 2.5\). Compare 1.80 with 1.645. It's bigger, so you reject.

The one-sided verdict is only legitimate if the question was "is training slower than 2.5 hours?" from the start. HW5 Problem 1 states \(H_0: \mu = 2.5\) with no direction, so the two-sided test is the right one there.

Easy to get wrong

  • Putting the claim you want to prove in \(H_0\). The equality always goes in \(H_0\) and the claim goes in \(H_1\).
  • Writing "we accept \(H_0\)". Say "fail to reject", because not rejecting isn't evidence for \(H_0\) (HT p.4).
  • Using \(z[\alpha/2]\) for a one-sided test, or \(z[\alpha]\) for a two-sided one. One-sided at 0.05 is 1.645, two-sided at 0.05 is 1.96, two-sided at 0.01 is 2.576, one-sided at 0.01 is 2.326.
  • For \(H_1: \mu < \mu_0\) the rejection region is the lower tail, so you reject when \(Z\) is more negative than \(-z[\alpha]\).

The test statistic, Z and t, and the link to the confidence interval

The idea

Every test in this lecture has the same shape. You take the estimate minus the value \(H_0\) claims, and divide by the standard error. The result tells you how many standard errors the data sit from what \(H_0\) predicts (HT p.8). If that distance is past the critical value, the data are "unlikely" under \(H_0\) and you reject.

When \(\sigma\) is known (or \(n\) is large, roughly over 30), you use the normal table and get a Z test. When you only have the sample standard deviation \(s\) and \(n\) is small, the extra uncertainty in \(s\) makes the distribution wider, so you use the t table with \(n-1\) degrees of freedom. One degree of freedom is spent estimating the mean before you estimate the spread, which is why it's \(n-1\). Past roughly \(n = 30\) the t and Z distributions are indistinguishable, so the choice only matters for small samples (p.11). The t test is the one you'll actually use in practice, and the Z test is the teaching device.

A two-sided test and a confidence interval are the same arithmetic read two ways. You reject \(H_0: \mu = \mu_0\) at level \(\alpha\) exactly when \(\mu_0\) falls outside the \(100(1-\alpha)\%\) interval (p.8-9). That's why the lecture calls a test "a confidence interval with everything but the verdict thrown away" (p.3).

What to know

\[Z = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{\sigma/\sqrt{n}} \qquad\qquad t = \frac{\bar{x} - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt{n}}, \ \ n-1 \text{ degrees of freedom}\]
SituationStatisticTwo-sided cutoff
\(\sigma\) known, or \(n\) large (use \(s\) for \(\sigma\))\(Z\)\(z[\alpha/2]\), 1.96 at \(\alpha = 0.05\) (p.8)
\(\sigma\) unknown, \(n\) small, population roughly normal\(t\)\(t[\alpha/2,\,n-1]\), e.g. 2.776 for \(n = 5\) (p.12)
Small \(n\), clearly not normalA rank-based test instead (p.11). The study guide names Mann-Whitney U (two independent samples) and Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired).

The recipe on p.15 fixes the order. State \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) and the direction, choose \(\alpha\), choose \(n\) from a power calculation, choose the test, then collect the data and compute the statistic once, and report the verdict, p, the effect size with its interval, and the planned \(n\). Steps 1 to 4 happen before any data exist, and that ordering is what makes \(\alpha\) mean anything.

The study guide adds the paired case. When both systems are measured on the same units (same benchmarks, same seeds), take the per-unit differences first and run a one-sample t test of their mean against 0, with \(t = \bar{d}/(s_d/\sqrt{n})\).

The study guide's formula sheet also has three tests that aren't on the slides but follow the same "estimate minus null, over standard error" shape.

TestStatisticWhen
Two proportions\(Z = \dfrac{\hat{p}_1 - \hat{p}_2}{\sqrt{\hat{p}(1-\hat{p})(1/n_1 + 1/n_2)}}\)Two independent groups with a yes/no outcome (A/B conversion). \(\hat{p}\) is the pooled proportion, total successes over total trials, because under \(H_0\) both groups share one rate.
McNemar\(Z = \dfrac{b - c}{\sqrt{b + c}}\)Two classifiers on the same test items. \(b\) is items only A got right, \(c\) is items only B got right, and you want \(b + c \gtrsim 25\). Items they agree on carry no information about which is better, so they drop out. It's the paired design for yes/no outcomes.
Welch (two independent means)\(t = \dfrac{\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2}{\sqrt{s_1^2/n_1 + s_2^2/n_2}}\)Degrees of freedom from the Welch-Satterthwaite formula, so in practice software. This one is developed in the comparing-alternatives lecture.

Worked example

Z test, HW5 Problem 1. \(\mu_0 = 2.5\) hours, \(\sigma = 0.8\) known, \(n = 36\), \(\bar{x} = 2.74\), \(\alpha = 0.05\), two-sided.

\[\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{0.8}{6} = 0.1333, \qquad Z = \frac{2.74 - 2.5}{0.1333} = \frac{0.24}{0.1333} = 1.80.\]

Since \(1.80 < 1.96\), you fail to reject \(H_0\). Check with the interval. \(1.96 \times 0.1333 = 0.2613\), so the 95% interval is \(2.74 \pm 0.261 = (2.479, 3.001)\). It contains 2.5, so the verdict matches.

Smallest mean that rejects (HW5 Problem 1.3). At \(\alpha = 0.01\) two-sided, the cutoff is \(z[0.005] = 2.576\). You need \((\bar{x} - 2.5)/0.1333 > 2.576\), so

\[\bar{x} > 2.5 + 2.576 \times 0.1333 = 2.5 + 0.3435 = 2.8435 \approx 2.84 \text{ hours.}\]

If the alternative were one-sided (\(\mu > 2.5\)) the cutoff would be \(z[0.01] = 2.326\), and the bound becomes \(2.5 + 2.326 \times 0.1333 = 2.5 + 0.3101 = 2.810\) hours. HW5 kept the two-sided version because \(H_0\) was stated without a direction.

Large-n test with s, HW5 Problem 2.2. \(n = 50\), \(\bar{x} = 706\), \(s = 96.1\), \(H_1: \mu < 740\), \(\alpha = 0.01\). Since \(n > 30\), use Z with \(s\) in place of \(\sigma\) (p.11).

\[\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{96.1}{7.071} = 13.59, \qquad Z = \frac{706 - 740}{13.59} = \frac{-34}{13.59} = -2.50.\]

The one-sided lower cutoff is \(-z[0.01] = -2.326\). Since \(-2.50 < -2.326\), reject \(H_0\). The data support a mean time to first fault below 740 hours. (With the t table at 49 degrees of freedom the cutoff is about 2.405, and you still reject.)

t test where Z would mislead. Nine runs of a benchmark give \(\bar{x} = 10.6\) s and \(s = 0.9\) s. Is the mean different from 10 s at \(\alpha = 0.05\)?

\[\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{0.9}{3} = 0.3, \qquad t = \frac{10.6 - 10}{0.3} = 2.0, \qquad t[0.025,\,8] = 2.306.\]

Since \(2.0 < 2.306\), fail to reject. The 95% interval is \(10.6 \pm 2.306 \times 0.3 = 10.6 \pm 0.692 = (9.908, 11.292)\), which contains 10. If you'd wrongly used 1.96 you would have rejected, because \(2.0 > 1.96\). With small \(n\) the t cutoff matters.

The slide's own t example (HT p.12). Five files, \(\bar{x} = 12.3\) KB, \(s = 0.5\) KB, sizes assumed normal, \(H_0: \mu = 12.35\), two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

\[\frac{s}{\sqrt{n}} = \frac{0.5}{\sqrt{5}} = \frac{0.5}{2.236} = 0.2236, \qquad t = \frac{12.3 - 12.35}{0.2236} = \frac{-0.05}{0.2236} = -0.224, \qquad t[0.025,\,4] = 2.776.\]

\(|-0.224|\) sits well inside \(\pm 2.776\), so don't reject (the slide gives \(p = 0.83\)). The 95% interval is \(12.3 \pm 2.776 \times 0.2236 = 12.3 \pm 0.621 = (11.68, 12.92)\) KB. It contains 12.35 and almost everything else nearby, so "no difference" here means "with five files we couldn't have seen one" (p.12).

Two proportions. An A/B test gives 800 users each variant. A converts 144 (\(\hat{p}_A = 0.18\)) and B converts 176 (\(\hat{p}_B = 0.22\)). Two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\). The pooled rate is \(320/1600 = 0.20\), so

\[\text{SE} = \sqrt{0.20 \times 0.80 \times \left(\tfrac{1}{800} + \tfrac{1}{800}\right)} = \sqrt{0.16 \times 0.0025} = \sqrt{0.0004} = 0.02, \qquad Z = \frac{0.22 - 0.18}{0.02} = 2.0.\]

Since \(2.0 > 1.96\), reject, with \(p = 2 \times 0.0228 = 0.0456\). Compare study guide question 5, where 520 vs 580 out of 10,000 each gives a pooled rate of \(1100/20000 = 0.055\), a standard error of 0.00322, \(Z = 0.006/0.00322 = 1.86\) and \(p = 2 \times 0.0314 = 0.063\). It doesn't reject, because base rates near 5% need very large samples.

McNemar. Two classifiers on the same 500 items. A alone is right on 38, B alone on 20 (study guide question 6). \(Z = (38 - 20)/\sqrt{58} = 18/7.62 = 2.36 > 1.96\), so reject, \(p = 2 \times 0.0091 = 0.018\). A two-proportion test would be the wrong tool, since both classifiers answer the same items and their correctness is correlated (easy items are easy for both).

Easy to get wrong

  • Dividing by \(\sigma\) instead of \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\). The test is about the mean, so you need the standard error of the mean.
  • Using the normal cutoff with small \(n\) and an estimated \(s\). Use \(t[\alpha/2,\,n-1]\), and remember the degrees of freedom are \(n-1\), not \(n\) (HT p.11).
  • Running an unpaired test on paired data. The between-unit spread swamps the effect and you throw away most of the power (study guide, question 4).
  • Forgetting that a rank-based test answers a somewhat different null hypothesis, not the same one more robustly (study guide).
  • Using the separate rates \(\hat{p}_1\) and \(\hat{p}_2\) in the two-proportion standard error. Under \(H_0\) the rates are equal, so you use the pooled \(\hat{p}\).
  • Putting the agreements into McNemar. Only the disagreements \(b\) and \(c\) enter the statistic.

The p-value, and what it isn't

The idea

The critical-value approach gives you one bit, reject or not. The p-value gives the same decision on a finer scale. It's the probability, computed assuming \(H_0\) is true, of getting a test statistic at least as extreme as the one you got (HT p.13). You reject when \(p \le \alpha\), which always matches comparing \(Z\) with the cutoff.

Reporting the actual value is better than writing "\(p < 0.05\)", because 0.049 and 0.0001 aren't the same evidence (p.13). But the p-value is a statement about the data given a hypothesis. It's never a statement about the hypothesis given the data, and most misuses come from flipping that around.

What to know

\[p = \Pr[\text{a statistic at least this extreme} \mid H_0 \text{ true}]\]

Two-sided, \(p = 2\Pr[Z \ge |z|]\). One-sided, \(p\) is the single tail in the direction of \(H_1\) (study guide, formula sheet). For a t statistic you usually can't get an exact p from a printed table, so you bracket it (for example "\(0.01 < p < 0.05\)") using the columns of the t table.

What a p-value is not (p.14).

MisreadingWhy it's wrong
The probability that \(H_0\) is trueThat would need a prior, and a test has none. The p-value is already conditioned on \(H_0\).
The probability your result was a flukeSame mistake in different words.
A measure of sizep depends on \(n\) at least as much as on the difference.
A sharp boundary0.06 and 0.04 are nearly the same evidence.
Support for \(H_0\) when largeWith \(n = 5\) you might not have been able to see anything. Absence of evidence isn't evidence of absence.
A claim about the world"Significant" is a word about the test.
