One more from the study guide's true/false list. At \(\alpha = 0.05\) it's not true that 5% of significant published results are false positives. \(\alpha\) is the rate among true nulls, and the share of false claims among significant results also depends on power and on how many tested effects are real.

Worked example

Two-sided p, HW5 Problem 1.2. \(Z = 1.80\). The table gives \(\Pr[Z \ge 1.80] = 0.0359\). Two-sided, so double it.

\[p = 2 \times 0.0359 = 0.0718.\]

Since \(0.0718 > 0.05\), fail to reject, the same verdict as part 1. In words, if the true mean really were 2.5 hours, a sample mean at least 0.24 hours away from 2.5 (either side) would turn up about 7% of the time.

One-sided p, HW5 Problem 2.2. \(Z = -2.50\) with \(H_1: \mu < 740\). Take only the lower tail, \(p = \Pr[Z \le -2.50] = 0.0062\). That's below 0.01, so reject, matching the critical-value test.

The slide example (HT p.10). \(n = 50\) files, \(\bar{x} = 12.3\) KB, \(\sigma = 0.5\), \(H_0: \mu = 12.5\), two-sided. \(\sigma/\sqrt{n} = 0.5/7.071 = 0.0707\), so \(Z = -0.2/0.0707 = -2.83\). The table gives \(\Pr[Z \ge 2.83] = 0.0023\), so \(p = 2 \times 0.0023 = 0.0046 \approx 0.005\). Reject. The interval is \(12.3 \pm 1.96 \times 0.0707 = 12.3 \pm 0.139 = (12.16, 12.44)\), which excludes 12.5. The difference is 0.2 KB, and whether that matters is a separate question the test doesn't ask.

Easy to get wrong

  • Forgetting to double the tail for a two-sided test, or doubling it for a one-sided test.
  • Writing "p = 0.03, so there's a 3% chance \(H_0\) is true". The correct sentence is "if \(H_0\) were true, data this extreme would occur about 3% of the time" (HT p.13-14).
  • Reading a small p as a big effect. The slide 17 table makes a 0.3 ms effect "highly significant" just by raising \(n\).
  • Reporting only "\(p < 0.05\)". Give the value, plus the effect size and interval (p.13, p.15).

Type I and Type II errors, power, and how many runs

The idea

A test can be wrong in two ways. It can reject a true \(H_0\) (a false positive, Type I) or fail to reject a false one (a missed effect, Type II). You pick the Type I rate directly, since it's \(\alpha\). You don't pick the Type II rate \(\beta\). It follows from \(\alpha\), from \(n\), from the noise \(\sigma\), and from how big the real effect is (HT p.6).

At a fixed \(n\), lowering \(\alpha\) raises \(\beta\), because a stricter cutoff means you also miss more real effects. The only way to lower both is more data (p.6). That's why choosing \(n\) is part of the design, done before you collect anything (p.15, p.19). A test that almost never gives false positives because it almost never rejects anything is useless, not careful (study guide).

What to know

The four outcomes, with your decision down the side and reality across the top (p.7).

\(H_0\) true\(H_0\) false
Do not reject \(H_0\)Correct, confidence \(= 1-\alpha\)Type II error, \(\Pr = \beta\)
Reject \(H_0\)Type I error, \(\Pr = \alpha\)Correct, power \(= 1-\beta\)

You never find out which column you're in. You control the left column with \(\alpha\), and the right column has to be bought with sample size (p.7). A published result is one cell of this table reported without the table. And \(\alpha = 0.05\) is a convention, not a law of nature, it's just the false-positive rate you agreed to (p.6).

Why power 0.80? It's Cohen's convention. Pairing \(\beta = 0.20\) with \(\alpha = 0.05\) treats a missed effect as a quarter as serious as a false alarm, since \(0.05/0.20 = 1/4\) (p.19).

Same table, other fields. The handout reads a membership inference attack as a test of \(H_0\) "this example wasn't in the training set". The false-positive rate is \(\alpha\) and the true-positive rate is power. Carlini et al. argue you should fix \(\alpha\) very small first and then ask about power (the Neyman-Pearson ordering), instead of quoting accuracy or AUC, which average over all thresholds (Hypothesis Testing in the Wild, entry 9, and study guide question 19).

Power for a two-sided one-sample Z test (study guide formula sheet), with \(\delta\) the true \(\mu - \mu_0\).

\[1 - \beta \approx \Pr\!\left[Z > z[\alpha/2] - \frac{|\delta|\sqrt{n}}{\sigma}\right]\]

Sample size for a target power (p.19).

\[n = \left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\,\sigma}{\delta}\right)^2\]
InputWhere it comes from
\(\delta\)The smallest difference you'd care about. A judgement, not a statistic.
\(\sigma\)A pilot run or published measurements.
Usual targetPower 0.80 at \(\alpha = 0.05\), so \((1.96 + 0.84)^2 \approx 7.8\) and \(n \approx 7.8(\sigma/\delta)^2\).

Halving \(\delta\) multiplies \(n\) by four. Power 0.90 needs about 34% more runs than 0.80, and 0.95 needs about 66% more (p.19). For two independent groups the study guide doubles it, \(n = 2((z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\sigma/\delta)^2\) per group, and for a one-sided test you use \(z[\alpha]\) in place of \(z[\alpha/2]\). Round \(n\) up.

Power computed after the fact from your own p-value tells you nothing, and an underpowered study doesn't only miss effects, the ones it finds come out exaggerated (p.19).

Worked example

Power. \(H_0: \mu = 50\), two-sided, \(\sigma = 10\), \(n = 25\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). The true mean is 54. (Study guide question 7.)

The standard error is \(10/\sqrt{25} = 2\). You reject when \(|\bar{x} - 50| > 1.96 \times 2 = 3.92\). If \(\mu = 54\), \(\bar{x}\) is centred 4 above 50, which is \(4/2 = 2\) standard errors.

\[1 - \beta \approx \Pr[Z > 1.96 - 2] = \Pr[Z > -0.04] = 0.516, \qquad \beta \approx 0.484.\]

(The lower rejection tail, \(\Pr[Z < -1.96 - 2]\), is negligible.) This experiment misses a real 4-unit effect about half the time, so a "no significant difference" from it says very little.

How many runs for power 0.80? Same \(\sigma = 10\) and \(\delta = 4\).

\[n = \left(\frac{(1.96 + 0.84) \times 10}{4}\right)^2 = (2.8 \times 2.5)^2 = 7^2 = 49.\]

Check it. With \(n = 49\), the standard error is \(10/7 = 1.429\), and \(\delta\) is \(4/1.429 = 2.8\) standard errors. Power \(= \Pr[Z > 1.96 - 2.8] = \Pr[Z > -0.84] = 0.80\). (With the more precise \(z[0.20] = 0.842\) you get \(49.07\), which rounds up to 50.) To detect \(\delta = 2\) instead, you'd need \(4 \times 49 = 196\).

Paired versus two groups (study guide question 9). A pilot gives \(\sigma \approx 12\) minutes for job completion time. You want to detect a 5-minute reduction, two-sided, \(\alpha = 0.05\), power 0.80, with \(z[0.20] = 0.842\).

\[n = \left(\frac{(1.960 + 0.842) \times 12}{5}\right)^2 = (2.802 \times 2.4)^2 = 6.725^2 = 45.2 \to 46 \text{ jobs, each run under both schedulers.}\]

With two independent groups, double it, \(2 \times 45.2 = 90.4 \to 91\) jobs per group, 182 in total. In practice the paired \(\sigma\) (of per-job differences) is far smaller than 12, because job-to-job variation cancels, so pairing needs even fewer runs than 46.

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking \(\beta = 1 - \alpha\). They're probabilities under different realities, and \(\beta\) depends on \(n\), \(\sigma\) and the true effect (HT p.6).
  • Swapping the labels. Type I is the false positive (rejecting a true null), Type II is the miss.
  • Using \(z[\beta] = 1.96\). For power 0.80, \(\beta = 0.20\) and \(z[0.20] = 0.84\).
  • Rounding \(n\) down. Always round up, or you fall short of the target power.
  • Calling a method "safe" because its false-positive rate is low. The handout's entry 1 (Bouthillier et al.) found that comparing average scores kept false positives under 5% but missed about 90% of real improvements. Low \(\alpha\) bought with no power is the useless test the study guide warns about.
  • "We ran it five times" isn't a sample size, it's the absence of one (p.19).

Effect size versus significance, and why intervals beat tests

The idea

The statistic is effect divided by \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\), so \(n\) is half the formula. Hold the effect fixed and raise \(n\), and Z grows like \(\sqrt{n}\), so quadrupling the data doubles Z for the same effect (study guide formula sheet). With enough data every non-zero effect becomes significant, and with too little data an important one won't be (HT p.17). Significance is partly a statement about \(n\), so it can't tell you whether a difference matters.

Jain's argument (p.16) is that a test gives you one bit, while an interval gives you the same bit plus the size of the effect and how precisely you know it. Intervals of \((-100, 100)\) and \((-1, 1)\) both contain 0, so both say "not significant". The first means you have no idea. The second means any effect is tiny. Only the interval tells those apart, so report the interval.

What to know

The p.17 table, effect 0.3 ms and \(\sigma = 50\) ms in every row.

\(n\)\(Z\)pVerdict
1,0000.190.85not significant
100,0001.900.058not significant
1,000,0006.00\(2 \times 10^{-9}\)"highly significant"

Ways to state how big an effect is (p.18).

MeasureFormula or formUsed in
Raw differenceOriginal units, e.g. 0.3 msSystems and networking
Relative changePercent or speedup ratioArchitecture, compilers (geometric mean over benchmarks)
Cohen's d\((\bar{x}_1 - \bar{x}_2)/s\), with \(s\) pooled. Paired is \(\bar{d}/s_d\) (study guide)HCI, CS education, meta-analyses
Probability of superiority \(A_{12}\)\(\Pr[X_A > X_B] + \tfrac12\Pr[X_A = X_B]\), 0.5 means no difference (the tie term is from the study guide formula sheet)Fuzzing, test generation (ROC AUC is the same quantity)

Cohen's small, medium and large labels (0.2, 0.5, 0.8) come from behavioural science. With low-noise measurements \(d = 5\) can still be a negligible speedup, so don't lean on the labels (p.18). Before the experiment, decide the smallest effect that matters, since it's the \(\delta\) that sets \(n\). After it, report the effect with an interval.

Worked example

Same effect, bigger n. At \(n = 1{,}000{,}000\), \(\sigma/\sqrt{n} = 50/1000 = 0.05\), so \(Z = 0.3/0.05 = 6.0\). At \(n = 1000\), \(\sqrt{1000} \approx 31.6\), so the standard error is \(50/31.6 = 1.58\) and \(Z = 0.3/1.58 = 0.19\). The effect never changed. A 0.3 ms drop on a 250 ms page load is \(0.3/250 = 0.12\%\), which nobody notices (p.17).

Significant but small. A scheduler is replayed against a baseline on 16 paired job traces. Mean completion time drops from 40.0 to 39.2 minutes. The 16 per-trace differences have \(\bar{d} = 0.8\) and \(s_d = 0.4\).

\[\text{SE} = \frac{0.4}{\sqrt{16}} = 0.1, \qquad t = \frac{0.8}{0.1} = 8.0, \qquad t[0.025,\,15] = 2.131.\]

Reject easily. Now the size. The raw difference is 0.8 minutes, and relative to 40 minutes that's \(0.8/40 = 2\%\). Cohen's \(d = 0.8/0.4 = 2.0\), "large" by the labels, but only because replaying a trace is low-noise. The 95% interval is \(0.8 \pm 2.131 \times 0.1 = 0.8 \pm 0.213 = (0.587, 1.013)\) minutes. The sentence to write is "reduced mean completion time by 0.8 min (2%), 95% CI 0.59 to 1.01 min", not "a large effect".

Study guide question 21 is the same story with 12 replays, 42.0 to 41.2 minutes and \(s_d = 0.5\). The standard error is \(0.5/\sqrt{12} = 0.5/3.464 = 0.144\), so \(t = 0.8/0.144 = 5.54\) against \(t[0.025,\,11] = 2.201\), and you reject. The relative change is \(0.8/42.0 = 1.9\%\) and \(d = 0.8/0.5 = 1.6\), "large" only because replays are low-noise. What the authors should have fixed beforehand is the smallest improvement worth reporting, say 5%, since that \(\delta\) drives the power analysis and shows the real effect is well below it.

Computing \(A_{12}\). Fuzzer A covers 3, 5, 7 (thousand branches) in three runs and B covers 2, 4, 6. Of the \(3 \times 3 = 9\) pairs, A wins when it's 3 vs 2 (1 pair), 5 vs 2 or 4 (2 pairs), 7 vs anything (3 pairs). That's 6 wins and no ties, so \(A_{12} = 6/9 = 0.67\).

Easy to get wrong

  • Writing "highly significant" as if it meant "large". It means the test is confident the effect isn't zero (HT p.17).
  • Writing "not significant, so the two are equivalent". Look at the interval width first (p.16). Showing equivalence needs a narrow interval around zero or an equivalence test (study guide question 15c).
  • Quoting Cohen's labels for systems measurements. Give the raw and relative change too.
  • A table whose only number is p isn't reporting a result (p.14).

Independence, the experimental unit, and interference

The idea

The standard error \(\sigma/\sqrt{n}\) assumes \(n\) independent observations (HT p.20). Real measurements often aren't independent. Consecutive latency samples share caches, queues and background load. Requests from one user share that user. Runs on one boot share that boot. If you treat 10,000 requests from one run of each system as \(n = 10{,}000\), the standard error comes out far too small and the test rejects far too often.

The fix is to count the right thing. The experimental unit is what's independently replicated (a run, a machine, a benchmark, a user), and \(n\) counts units, not measurements (p.20). Ten thousand requests from one run is one replicate per system.

Interference is worse than correlation. If treated units change the outcomes of control units, the test measures the wrong quantity, and more data narrows the interval around that wrong quantity (p.22). This is Spang et al., the paper from HW5 Problem 3.

What to know

Lag-1 autocorrelation is \(\rho = \text{Corr}(x_t, x_{t+1}) = \text{Cov}(x_t, x_{t+1})/\sigma^2\), and it lies between \(-1\) and \(+1\). At \(\rho = 0\) each run is unrelated to the one before it. With it (p.21),

\[\text{Var}(\bar{x}) \approx \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\cdot\frac{1+\rho}{1-\rho}, \qquad n_{\text{eff}} \approx n\,\frac{1-\rho}{1+\rho}.\]

For \(\rho > 0\) (drift, warm-up, caches) \(n_{\text{eff}} < n\), and the naive Z is too big by \(\sqrt{(1+\rho)/(1-\rho)}\). At \(\rho = 0.5\), \(n_{\text{eff}} = n/3\) and Z is too big by \(\sqrt{3}\). The actual Type I rate at a nominal 0.05 is about 11% at \(\rho = 0.2\), 26% at 0.5, 51% at 0.8 and 65% at 0.9 (p.21). For \(\rho < 0\) (high and low alternating, like garbage collection every other run) \(n_{\text{eff}} > n\).

Designing for independence (p.22).

MoveWhat you do
Analyse at the unitOne summary per run (mean, p99), then test across runs. For one long correlated series the study guide adds batch means, which average consecutive blocks and treat the block means as the observations
Replicate what variesSeparate runs, reboots, machines, days
RandomiseInterleave the order the alternatives are measured in
Block or pairSame benchmark, machine and time for both
CheckPlot in time order, look at the lag-1 correlation
InterferenceRandomise whole clusters, not individual units, or switch all units between treatments over time

Spang et al. (IMC 2021) on Netflix bitrate capping. Tested on a small fraction of traffic, capping looked like about 5% lower throughput. When most traffic was capped it gave about 12% higher throughput (p.22). The naive A/B test wasn't miscomputed. It measured the effect of treating 5% of traffic on a shared congested link, which isn't the effect of capping everyone. The handout calls this "a valid test of the wrong quantity". The assumption that broke has a name, SUTVA (Stable Unit Treatment Value Assumption), meaning one unit's outcome doesn't depend on which treatment other units got. Their fix was paired links with different treatment allocations (one link mostly treated, a similar link mostly control, then compare links). A switchback design is the other fix, where a whole link or region flips between treatment and control over time so each period is all one or all the other (Hypothesis Testing in the Wild, entry 8, and study guide question 14).

The same unit question shows up in ML benchmarks. If you vary only the weight-initialization seed, your runs miss the variance from data splits, data order, augmentation and hyperparameter search. The standard error comes out too small and the Type I rate goes above \(\alpha\), so vary everything that would differ if someone else reran the pipeline (study guide question 13, handout entry 1).

Worked example

Two server configurations, 400 consecutive latencies from one run of each. The mean difference is 1.5 ms, and the usual formula gives a standard error of 0.5 ms (study guide question 22).

Naive. \(Z = 1.5/0.5 = 3.0\), and \(p = 2 \times 0.00135 = 0.0027\). Looks strong.

Corrected for \(\rho = 0.6\).

\[n_{\text{eff}} = 400 \times \frac{1 - 0.6}{1 + 0.6} = 400 \times \frac{0.4}{1.6} = 400 \times 0.25 = 100.\]

The variance factor is \(1.6/0.4 = 4\), so the standard error doubles to \(0.5 \times \sqrt{4} = 1.0\) ms. Then \(Z = 1.5/1.0 = 1.5\), and \(p = 2 \times 0.0668 = 0.134\). Not significant.

Even the corrected test only compares these two runs. The run is the unit, and there's one of each, so machine, boot and background load are tangled with the configuration. The real fix is many interleaved runs per configuration with one summary per run.

HW5 Problem 3 reading. The HW5 answer counted hourly cells as \(n\), not the roughly fourteen million sessions, because sessions in the same hour share the same congestion. That's the p.20 rule applied.

Easy to get wrong

  • Treating more samples from the same run as more evidence. They sharpen a wrong answer (HT p.21).
  • Thinking correlation only widens the interval. It invalidates it, because the Type I rate is no longer \(\alpha\) (p.21).
  • Believing a fancier test fixes interference. No choice of test repairs observations that influence each other (study guide).
  • Plugging \(\rho\) in upside down. Positive \(\rho\) shrinks \(n_{\text{eff}}\), and \((1-\rho)/(1+\rho)\) is below 1 when \(\rho > 0\).

Peeking and multiple comparisons

The idea

\(\alpha\) is the false-positive rate of one test, run once, at an \(n\) fixed in advance. Two common habits break that. The first is peeking, which means computing p after every batch and stopping as soon as it drops below \(\alpha\) (HT p.23). Every look is a fresh chance for noise to cross the line, so the procedure's Type I rate climbs even though each p is computed correctly.

The second is running many tests. A real evaluation compares several systems on several workloads and metrics. Each test has a 5% chance of a false positive, so a big table almost surely has some (p.26). These are the same failure, many chances to cross the same line (p.32). Both are fixed by deciding the stopping rule, the family of tests and the correction before you see any data.

What to know

Peeking costs (simulated A/A test, p.24). At a nominal 0.05 the actual Type I rate is 5% with 1 look, about 8% with 2, 14% with 5, 19% with 10, 25% with 20, 32% with 50 and 37% with 100. The study guide puts 14 daily looks at about 22%. Each p-value at the stopping point is computed correctly. It's the whole procedure whose Type I rate is no longer \(\alpha\) (p.23). Keep looking and you'll always reject. Rules that keep \(\alpha\) (p.25) are a fixed horizon (pick \(n\) by power analysis, look once), a group sequential design with a stricter boundary planned in advance (Pocock uses \(|Z| > 2.41\) at each of 5 looks, and the study guide gives 2.18 for 2 looks, while O'Brien-Fleming is very strict early and near 1.96 at the end), and always-valid p-values (sequential probability ratio tests, used in A/B platforms, which allow any stopping rule). Stopping for a deadline or budget is fine, since that isn't driven by the result. Stopping the moment you hit significance also inflates the effect you report, so state the stopping rule either way.

Counting tests. \(k\) systems compared pairwise give \(k(k-1)/2\) tests, times every benchmark and metric. The slide's example is 5 schedulers \(\times\) 8 workloads \(\times\) 3 metrics \(= 240\) tests, and with nothing real you'd expect \(240 \times 0.05 = 12\) "findings". The family is the set of tests whose conclusions are read together, and you have to define it yourself. Picking out the significant results afterwards and reporting only those doesn't keep the Type I rate at \(\alpha\) (p.26).

Family-wise error rate (p.27), the probability of at least one false positive among \(m\) independent tests with every null true.

\[\text{FWER} = 1 - (1-\alpha)^m, \qquad \text{FWER} \le m\alpha \text{ for any dependence.}\]

At \(\alpha = 0.05\) that's 23% for \(m = 5\), 40% for \(m = 10\), 64% for \(m = 20\), 92% for \(m = 50\) and 99% for \(m = 100\). Twenty tests is a coin flip on whether your table holds a false claim (p.27).

ProcedureRuleControls
BonferroniReject if \(p \le \alpha/m\)FWER, any dependence
HolmSort the p's. Reject \(p_{(k)}\) while \(p_{(k)} \le \alpha/(m-k+1)\), and stop at the first failureFWER, never fewer rejections than Bonferroni
Benjamini-HochbergFind the largest \(k\) with \(p_{(k)} \le k\alpha/m\), and reject \(p_{(1)}\) through \(p_{(k)}\)FDR, the expected fraction of rejections that are false

Use FWER when one false claim is costly (a headline result) and FDR when you're screening many candidates for follow-up (p.28). Once you have the p-values there's no reason to use plain Bonferroni over Holm. Bonferroni costs power, since at \(m = 10\) the two-sided cutoff rises from \(z[0.025] = 1.96\) to \(z[0.0025] = 2.81\). The same idea gives simultaneous intervals, where each of \(m\) intervals is built at level \(1 - \alpha/m\), so you use \(z[\alpha/(2m)]\) and all \(m\) cover together with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\) (study guide).

When you compare more than two systems at once, the study guide's route is one omnibus test first (ANOVA, or Friedman if the data aren't normal) and then a post-hoc procedure built for all pairs (Tukey HSD, Nemenyi). That machinery comes in the later ANOVA and comparing-alternatives lectures. Either way, report every test you ran, not only the significant ones.

Worked example

FWER by hand. For \(m = 20\), square repeatedly. \(0.95^2 = 0.9025\), \(0.95^4 = 0.8145\), \(0.95^5 = 0.8145 \times 0.95 = 0.7738\), \(0.95^{10} = 0.7738^2 = 0.5987\), \(0.95^{20} = 0.5987^2 = 0.3585\). So FWER \(= 1 - 0.3585 = 0.64\). The bound \(m\alpha = 1.0\) is useless here, which is why you compute the exact form. HW5 Problem 3 used this for Spang et al.'s 28 intervals. \(0.95^{8} = 0.95^5 \times 0.95^2 \times 0.95 = 0.7738 \times 0.9025 \times 0.95 = 0.6634\), so \(0.95^{28} = 0.3585 \times 0.6634 = 0.2378\) and FWER \(\approx 76\%\).

Six p-values, four procedures (p.29). Sorted p-values 0.001, 0.008, 0.012, 0.030, 0.041, 0.200, with \(\alpha = 0.05\) and \(m = 6\).

\(k\)\(p_{(k)}\)Bonferroni \(0.05/6\)Holm \(0.05/(7-k)\)BH \(k \times 0.05/6\)
10.0010.0083, reject0.0083, reject0.0083, yes
20.0080.0083, reject0.0100, reject0.0167, yes
30.0120.0083, no0.0125, reject0.0250, yes
40.0300.0083, no0.0167, fail, stop0.0333, yes
50.0410.0083, nonot tested0.0417, yes
60.2000.0083, nonot tested0.0500, no

No correction rejects 5 (everything under 0.05). Bonferroni rejects 2, Holm rejects 3, and BH rejects 5 because the largest \(k\) that passes is 5 (\(0.041 \le 0.0417\)). Same data, four answers, which is why you choose the procedure first.

Peeking with a plan. A student gets p = 0.07 at 10 trials, runs 10 more and gets p = 0.04. That's two looks, so the real Type I rate is about 8%, not 5%. Had they planned a 2-look Pocock design, each look would need \(|Z| > 2.18\), which is about \(p < 0.029\). So 0.04 wouldn't count (study guide question 24).

Simultaneous intervals (study guide question 25). Four schedulers against a baseline, reductions with large-sample SE of 1.0 min each, S1 3.1, S2 2.4, S3 1.2, S4 0.6. Ordinary 95% intervals are estimate \(\pm 1.96\). S1 gives (1.14, 5.06) and S2 gives (0.44, 4.36), so both exclude 0, while S3 (\(-0.76\), 3.16) and S4 (\(-1.36\), 2.56) don't. Bonferroni with \(m = 4\) builds each at \(1 - 0.05/4 = 98.75\%\), using \(z[0.05/8] = z[0.00625] = 2.498\). Now S1 is (0.60, 5.60) and S2 is (\(-0.10\), 4.90), so only S1 stays significant. If all four true reductions were 0, the chance at least one ordinary interval excludes 0 is \(1 - 0.95^4 = 1 - 0.8145 = 0.185\).

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(m\alpha\) as the FWER. It's an upper bound and goes past 1 quickly. The exact value for independent tests is \(1 - (1-\alpha)^m\) (HT p.27).
  • Holm divides by \(m - k + 1\), so the first threshold is \(\alpha/m\) and the thresholds loosen. Stop at the first failure even if a later p would pass.
  • BH keeps going past failures. You want the largest \(k\) that passes, then reject everything up to it.
  • Reporting only the significant cells of a big table. A results table with one asterisk per benchmark is a family of tests (p.30).
  • "p = 0.07 after ten runs, let's run a few more" is peeking (p.23).

Six ways it goes wrong, and the papers in the wild

The idea

The second half of the lecture is a list of ways a correctly computed p-value stops meaning what it says. Slide 31 collects six of them, and five of the six are decided before any statistic is computed (HT p.31). So the fix is almost always in the design, not in a better formula.

The "Hypothesis Testing in the Wild" handout shows each idea in a real CS paper, and HW5 Problem 3 asked you to run its Part III checklist on one paper. For any paper it asks two things. What are \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) here, and which error is more expensive? Would an interval on the effect have told the reader more than the verdict?

What to know

The six ways (p.31). Choosing the direction of \(H_1\) after seeing the data. Reporting "not significant" as "equivalent" on \(n = 5\). Reporting p with no effect size, interval or sample size. Treating 10,000 requests from one run as 10,000 independent observations. Stopping the morning the dashboard turns green. Running 240 tests and reporting the twelve that cleared 0.05.

Four communities that hit multiple comparisons (p.30). FuzzBench uses per-benchmark Mann-Whitney tests plus Friedman/Nemenyi critical-difference diagrams across benchmarks. Otero et al. (IR) find unadjusted pairwise tests badly inflate Type I error and recommend Benjamini-Hochberg. Zhao et al. (data systems) point out every chart in an exploration tool is an implicit test, and 100 tests at 0.05 give about 13 "discoveries", roughly 40% bogus. Cockburn et al. (HCI) name p-hacking and HARKing.

Handout entryLecture idea it shows
1. Bouthillier et al., ML benchmarksAverage-score comparison had under 5% false positives but about 90% false negatives. Their test on \(P(A > B)\) needs the result to be significant and \(P(A > B) \ge 0.75\), and recommends 29 runs
2. Patterson et al., RL"5 runs is insufficient", skew breaks the t test at small n, and reporting the best of many tuned settings (maximization bias) inflates Type I error
3. Marie et al., machine translationSignificance testing fell after 2016, and about 40% of papers compared against copied scores computed differently, so the test compares two different things
4. Urbano et al., IRType I rate measured, not assumed. t and permutation tests hold \(\alpha\). Also Type III error, rejecting a two-sided \(H_0\) but concluding the wrong direction
5. FuzzBenchMann-Whitney U with the \(A_{12}\) effect size per benchmark, Friedman/Nemenyi across benchmarks
6. Daly et al., MongoDB regressionsA fixed 10% alert threshold ignores each test's variance, so noisy tests get high \(\alpha\) and quiet ones get high \(\beta\) at the same time
7. Johari et al., A/B testingContinuous monitoring can raise Type I error fivefold, and always-valid p-values fix it
8. Spang et al., NetflixInterference (SUTVA), a valid test of the wrong quantity (p.22)
9. Carlini et al., membership inferenceAn attack is a test, false-positive rate is \(\alpha\), true-positive rate is power
10. Cockburn et al., HCIHARKing (hypothesizing after the results are known) breaks step 1 of the recipe on p.15
11. Zhao et al., data explorationThe family isn't known in advance, so they use \(\alpha\)-investing to control false discoveries as tests arrive

The handout's eight pitfalls repeat slide 31 and add three. A p-value isn't the probability \(H_0\) is true, the test has to match how the data were collected (paired data need a paired test), and a valid test can still measure the wrong quantity.

Worked example

Counting FuzzBench's tests (handout entry 5). Ten fuzzers give \(10 \times 9/2 = 45\) pairs per benchmark. Over 20 benchmarks that's \(45 \times 20 = 900\) tests. With every null true and no correction you'd expect \(900 \times 0.05 = 45\) false positives. The Bonferroni threshold for the report would be \(0.05/900 \approx 0.0000556\), which is why the platform uses Friedman/Nemenyi instead of 900 separate corrected tests.

Reconstructing Zhao et al.'s numbers (handout entry 11). Suppose 10 of the 100 correlations are real and the test has power 0.8. The real ones give \(10 \times 0.8 = 8\) discoveries, and the 90 nulls give \(90 \times 0.05 = 4.5\) false ones. That's \(8 + 4.5 = 12.5\), about 13 discoveries, and \(4.5/12.5 = 36\%\) of them are false, close to the paper's 40%. This is why \(\alpha = 0.05\) doesn't mean 5% of significant results are false.

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking a better test fixes a design problem. Interference, copied baselines and maximization bias make the test answer a different question, and no test repairs that (handout pitfall 7).
  • Confusing Type III with Type II. Type III means you correctly rejected but named the wrong direction (handout entry 4).
  • Treating a single-threshold alarm as neutral. A fixed percentage cutoff sets a different \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) for every test, depending on its noise (handout entry 6).

Chi-square goodness of fit

The idea

The same test machinery works for checking whether data follow a claimed distribution. \(H_0\) says the data come from that distribution, and \(H_1\) says they don't. You bin the data, work out how many observations each bin should get under \(H_0\), and add up how far the observed counts are from those expected counts. A big total means the claimed distribution fits badly (chi-square goodness-of-fit handout). This isn't on the HT slides, so treat it as the handout's extension of the same logic.

The asymmetry from p.4 still applies. Failing to reject means the data are consistent with the distribution, not that the distribution is right.

What to know

\[\chi^2 = \sum_{i=1}^{k} \frac{(o_i - e_i)^2}{e_i}, \qquad \text{degrees of freedom} = k - m\]

Here \(o_i\) and \(e_i\) are observed and expected counts, \(k\) is the number of bins, and \(m\) is the number of quantities taken from the data to get the expected counts. When only the total is used, \(m = 1\). Reject if \(\chi^2\) is bigger than the upper-tail critical value \(\chi^2_{\alpha}\) at \(k - m\) degrees of freedom. It's always a one-tailed (upper) test, since only large discrepancies count against \(H_0\). Merge neighbouring bins so that no expected count is below 5 (handout).

Worked example

A load balancer is claimed to spread requests evenly over 4 servers. Out of 120 requests the servers got 36, 24, 33, 27. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\).

Under \(H_0\) each server expects \(120/4 = 30\). All expected counts are at least 5, so no merging.

\[\chi^2 = \frac{(36-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(24-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(33-30)^2}{30} + \frac{(27-30)^2}{30} = \frac{36 + 36 + 9 + 9}{30} = \frac{90}{30} = 3.0.\]

Only the total was used to get the expected counts, so the degrees of freedom are \(4 - 1 = 3\). The table gives \(\chi^2_{0.05} = 7.815\) at 3 degrees of freedom. Since \(3.0 < 7.815\), fail to reject. The counts are consistent with an even split.

In the handout's own example (radio messages vs a Poisson with \(\lambda = 4.6\)), 14 counts are merged into 10 bins, which gives \(\chi^2 = 6.749\) with \(10 - 1 = 9\) degrees of freedom, and \(H_0\) isn't rejected.

Easy to get wrong

  • Dividing by the observed count instead of the expected one.
  • Using \(k\) degrees of freedom. Subtract one for the total, and one more for each parameter you estimated from the data.
  • Leaving bins with expected counts under 5. Merge them first, then count \(k\).
  • The handout's example says \(\alpha = 0.01\) but quotes 16.919, which is the 0.05 value at 9 degrees of freedom. The 0.01 value is 21.666. The decision doesn't change because 6.749 is below both, but read your critical value from the right column.

Check yourself

1. You want to show a new cache cuts mean miss latency below the current 80 ns. Write \(H_0\) and \(H_1\), say one- or two-sided, and say why the claim doesn't go in \(H_0\).

\(H_0: \mu = 80\) (or \(\mu \ge 80\)) and \(H_1: \mu < 80\). It's one-sided because only a decrease supports the claim. The claim goes in \(H_1\) because a test can only give evidence by rejecting \(H_0\). Failing to reject is never evidence for \(H_0\), so if the claim sat in \(H_0\), a small noisy experiment would "support" it automatically (HT p.4-5).

2. \(\sigma = 6\) ms is known. You sample \(n = 36\) requests and get \(\bar{x} = 102\) ms. Test \(H_0: \mu = 100\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\), give p, and give the 95% interval.

The standard error is \(6/6 = 1\), so \(Z = (102 - 100)/1 = 2.0\). Since \(2.0 > 1.96\), reject. \(\Pr[Z \ge 2.0] = 0.0228\), so \(p = 2 \times 0.0228 = 0.0456\). The interval is \(102 \pm 1.96 = (100.04, 103.96)\), which just excludes 100, matching the verdict (HT p.8, p.13).

3. Same data as question 2, but \(\alpha = 0.01\). What's the smallest \(\bar{x}\) above 100 that rejects, two-sided and one-sided (\(H_1: \mu > 100\))?

Two-sided uses \(z[0.005] = 2.576\), so \(\bar{x} > 100 + 2.576 \times 1 = 102.576\). One-sided uses \(z[0.01] = 2.326\), so \(\bar{x} > 102.326\). This is the HW5 Problem 1.3 method. The observed 102 fails both at this \(\alpha\) (HT p.9).

4. Four runs give \(\bar{x} = 5.6\) s and \(s = 0.4\) s. Test \(H_0: \mu = 5\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\). \(t[0.025,\,3] = 3.182\).

Use t since \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n\) is small. The standard error is \(0.4/\sqrt{4} = 0.2\), so \(t = 0.6/0.2 = 3.0\) with 3 degrees of freedom. Since \(3.0 < 3.182\), fail to reject. A Z test would have rejected (\(3.0 > 1.96\)), which is exactly the mistake the t table protects you from (HT p.11-12).

5. A paper says "p = 0.02, so there's only a 2% chance our improvement is a fluke." What's wrong, and what should it say?

The p-value is computed assuming \(H_0\) is true, so it can't also be the probability that \(H_0\) is true or that the result is a fluke. That would need a prior. The correct reading is "if there were no improvement, a difference at least this large would show up about 2% of the time" (HT p.13-14).

6. A study reports "no significant difference (p = 0.30, n = 5), so the two systems are equivalent." Write the reviewer's objection.

Failing to reject isn't evidence for \(H_0\). With \(n = 5\) the test has little power, so a real difference could easily produce p = 0.30. Ask for the difference with a confidence interval. If the interval is wide, like \((-100, 100)\), the experiment can't say anything, and only a narrow interval around 0 supports "effectively equivalent" (HT p.14, p.16).

7. \(H_0: \mu = 20\), two-sided, \(\sigma = 8\), \(n = 16\), \(\alpha = 0.05\). The true mean is 24. Find the power, then the \(n\) needed for power 0.80 (use \(z[0.20] = 0.84\)).

The standard error is \(8/4 = 2\), and the true shift is \(4/2 = 2\) standard errors. Power \(\approx \Pr[Z > 1.96 - 2] = \Pr[Z > -0.04] = 0.516\), so \(\beta \approx 0.48\). For power 0.80, \(n = ((1.96 + 0.84) \times 8/4)^2 = (2.8 \times 2)^2 = 5.6^2 = 31.36\), which rounds up to 32 (HT p.19).

8. A web company reports page load fell from 250.0 to 249.7 ms with \(p < 10^{-9}\) over a million loads and calls it "highly significant". What's right and what's misleading?

Right, the 0.3 ms drop is very likely not zero. Misleading, "highly significant" sounds large, but the tiny p comes from the huge \(n\). With \(\sigma = 50\) ms and \(n = 10^6\), \(Z = 0.3/0.05 = 6\). The change is \(0.3/250 = 0.12\%\), which no user notices. They should report the raw and relative change with an interval and say what size would matter (HT p.17-18).

9. You record 900 consecutive latencies from one run of each of two systems, with lag-1 autocorrelation \(\rho = 0.8\). What's \(n_{\text{eff}}\) per series, by how much is the naive Z too big, and what's the experimental unit?

\(n_{\text{eff}} = 900 \times (1 - 0.8)/(1 + 0.8) = 900 \times 0.2/1.8 = 100\). The variance factor is \(1.8/0.2 = 9\), so the naive Z is too big by \(\sqrt{9} = 3\). At \(\rho = 0.8\) the actual Type I rate is about 51% at a nominal 5%. The experimental unit is the run, and there's one per system, so you need many independent runs with one summary each (HT p.20-21).

10. A paper compares 5 systems pairwise on 4 workloads. How many tests is that, and what's the FWER bound if every null is true? Then apply Holm at \(\alpha = 0.05\) to the four p-values 0.004, 0.011, 0.020, 0.300.

Five systems give \(5 \times 4/2 = 10\) pairs, times 4 workloads is 40 tests. The bound \(m\alpha = 40 \times 0.05 = 2\) is useless, and the independent-test value \(1 - 0.95^{40}\) is about 0.87. For Holm with \(m = 4\), compare 0.004 with \(0.05/4 = 0.0125\) (reject), 0.011 with \(0.05/3 = 0.0167\) (reject), 0.020 with \(0.05/2 = 0.025\) (reject), 0.300 with 0.05 (fail). Holm rejects 3. Bonferroni at \(0.0125\) would reject only 2 (HT p.26-28).

11. Same four p-values, Benjamini-Hochberg at \(\alpha = 0.05\). How many rejections, and when would you prefer BH over Holm?

Thresholds \(k \times 0.05/4\) are 0.0125, 0.025, 0.0375, 0.05. Checking, 0.004 \(\le\) 0.0125, 0.011 \(\le\) 0.025, 0.020 \(\le\) 0.0375, and 0.300 > 0.05. The largest passing \(k\) is 3, so reject 3. Use BH, which controls the false discovery rate, when screening many candidates for a follow-up study. Use Holm when a single false claim is costly, like a headline result (HT p.28).

12. A team checks its A/B dashboard every morning and plans to stop the first day p < 0.05. What goes wrong, and name two fixes.

Each look is another chance for noise to cross 1.96, so the procedure's Type I rate climbs well past 5% (about 14% for 5 looks, 25% for 20). The reported effect is also inflated. Fixes are a fixed horizon with \(n\) from a power analysis and one look, or a pre-planned group sequential design (Pocock \(|Z| > 2.41\) at each of 5 looks, or O'Brien-Fleming), or always-valid p-values (HT p.23-25).

13. A die is rolled 60 times and gives counts 8, 12, 9, 11, 6, 14. Is it fair at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? \(\chi^2_{0.05} = 11.070\) at 5 degrees of freedom.

Each face expects 10. The squared deviations are 4, 4, 1, 1, 16, 16, which sum to 42, so \(\chi^2 = 42/10 = 4.2\). Degrees of freedom are \(6 - 1 = 5\). Since \(4.2 < 11.070\), fail to reject. The counts are consistent with a fair die, which isn't the same as proving it fair (chi-square goodness-of-fit handout).

14. An A/B test gives 200 users each variant. A converts 92 and B converts 108. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\) whether the rates differ.

\(\hat{p}_A = 0.46\), \(\hat{p}_B = 0.54\), and the pooled rate is \(200/400 = 0.5\). The standard error is \(\sqrt{0.5 \times 0.5 \times (1/200 + 1/200)} = \sqrt{0.25 \times 0.01} = \sqrt{0.0025} = 0.05\). \(Z = 0.08/0.05 = 1.6\), below 1.96, so fail to reject, with \(p = 2 \times 0.0548 = 0.11\). An 8-point difference isn't distinguishable from noise at this size, which is a power problem, not evidence of no effect (study guide formula sheet, question 5).

15. Two classifiers are run on the same 400 items. Only A is right on 25, only B is right on 11, and they agree on the rest. Do their accuracies differ at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? Why not use the two-proportion test?

Use McNemar, since only disagreements say which is better. \(b + c = 36\), so \(\sqrt{36} = 6\) and \(Z = (25 - 11)/6 = 2.33\). That's above 1.96, so reject, with \(p = 2 \times 0.0099 = 0.020\). The two-proportion test assumes two independent samples, but both classifiers answer the same items and their correctness is correlated, so it overstates the variance and loses power (study guide question 6).

16. A paper makes 10 comparisons. One has estimate 2.5 with SE 1.0. Is it significant with an ordinary 95% interval, and with a Bonferroni-adjusted one?

Ordinary, \(2.5/1.0 = 2.5 > 1.96\), so the interval \(2.5 \pm 1.96 = (0.54, 4.46)\) excludes 0. With \(m = 10\), each interval uses \(z[0.05/20] = z[0.0025] = 2.81\), giving \(2.5 \pm 2.81 = (-0.31, 5.31)\), which contains 0. So it's not significant after correction, though the interval is still mostly positive, so "not significant" isn't "no effect" (HT p.28, study guide).

17. A systems paper runs a t test for each of 8 workloads \(\times\) 3 metrics and highlights the two with p < 0.05, calling the other 22 "comparable". What's wrong?

That's 24 tests. With every null true you'd expect \(24 \times 0.05 = 1.2\) false positives, and at least one with probability \(1 - 0.95^{24} \approx 0.71\). Two hits is about what noise gives, and picking them afterwards is a selection effect (HT p.26). A Bonferroni threshold of \(0.05/24 \approx 0.002\) would likely kill both. Ask whether those metrics were declared primary in advance, and ask for effect sizes and intervals for all 24. "Comparable" for the other 22 is an unsupported equivalence claim (study guide question 18).

18. A membership inference paper reports 62% accuracy. Another reports a 1.2% true-positive rate at a 0.1% false-positive rate. Put both in Type I / Type II language. Which is more alarming?

\(H_0\) is "this example wasn't in the training set". The false-positive rate is \(\alpha\) and the true-positive rate is power. The second paper fixes \(\alpha = 0.001\) and gets power 0.012, so it flags 12 real members per 1,000 for every 1 false alarm, which means it can name specific training examples with confidence. Accuracy averages over everything and can come from a weak diffuse signal. The second is more alarming (HT p.6-7, handout entry 9).

19. A service's mean response time is specified as 100 ms with known \(\sigma = 8\) ms. After a change, 64 requests average 103.1 ms. Test \(H_0: \mu = 100\) two-sided at \(\alpha = 0.05\), with the p-value and the interval.

The standard error is \(8/\sqrt{64} = 8/8 = 1.0\) ms, so \(Z = (103.1 - 100)/1.0 = 3.10\). Since \(3.10 > 1.96\), reject. \(\Pr[Z \ge 3.10] = 0.00097\), so \(p = 2 \times 0.00097 = 0.0019\). The 95% interval is \(103.1 \pm 1.96 = (101.1,\ 105.1)\) ms, which excludes 100 and also tells you the change added roughly 1 to 5 ms, something the verdict alone doesn't (HT p.8, p.13, study guide question 1).

20. A storage benchmark is specified at 12.0 s. Eight runs on a new SSD give \(\bar{x} = 12.9\) s and \(s = 0.9\) s, roughly normal. Does the mean differ from 12.0 s at \(\alpha = 0.05\)? \(t[0.025,\,7] = 2.365\).

\(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n = 8\) is small, so use t with 7 degrees of freedom. The standard error is \(0.9/\sqrt8 = 0.9/2.828 = 0.318\), so \(t = 0.9/0.318 = 2.83\). Since \(2.83 > 2.365\), reject. From the table you can only bracket the p-value, \(0.01 < p < 0.05\) (the exact value is about 0.025). Using 1.96 would give the same verdict here, but that cutoff is 17% too small, so a t near 2.1 would have been wrongly rejected (HT p.11-12, study guide question 3).

21. A new allocator and the baseline run six benchmarks. Baseline 12.1, 8.4, 15.3, 9.8, 20.2, 11.0 s. New 11.5, 8.1, 14.2, 9.6, 19.1, 10.4 s. Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\), and explain why an unpaired t test on the two columns gives \(p \approx 0.79\).

The same benchmarks ran under both, so take differences (new minus baseline) first. They are \(-0.6, -0.3, -1.1, -0.2, -1.1, -0.6\), summing to \(-3.9\), so \(\bar{d} = -0.65\). Deviations from \(-0.65\) are \(0.05, 0.35, -0.45, 0.45, -0.45, 0.05\), and their squares add to 0.735, so \(s_d^2 = 0.735/5 = 0.147\) and \(s_d = 0.383\). The standard error is \(0.383/\sqrt6 = 0.383/2.449 = 0.157\), so \(t = -0.65/0.157 = -4.15\) with 5 degrees of freedom. Since \(4.15 > t[0.025,\,5] = 2.571\), reject (\(p \approx 0.009\)). The new allocator wins on every benchmark. The unpaired test compares the column means 12.80 and 12.15 against column standard deviations of about 4.3 and 4.0 s, which measure how different the benchmarks are from each other. A 0.65 s effect disappears in 4 s of spread, so \(t \approx -0.27\). Pairing removes that spread, and ignoring it throws away almost all the power (study guide question 4).

22. You're comparing 5 fuzzers on 20 targets by coverage after 24 hours. How many trials per fuzzer-target pair, which test per target, how do you handle the number of comparisons, and what do you report besides p?

Trials come from the recipe on (HT p.15) and the formula on (p.19). Run a pilot to estimate the run-to-run \(\sigma\) of coverage, pick the smallest effect worth detecting, compute \(n\) for power 0.80, and report all three so the choice can be checked. Coverage across trials is often skewed or bimodal and \(n\) is modest, so per target a two-sided Mann-Whitney U test is a sensible default (p.11). Five fuzzers give \(5 \times 4/2 = 10\) pairs per target, so 200 tests over 20 targets, and with every null true you'd expect \(200 \times 0.05 = 10\) false positives (p.26). Either correct with Holm or Benjamini-Hochberg (p.28), or use Friedman plus Nemenyi across targets as FuzzBench does (p.30). Report the \(A_{12}\) effect size per pair, medians with intervals and the full distributions (p.18, study guide question 12).

23. To compare two GPU cluster schedulers, a labmate proposes one replay of a 500-job trace where a random half of the jobs use the new policy, then a two-sample t test on the 250 + 250 completion times. What's wrong, what's the unit, and what would you do instead?

Three problems. The halves share the same 16 nodes, so each policy's placements change the other's queueing delays, which is interference, and the test measures the new policy running beside the old one rather than deployed on its own (HT p.22). Jobs in one replay are correlated through shared queues, so 250 per group isn't 250 independent observations and the standard error is too small (p.20-21). And with one replay, run-to-run variation is never measured. The experimental unit is one complete replay of the trace under one scheduler. Instead, run each scheduler on the whole cluster for \(R\) replays, pair them back to back on the same testbed and alternate which goes first, spread the pairs over days, reduce each replay to one summary (mean JCT, plus p99 if the tail matters), and run a paired t test on the per-pair differences. Choose \(R\) in advance from a pilot and a power calculation (p.15, p.19, study guide question 23).

24. Would you control FWER or FDR, and with which procedure, for (a) a headline claim that your scheduler beats each of three published baselines, and (b) screening 200 configuration parameters to pick a few for a later experiment?

(a) FWER with Holm. It's a confirmatory headline claim, so a single false "beats baseline B" is the costly error, and with a family of three the power cost is small. (b) FDR with Benjamini-Hochberg. It's screening, so some false leads are fine because the follow-up experiment weeds them out, and with \(m = 200\) Bonferroni's threshold \(0.05/200 = 0.00025\) would miss most real effects. The follow-up must use fresh data, since retesting the selected parameters on the screening data reuses the same false positives (HT p.28, study guide question 26).

Comparing two alternatives

Slides: Comp (plus CI for the proportion interval), the comparing-alternatives study guide, and the Comparing Two Systems in the Wild handout. Homework: HW6 Problems 1, 2 and 3.

Paired or unpaired, and how to tell

The idea

Every question in this lecture is about a difference. "Is A better than B?" is answered by a confidence interval for A minus B, and the verdict is whether that interval contains 0 (Comp p.3). The interval also tells you how big the difference is, which a yes or no verdict never does.

Before any arithmetic you have to decide the design, because it decides which formula you use and how wide the interval comes out. The design is fixed when the data are collected. You can swap tests afterwards, but you can't turn unpaired data into paired data (Comp p.4).

Comp p.4 lists three things to settle before any test is chosen. The first is the response, such as mean latency, p99, hit ratio or energy per request, picked before measuring because a test on the wrong quantity can't support the claim. The second is the experimental unit, meaning one run, one benchmark or one user, because n counts units and not measurements. The third is the design, whether both systems can be measured on the same unit. The first two carry over unchanged from the hypothesis-testing lecture, and the third is this lecture's subject.

The test for the exam is simple. Ask whether each measurement of A has a natural partner measurement of B taken on the same unit, meaning the same benchmark, workload, trace, input, user or machine. If yes, the data are paired. If the two lists are just two independent samples, often with different lengths, they're unpaired (Comp p.5).

Pairing helps because units differ from each other far more than systems do. Benchmark b4 takes 64 seconds and b3 takes 7, and that spread has nothing to do with A versus B. When you subtract within a pair, the unit's own difficulty cancels, so only the system effect and a little noise are left (Comp p.6).

What to know

The variance of a difference is (Comp p.6)

\[ \mathrm{Var}(A-B) = \sigma_A^2 + \sigma_B^2 - 2\rho\,\sigma_A\sigma_B, \qquad \rho = \frac{\mathrm{Cov}(A,B)}{\sigma_A\sigma_B}. \]

On paired data \(\rho\) is usually close to 1, so the variance of the difference is far smaller than either variance. On unpaired data there is no correspondence, so \(\rho = 0\) and the between-unit spread stays in the standard error. The price of pairing is degrees of freedom, \(n-1\) instead of \(n_A+n_B-2\), and it's worth paying whenever \(\rho > 0\) (Comp p.6).

SituationDesignWhy
Same 9 workloads injected under two routing algorithms (HW6 Problem 1)PairedWorkload \(i\) gives \((A_i, B_i)\)
30 transactions on System A, 24 different ones on System B (HW6 Problem 2)UnpairedDifferent counts, no row of A goes with a row of B
Each user session sees only one schedulerUnpairedThe unit can't be measured twice
Measuring under A changes the unit for B (cache pollution, learning effects)Don't pairThe pair is no longer comparable (Comp p.5)

Two errors from Comp p.5 and p.27 are worth memorizing. A paired analysis of unpaired data is wrong, because it invents a correspondence that doesn't exist. An unpaired analysis of paired data is only wasteful, because it throws away the power you already paid for.

The lecture's demonstration (Comp p.7-8) ran eight benchmarks under both systems. The estimate was +1.83 s either way. Paired, the interval was (+0.65, +3.00) s with p = 0.008. Unpaired Welch on the same 16 numbers gave (−22.0, +25.7) s with p = 0.87, which is 20 times wider and the wrong conclusion.

Worked example

Five benchmarks, response time in seconds, each run on both systems.

Benchmark12345Mean
A12251840920.8
B10211535817.8
\(d = A-B\)243513.0

Same benchmark in each column, so this is paired. To see what pairing buys, compare the spreads. The column variances are

\[ s_A^2 = \frac{(-8.8)^2 + 4.2^2 + (-2.8)^2 + 19.2^2 + (-11.8)^2}{4} = \frac{77.44 + 17.64 + 7.84 + 368.64 + 139.24}{4} = \frac{610.8}{4} = 152.7, \] \[ s_B^2 = \frac{(-7.8)^2 + 3.2^2 + (-2.8)^2 + 17.2^2 + (-9.8)^2}{4} = \frac{60.84 + 10.24 + 7.84 + 295.84 + 96.04}{4} = \frac{470.8}{4} = 117.7. \]

The differences have mean 3 and deviations \(-1, 1, 0, 2, -2\), so

\[ s_d^2 = \frac{1 + 1 + 0 + 4 + 4}{4} = \frac{10}{4} = 2.5. \]

The variance of the differences is 2.5 while each column's variance is over 100. That's \(\rho\) near 1 doing its job. The next topic finishes the paired interval, and the Welch topic shows what the unpaired analysis of these same numbers would give.

Easy to get wrong

  • Equal sample sizes don't make data paired. Pairing comes from a shared unit, not from the counts matching.
  • n counts units, not measurements. Thirty iterations inside one run are one unit, because they share the same process state (Comp p.4).
  • The mean of the differences equals the difference of the means, so the estimate is identical under both designs. Only the standard error changes (Comp p.8).
  • Pairing doesn't create \(\rho\). It lets you use the correlation that's already in the data.
  • "A paired test always has more power than an unpaired one on the same data" is false. If \(\rho \approx 0\) and n is small, losing half the degrees of freedom can make the unpaired analysis slightly stronger (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 19b).
  • Pairing is the two-system version of blocking. The unit is the block, and its own difficulty cancels out (Comp p.6). The ANOVA lectures pick this idea up again.

The paired confidence interval

The idea

Once the data are paired, there's nothing new to learn. You turn the two columns into one column of differences and build the ordinary one-sample t interval on it (Comp p.9). If the interval contains 0, the data don't establish a difference. If it excludes 0, the sign of the interval tells you which system wins.

The interval is the finding, not just the verdict. An interval that straddles 0 but is wide says the experiment was too small to tell, which is different from saying the systems are the same (Comp p.11). The cache example's 90% interval (−0.041, +0.081) is compatible with B being 4 points better and with A being 8 points better, so the slide's reading is "six workloads told us very little", not "the policies are equivalent".

What to know

The five steps on Comp p.9 are to form \(d_i = A_i - B_i\), compute \(\bar d\) and \(s_d\), build the interval, check for 0, and report the difference with its interval.

\[ \bar d \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n-1]}\,\frac{s_d}{\sqrt n}, \qquad t = \frac{\bar d}{s_d/\sqrt n}\ \text{with } n-1 \text{ df}. \]

Here n is the number of pairs, not the number of measurements. The slides use upper-tail notation, so \(t_{[0.025,\,8]} = 2.306\) is the value for a two-sided 95% interval on 8 degrees of freedom, and \(t_{[0.05,\,5]} = 2.015\) is the one for a two-sided 90% interval on 5 (Comp p.11).

The normality assumption is about the differences, not about A or B separately (Comp p.17). A and B can both be skewed as long as the differences are reasonably well behaved.

For speed with a hand calculation, use the shortcut \(s_d^2 = \dfrac{\sum d_i^2 - n\bar d^2}{n-1}\).

Worked example

Small example. Continue the five benchmarks from the last topic, with \(\bar d = 3\) and \(s_d^2 = 2.5\). Build a 95% interval.

\(s_d = \sqrt{2.5} = 1.581\). The standard error is \(1.581/\sqrt5 = 1.581/2.236 = 0.707\). With \(n-1 = 4\) degrees of freedom, \(t_{[0.025,\,4]} = 2.776\), so the half-width is \(2.776 \times 0.707 = 1.963\).

\[ 3 \pm 1.963 = (1.04,\ 4.96)\ \text{s}. \]

Zero is outside, so A is slower than B by somewhere between 1.0 and 5.0 seconds. The test statistic is \(t = 3/0.707 = 4.24\), above 2.776, which agrees.

The lecture's demonstration by hand (Comp p.7-8, study guide questions 2 and 3). The eight differences B − A are 0.8, 2.2, 0.5, 4.2, 1.5, 1.3, 3.6, 0.5. They add to 14.6, so \(\bar d = 14.6/8 = 1.825\) s. The squares add to \(0.64 + 4.84 + 0.25 + 17.64 + 2.25 + 1.69 + 12.96 + 0.25 = 40.52\), so

\[ s_d^2 = \frac{40.52 - 8(1.825)^2}{7} = \frac{40.52 - 26.645}{7} = \frac{13.875}{7} = 1.982, \qquad s_d = 1.408. \]

The standard error is \(1.408/\sqrt8 = 1.408/2.828 = 0.498\), so \(t = 1.825/0.498 = 3.67\) on 7 df, past \(t_{[0.025,\,7]} = 2.365\). The interval is \(1.825 \pm 2.365 \times 0.498 = 1.825 \pm 1.18 = (0.65,\ 3.00)\) s. Said in the response's units, B is slower by 0.65 to 3.0 s on benchmarks averaging about 30 s, so roughly 2% to 10%.

The unpaired analysis of the same numbers uses the row SDs 21.51 and 22.90 instead. Its \(s = \sqrt{(21.51^2 + 22.90^2)/8} = \sqrt{(462.7 + 524.4)/8} = \sqrt{123.4} = 11.1\) s, so \(t = 1.825/11.1 = 0.16\) and the interval is \(1.825 \pm 2.145 \times 11.1 = (-22.0,\ 25.7)\) s. Same estimate, but the between-benchmark spread is now in the denominator.

The lecture's cache example (Comp p.11, study guide question 1). Six workloads, \(\bar d = 0.020\), \(s_d = 0.0743\). The standard error is \(0.0743/\sqrt6 = 0.0743/2.449 = 0.0303\). At 90%, \(0.020 \pm 2.015 \times 0.0303 = 0.020 \pm 0.061 = (-0.041,\ 0.081)\). At 95%, \(0.020 \pm 2.571 \times 0.0303 = 0.020 \pm 0.078 = (-0.058,\ 0.098)\). Both contain 0, and the 95% one is wider, so the verdict doesn't change but the width is the real finding.

HW6 Problem 1. The same workload went through both routing algorithms, so it's paired. Delays and differences \(d_i = A_i - B_i\) are

i123456789
A115.089.3102.194.3117.3120.398.987.6104.5
B125.397.685.3101.3112.787.9104.296.3113.2
d−10.3−8.316.8−7.04.632.4−5.3−8.7−8.7

Running sum of the d values goes −10.3, −18.6, −1.8, −8.8, −4.2, 28.2, 22.9, 14.2, 5.5. So \(\bar d = 5.5/9 = 0.611\).

The squares are 106.09, 68.89, 282.24, 49.00, 21.16, 1049.76, 28.09, 75.69, 75.69, which add to \(\sum d_i^2 = 1756.61\). Then

\[ s_d^2 = \frac{1756.61 - 9(0.611)^2}{8} = \frac{1756.61 - 3.36}{8} = \frac{1753.25}{8} = 219.16, \qquad s_d = 14.804. \]

The standard error is \(14.804/\sqrt9 = 14.804/3 = 4.935\). With \(t_{[0.025,\,8]} = 2.306\) the half-width is \(2.306 \times 4.935 = 11.38\), so

\[ 0.611 \pm 11.38 = (-10.77,\ 11.99). \]

Zero is inside, so you can't say at 95% confidence that A has lower delay. Seven of nine workloads favor A, but workloads 3 and 6 favor B by a lot, and that spread makes the interval wide.

Easy to get wrong

  • Degrees of freedom are pairs minus 1. HW6 Problem 1 has 18 numbers but 8 df, not 16.
  • Write down which way you subtracted. With \(d = A - B\) on a delay, negative means A is better. Flip it and the interval flips sign.
  • "Contains 0" means "not shown", not "equal". The HW6 interval is compatible with A being 10 ms better and with B being 12 ms better.
  • Don't run the paired formula on HW6 Problem 2 just because you could line up the first 24 rows. There's no shared unit.

The unpaired interval with Welch degrees of freedom

The idea

When each system is measured on its own set of units, you can't subtract row by row. Instead you subtract the two sample means and ask how uncertain that difference is. Both means carry their own error, and those errors add in variance, so the standard deviation of the difference combines both samples (Comp p.12).

The catch is the degrees of freedom. With two variances that differ, there's no exact t distribution, so Welch's method computes an effective \(\nu\) from the data. It's usually fractional, and you either use it as is or round down before reading the t table.

What to know

From Comp p.12,

\[ s = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{n_B}}, \qquad (\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,\nu]}\, s, \qquad t = \frac{\bar x_A - \bar x_B}{s}, \] \[ \nu = \frac{\left(\dfrac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \dfrac{s_B^2}{n_B}\right)^2}{\dfrac{(s_A^2/n_A)^2}{n_A-1} + \dfrac{(s_B^2/n_B)^2}{n_B-1}}. \]

Facts about \(\nu\) from Comp p.12. It lies between \(\min(n_A,n_B)-1\) and \(n_A+n_B-2\), closer to the low end when the variances differ. Use it as is or round down, and never just use \(n_A - 1\). Rounding down gives a slightly larger t and so a slightly wider, more conservative interval, which is the safe direction with a printed table.

The hand procedure that avoids mistakes is to compute \(a = s_A^2/n_A\) and \(b = s_B^2/n_B\) once, then \(s = \sqrt{a+b}\) and \(\nu = (a+b)^2 / \big(a^2/(n_A-1) + b^2/(n_B-1)\big)\).

The lecture's example (Comp p.14) has \(n_A = 7\), \(\bar x_A = 0.2657\), \(s_A = 0.0934\) and \(n_B = 9\), \(\bar x_B = 0.4011\), \(s_B = 0.1447\). That gives \(s = 0.0598\), \(\nu = 13.6\), \(t_{[0.05,\,13.6]} = 1.765\) and the 90% interval (−0.241, −0.029). Zero is outside, so B has the higher hit ratio. The Welch statistic is \(t = -0.1354/0.0598 = -2.26\). At 95% the study guide's \(t_{[0.025,\,13.6]} = 2.150\) gives \(-0.135 \pm 0.129 = (-0.264,\ -0.007)\), which still excludes 0 but only just (study guide, answer 4). The size of the effect is 3 to 24 hit-ratio points, a wide range you should say out loud (Comp p.14).

Worked example

Small Welch example. System A on 5 independent runs gives 8, 8, 10, 12, 12 ms. System B on 6 other runs gives 9, 12, 15, 15, 18, 21 ms. Is there a difference at 95%?

Means are \(\bar x_A = 50/5 = 10\) and \(\bar x_B = 90/6 = 15\), so the estimate is \(10 - 15 = -5\).

Variances. A's deviations are −2, −2, 0, 2, 2, so \(s_A^2 = 16/4 = 4\). B's deviations are −6, −3, 0, 0, 3, 6, so \(s_B^2 = (36+9+0+0+9+36)/5 = 90/5 = 18\).

The two pieces are \(a = 4/5 = 0.8\) and \(b = 18/6 = 3.0\). So \(s = \sqrt{0.8 + 3.0} = \sqrt{3.8} = 1.949\).

Effective degrees of freedom, step by step.

\[ \text{numerator} = (0.8 + 3.0)^2 = 3.8^2 = 14.44, \] \[ \text{denominator} = \frac{0.8^2}{4} + \frac{3.0^2}{5} = \frac{0.64}{4} + \frac{9}{5} = 0.16 + 1.80 = 1.96, \] \[ \nu = \frac{14.44}{1.96} = 7.37. \]

Sanity check, \(\nu\) must sit between \(\min(5,6)-1 = 4\) and \(5+6-2 = 9\), and 7.37 does. Round down to 7, so \(t_{[0.025,\,7]} = 2.365\). The half-width is \(2.365 \times 1.949 = 4.61\), and the interval is

\[ -5 \pm 4.61 = (-9.61,\ -0.39)\ \text{ms}. \]

Zero is outside, so A is faster by between about 0.4 and 9.6 ms. Say that the range is wide, which is the honest reading of 11 runs.

The paired benchmarks analyzed the wrong way. Go back to the five benchmarks from the first topic and pretend they were unpaired. Then \(a = 152.7/5 = 30.54\) and \(b = 117.7/5 = 23.54\), so \(s = \sqrt{54.08} = 7.354\). Next, \(\nu = 54.08^2 / \big((30.54^2 + 23.54^2)/4\big) = 2924.6/371.7 = 7.87\), rounded down to 7. The interval is \(3 \pm 2.365 \times 7.354 = 3 \pm 17.39 = (-14.39,\ 20.39)\). The paired interval was (1.04, 4.96). Same data, roughly nine times wider, and the opposite verdict.

HW6 Problem 2. The summary is \(n_A = 30\), \(\bar x_A = 1.8830\), \(s_A = 1.9017\) and \(n_B = 24\), \(\bar x_B = 2.2623\), \(s_B = 2.9108\). Then \(a = 1.9017^2/30 = 0.12055\) and \(b = 2.9108^2/24 = 0.35303\), so \(s = \sqrt{0.47358} = 0.6882\). For \(\nu\), the numerator is \(0.47358^2 = 0.22428\) and the denominator is \(0.12055^2/29 + 0.35303^2/23 = 0.000501 + 0.005419 = 0.005920\), so \(\nu = 0.22428/0.005920 = 37.89\). At 90%, \(t_{[0.05,\,37.89]} \approx 1.686\), giving \(-0.3793 \pm 1.686 \times 0.6882 = -0.3793 \pm 1.160 = (-1.540,\ 0.781)\). Zero is inside, so A isn't shown to be faster at 90%.

Easy to get wrong

  • Squares in the \(\nu\) denominator. It's \((s_A^2/n_A)^2\), the square of a variance-over-n, not \(s_A^2\) again.
  • The denominators inside \(\nu\) are \(n_A - 1\) and \(n_B - 1\), while the ones inside \(s\) are \(n_A\) and \(n_B\). Mixing them up is the most common slip.
  • A 90% two-sided interval uses \(t_{[0.05,\,\nu]}\), not \(t_{[0.10,\,\nu]}\). Half of \(\alpha\) goes in each tail.
  • If your table doesn't have \(\nu = 37\), use the next smaller row. Never jump up to a larger df, since that shrinks t.
  • s here is the standard deviation of the difference of means, already divided by n. Don't divide by \(\sqrt n\) again.

Pooled t or Welch

The idea

The older unpaired test assumes both populations have the same variance and pools the two sample variances into one estimate. That buys a few degrees of freedom. Welch drops the assumption and pays a little in df. The lecture's position is that Welch should be your default, chosen before you see the data, because it loses almost nothing when variances really are equal and protects the error rate when they aren't (Comp p.13).

The real danger is running both and reporting the one that clears 0.05. On the lecture's unpaired example the pooled test gives p = 0.0501 and Welch gives p = 0.0404, which are opposite verdicts at \(\alpha = 0.05\). Picking after the fact is p-hacking (Comp p.13).

What to know

The pooled version, from the study guide's formula sheet, is

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{(n_A-1)s_A^2 + (n_B-1)s_B^2}{n_A+n_B-2}, \qquad (\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t_{[\alpha/2,\,n_A+n_B-2]}\; s_p\sqrt{\frac1{n_A} + \frac1{n_B}}. \]
Pooled tWelch t
Assumes\(\sigma_A^2 = \sigma_B^2\)Nothing about the variances
df\(n_A+n_B-2\)\(\nu\) from the formula
Old rulePool if one sample variance is under about 4 times the other
Current adviceWelch by default, decided in advance (Comp p.13). The source is Delacre, Lakens and Leys (2017), listed in the readings (Comp p.30)

Worked example

Use the Welch data from the last topic (\(n_A = 5\), \(s_A^2 = 4\), \(n_B = 6\), \(s_B^2 = 18\)).

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{4(4) + 5(18)}{9} = \frac{16 + 90}{9} = \frac{106}{9} = 11.78, \qquad s_p = 3.432. \]

Then \(\sqrt{1/5 + 1/6} = \sqrt{11/30} = \sqrt{0.3667} = 0.6055\), so the standard error is \(3.432 \times 0.6055 = 2.078\). With 9 df, \(t_{[0.025,\,9]} = 2.262\) and the half-width is \(2.262 \times 2.078 = 4.70\). The interval is \(-5 \pm 4.70 = (-9.70,\ -0.30)\).

Welch gave (−9.61, −0.39). Here they agree, but B's variance is 4.5 times A's, past the old rule's line, and the small group has the small variance. That makes the pooled test too conservative here (its interval is the wider one). With the variances the other way round, the small group having the big variance, it rejects too often. Either way its error rate isn't what it claims, which is why you commit to Welch up front.

The lecture's example pooled (Comp p.13-14, study guide question 5). With \(n_A = 7\), \(s_A = 0.0934\), \(n_B = 9\), \(s_B = 0.1447\),

\[ s_p^2 = \frac{6(0.0934)^2 + 8(0.1447)^2}{14} = \frac{0.0523 + 0.1675}{14} = \frac{0.2198}{14} = 0.0157, \qquad s_p = 0.1253. \]

Then \(\sqrt{1/7 + 1/9} = \sqrt{16/63} = \sqrt{0.254} = 0.504\), the standard error is \(0.1253 \times 0.504 = 0.0632\), and \(t = -0.1354/0.0632 = -2.14\) on 14 df. Welch gave \(t = -2.26\) on 13.6 df. Those are the p = 0.0501 and p = 0.0404 on the slide. You report Welch because it makes the weaker assumption, and the choice should have been made before either number existed.

Easy to get wrong

  • Pre-testing for equal variances and then choosing is a two-stage procedure with worse error control than always using Welch (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 19c).
  • The pooled weights are \(n-1\), not n, and the divisor is \(n_A+n_B-2\), the same number as the df.
  • Unequal sample sizes are fine for Welch, but ask why one side has fewer runs before trusting anything (Comp p.14).
  • A paper that writes "Student's t test assuming equal variances" with unequal group sizes and spreads is showing a red flag (Wild handout, reference table).

Do the error bars overlap?

The idea

Papers often plot each system's mean with its own interval and let the reader judge by eye. That picture answers a question about two systems, but your claim is about their difference (Comp p.15). The eyeball rule only works in one direction.

If the two intervals don't overlap, the systems differ at that level, and you can say so. If they overlap a little, they may still differ significantly, and you have to do the real test. If each mean sits inside the other's interval, there's no evidence of a difference, and that still isn't evidence they're equal (Comp p.15).

The reason overlap misleads is arithmetic. Two separate intervals have half-widths proportional to \(s_A/\sqrt{n_A}\) and \(s_B/\sqrt{n_B}\). For the bars to overlap the means can be up to the sum of those apart. But the interval on the difference uses \(\sqrt{s_A^2/n_A + s_B^2/n_B}\), which is always smaller than that sum, because the square root of a sum of squares is less than the sum. So the difference can exclude 0 while the bars still touch.

What to know

  • Non-overlap implies significant. Overlap implies nothing (Comp p.15).
  • For 95% intervals, an overlap of up to about half a margin of error can still be significant at 0.05 (Comp p.15).
  • The lecture's example (Comp p.16) has A's 90% interval (0.197, 0.334) and B's (0.311, 0.491). They overlap between 0.311 and 0.334, neither mean is inside the other interval, and the interval on the difference (−0.241, −0.029) excludes 0. Eyeballing says "no difference", the correct analysis says otherwise.
  • An error bar is meaningless unless the caption says what it is. A standard deviation, a standard error and a 95% interval differ by about a factor of \(\sqrt n\) and then another factor of about 2 (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 20). SD describes the spread of the data, SE and CI describe how precisely the mean is known.
  • The fix is to plot the difference and its interval. One picture, the right question (Comp p.16).

Worked example

Take the small Welch data (A = 8, 8, 10, 12, 12 and B = 9, 12, 15, 15, 18, 21) and draw individual 95% intervals.

A has \(s_A = 2\), so its standard error is \(2/\sqrt5 = 0.894\). With \(t_{[0.025,\,4]} = 2.776\), the half-width is \(2.776 \times 0.894 = 2.48\) and the interval is (7.52, 12.48).

B has \(s_B = \sqrt{18} = 4.243\), so its standard error is \(4.243/\sqrt6 = 1.732\). With \(t_{[0.025,\,5]} = 2.571\), the half-width is \(2.571 \times 1.732 = 4.45\) and the interval is (10.55, 19.45).

The bars overlap from 10.55 to 12.48. A's mean 10 isn't inside B's interval and B's mean 15 isn't inside A's, so this is case 2, "must do the test". The Welch interval on the difference was (−9.61, −0.39), which excludes 0. So the systems do differ, even though the bars overlap.

Notice why. The two standard errors add to \(0.894 + 1.732 = 2.626\), but the standard error of the difference is \(\sqrt{0.894^2 + 1.732^2} = \sqrt{0.8 + 3.0} = 1.949\), which is noticeably smaller.

Easy to get wrong

  • "The error bars overlap, so the difference isn't significant" is false. This is the exact sentence the study guide asks you to criticize.
  • Heavy overlap means "no evidence", not "the systems are the same".
  • If the data are paired, separate intervals per system are even more misleading, since they include the between-unit spread that pairing would cancel.

Comparing two proportions

The idea

Sometimes the response is a yes or no per unit, such as a cache hit, a passed test question or a crash. Then each system has a proportion, and you want an interval on the difference of proportions. The Comp slides don't have a slide on this, so treat it as lower priority. It's built from the single-proportion interval in the CI deck (CI p.47-48) and the textbook's two-alternatives chapter (Lilja Ch. 5), and the comparing-alternatives study guide uses it in practice question 16.

The same paired-or-unpaired question applies. If both systems answered the same 400 questions, it's paired, and you can score each question as +1, 0 or −1 and run the paired interval on those numbers.

What to know

Single proportion, from CI p.48, with \(p\) the sample proportion and n large enough for the normal approximation,

\[ p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}. \]

The CI deck writes \(z_{1-\alpha/2}\) as a quantile, while Comp writes \(z[\alpha/2]\) as an upper-tail value. They're the same number, 1.960 for 95% and 1.645 for 90%.

Two independent proportions (unpaired). Variances add, just like Welch,

\[ (p_1 - p_2) \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p_1(1-p_1)}{n_1} + \frac{p_2(1-p_2)}{n_2}}. \]

Paired proportions. Score each unit \(d_i = +1\) if only A succeeded, \(-1\) if only B did, and 0 if they agreed. Then use \(\bar d \pm z\,s_d/\sqrt n\). Only the disagreeing units carry information, which is the idea behind McNemar's test (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 16).

Worked example

Unpaired. Model A gets 87% and model B gets 89% of 400 questions each, treated as independent samples. The two variance pieces are \(0.87 \times 0.13/400 = 0.1131/400 = 0.000283\) and \(0.89 \times 0.11/400 = 0.0979/400 = 0.000245\). Their sum is 0.000528 and its square root is 0.0230. At 95% the half-width is \(1.96 \times 0.0230 = 0.045\), so the interval is \(-0.02 \pm 0.045 = (-0.065,\ 0.025)\). Not significant.

Paired, same data. Suppose both models got 344 questions right, only A got 4, only B got 12, and 40 were missed by both. Check, A has \(344 + 4 = 348 = 87\%\) and B has \(344 + 12 = 356 = 89\%\). The d values are four +1s, twelve −1s and 384 zeros. So \(\bar d = (4 - 12)/400 = -0.02\) and \(\sum d_i^2 = 16\).

\[ s_d^2 = \frac{16 - 400(0.02)^2}{399} = \frac{16 - 0.16}{399} = \frac{15.84}{399} = 0.0397, \qquad s_d = 0.199. \]

The standard error is \(0.199/\sqrt{400} = 0.199/20 = 0.00996\), the half-width is \(1.96 \times 0.00996 = 0.0195\), and the interval is \(-0.02 \pm 0.0195 = (-0.0395,\ -0.0005)\). It just excludes 0. Pairing cut the half-width from 0.045 to 0.020, because the models agree on most questions.

Easy to get wrong

  • Use z, not t, for proportions. The variance comes from the binomial formula, not from a sample standard deviation.
  • The normal approximation needs enough successes and failures in each group. With a handful of events, don't trust it.
  • Two leaderboard percentages from the same test set are paired. Comparing them as independent throws away the agreement.

Rank-based tests when normality fails

The idea

Runtimes and latencies are skewed and have outliers, and t intervals assume roughly normal data (the differences, for paired designs). The lecture gives four ways out, which are to analyze \(\log x\), use a rank-based test, bootstrap an interval, or compare the tail statistic directly if the claim is about p99 (Comp p.18). All of these must be chosen before you look at the results.

Rank tests replace values with their ranks, so one huge observation counts as "the largest" and nothing more. Each design has its own rank test. The decision chart on Comp p.17 is the one to memorize.

What to know

Design and dataUse
Paired, differences roughly normalPaired t on the differences
Paired, outliers but symmetricWilcoxon signed-rank
Paired, skewed differencesSign test or bootstrap
Unpaired, roughly normalWelch
Unpaired, skewed or ordinalMann-Whitney U, or bootstrap

Source is Comp p.17.

Mann-Whitney U (unpaired, also called Wilcoxon rank-sum, Comp p.19). Rank all \(n_1 + n_2\) values together, ties get the average of their ranks. With \(W_1\) the rank sum of sample 1,

\[ U_1 = W_1 - \frac{n_1(n_1+1)}{2}, \quad \mu_U = \frac{n_1 n_2}{2}, \quad \sigma_U^2 = \frac{n_1 n_2 (n_1+n_2+1)}{12}, \quad Z = \frac{U_1 - \mu_U}{\sigma_U}. \]

The normal approximation is fine once both samples exceed about 8. The null is that the populations are identical, \(P(X > Y) = 1/2\), not that the medians are equal. Report \(A_{12} = U_1/(n_1 n_2)\), the probability a random value from sample 1 beats one from sample 2.

Wilcoxon signed-rank (paired, Comp p.21). Drop zero differences, rank \(|d_i|\) from smallest, and sum the ranks of the positive and the negative ones. Then \(W^+ + W^- = n(n+1)/2\) and \(W = \min(W^+, W^-)\). For small n compute the exact p from all \(2^n\) sign patterns. Above about 20 pairs use \(\mu = n(n+1)/4\), \(\sigma^2 = n(n+1)(2n+1)/24\). Effect size is \(r = (W^+ - W^-)/(n(n+1)/2)\). It's robust to outliers but assumes symmetric differences, so it isn't safe under skew.

On the cache data the normal approximation gives \(\mu = 6(7)/4 = 10.5\), \(\sigma^2 = 6(7)(13)/24 = 22.75\), \(\sigma = 4.77\), and \(Z = (6 - 10.5)/4.77 = -0.94\) with p = 0.35. The exact p is 0.44 (14 of the 64 sign patterns give \(W^- \le 6\), so \(2 \times 14/64\)). The approximation is too rough at n = 6, so report the exact p (Comp p.22). The slide also gives the Hodges-Lehmann estimate of the shift, +0.02, as the rank test's companion to \(\bar d\). At n = 6 the smallest possible two-sided p is \(2/64 = 0.031\) (study guide, answer 8).

Sign test (paired, Comp p.23). Count the positive differences S among the n nonzero ones. Under the null \(S \sim \text{Binomial}(n, 1/2)\), and for large n, \(z = (S - n/2)/(\sqrt n/2)\). No symmetry assumption, but it's the least powerful because it ignores sizes. On the cache data S = 3 of 6, the most likely outcome under the null, so p = 1.00. That's far above the signed-rank's 0.44 because the three positive differences happen to be the three largest, which only the signed-rank sees (study guide, answer 9).

Transform before you rank (Comp p.18). Runtime skew is usually multiplicative, so take \(\log(A_i/B_i)\) per unit. Logs turn ratios into differences and are often close to symmetric, so a paired t (or signed-rank) on them is fine, and the result back-transforms into a geometric-mean speedup with an interval (study guide, answer 18). The slide also notes that t tests are fairly robust with equal-sized samples. The deeper question is whether the mean is the quantity your claim is about. If the claim is about p99, compare p99s (Comp p.18, p.27).

Software (Comp p.10). scipy's ttest_rel is the paired t, ttest_ind with equal_var=False is Welch, wilcoxon is signed-rank and mannwhitneyu is rank-sum. None of them returns a confidence interval by default, so you compute it or use stats.bootstrap, which gives an interval for any statistic, including a difference of medians or of p99s. In R, t.test(a, b, paired=TRUE) and wilcox.test() run the same tests and do print the interval. The slide's point is that you need the arithmetic to read the output, not to do it by hand in practice.

Readings (Comp p.30) are Jain Ch. 13 on paired and unpaired comparisons, Lilja Ch. 5 on comparing two alternatives, Delacre, Lakens and Leys (2017) on Welch by default, Cumming and Finch (2005) on reading pictures of data, and the scipy.stats docs. The slide's closing note is that Mann-Whitney U and the Wilcoxon rank-sum test are the same test, while the signed-rank test is a different one.

With 5 or fewer pairs, neither the signed-rank nor the sign test can reach p < 0.05 two-sided, since the smallest possible p is \(2/32 = 0.0625\) (Comp p.22-23).

Worked example

Mann-Whitney by hand. A gives 3.1, 4.5, 2.8, 6.0 and B gives 5.2, 7.1, 4.5, 8.3, 6.6. Sort all nine.

Value2.83.14.54.55.26.06.67.18.3
SampleAAABBABBB
Rank123.53.556789

The tied 4.5s share ranks 3 and 4, so each gets 3.5. Then \(W_A = 1 + 2 + 3.5 + 6 = 12.5\) and \(U_A = 12.5 - 4(5)/2 = 12.5 - 10 = 2.5\). Check by counting pairs where A beats B. 6.0 beats 5.2 and 4.5 for 2, and 4.5 ties 4.5 for 0.5, giving 2.5. So \(A_{12} = 2.5/20 = 0.125\), meaning a random A value beats a random B value only 12.5% of the time. With samples this small, don't use the Z approximation.

Mann-Whitney with Z (the lecture's crash data, Comp p.20). \(n_1 = 15\), \(n_2 = 14\), \(W_1 = 162\). Then \(U_1 = 162 - 15(16)/2 = 162 - 120 = 42\), \(\mu_U = 15(14)/2 = 105\), \(\sigma_U^2 = 15(14)(30)/12 = 6300/12 = 525\), \(\sigma_U = 22.91\), and \(Z = (42 - 105)/22.91 = -2.75\). That's beyond \(-2.576\), so reject at \(\alpha = 0.01\). \(A_{12} = 42/210 = 0.20\), so System II runs longer between crashes four times out of five. The medians, 0.43 against 0.885 weeks, say the same thing in the response's units, about a factor of two. Put both in the paper, because the p-value only says "different" (Comp p.20).

Signed-rank and sign test on the five benchmarks. The differences 2, 4, 3, 5, 1 are all positive. Ranks of \(|d|\) are 2, 4, 3, 5, 1, so \(W^+ = 15\), \(W^- = 0\), and \(W = 0\). Only one of the 32 sign patterns gives \(W^- = 0\), so the exact two-sided p is \(2 \times 1/32 = 0.0625\). The sign test has S = 5 of 5 and the same p of 0.0625. Neither rejects at 0.05, even though the paired t gave t = 4.24 with 4 df, well past 2.776. With five pairs, rank tests can't detect anything at 0.05.
