Signed-rank on the lecture's cache data (Comp p.22). The six differences sorted by size are −0.03, −0.05, −0.06, +0.07, +0.09, +0.10, with ranks 1 to 6. So \(W^+ = 4+5+6 = 15\), \(W^- = 1+2+3 = 6\), and they add to \(21 = 6(7)/2\). Effect size \(r = (15-6)/21 = 0.43\). The exact two-sided p is 0.44, agreeing with the paired t's p = 0.54.

Easy to get wrong

  • Signed-rank is the paired test. Rank-sum (Mann-Whitney) is the unpaired one (Comp p.21, p.30).
  • Mann-Whitney doesn't test medians. It tests whether one distribution tends to produce larger values.
  • Signed-rank still assumes symmetry. For skewed differences use the sign test or a bootstrap.
  • Choosing the rank test because the t test missed 0.05 is p-hacking (Comp p.18).
  • HW6 Problem 2's CPU times are visibly skewed (values like 9.59 and 11.73 among mostly 0 to 4). By the Comp p.17 chart that's a Mann-Whitney or log-transform case, though the homework asked for the t interval.

Effect size, how many runs, and what to report

The idea

A p-value says whether a difference is distinguishable from noise, not whether it's big enough to matter. So report the raw difference with its interval in the response's units, which is usually the most useful statement (Comp p.24).

The number of runs should be planned before collecting data, from the smallest difference you'd care about. Pairing changes that number a lot, because the paired formula uses the small \(s_d\) and the unpaired one uses the large raw s (Comp p.25).

What to know

Effect sizes from Comp p.24 are the raw difference with its interval (+1.83 s, or −0.135 hit ratio), a ratio or speedup (B is \(31.61/29.79 = 1.06\times\) slower), a standardized \(d_z = \bar d/s_d\) for paired data or Cohen's \(d = (\bar x_A - \bar x_B)/s_p\) for unpaired, and the probability of superiority \(A_{12}\), where 0.5 means no difference. The standardized ones are for combining across studies. \(A_{12}\) needs no distributional assumption and, unlike a p-value, doesn't grow just because you added runs (Wild handout, entry 5).

Sample size from Comp p.25, with \(\delta\) the difference worth detecting and \(z[\beta] = 0.842\) for 80% power,

\[ \text{paired: } n = \left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s_d}{\delta}\right)^2, \qquad \text{unpaired: } n \text{ per group} = 2\left(\frac{(z[\alpha/2] + z[\beta])\, s}{\delta}\right)^2. \]

Always round up. A post-hoc power calculation from your own p-value tells you nothing (Comp p.25).

Report the design (paired or not, what the unit was, how many), the test and why it was chosen in advance, the statistic with df and an exact p, the difference with its interval, and every comparison including non-significant ones (Comp p.26). If you measured three systems and report only one pair, say so, because the error rate isn't what the reader assumes (Comp p.26).

With c alternatives there are \(c(c-1)/2\) pairs. At c = 4 that's six tests and \(1 - 0.95^6 \approx 0.26\) chance of at least one false positive, which is why three or more systems need ANOVA first (Comp p.28). In general it's \(1 - (1-\alpha)^{c(c-1)/2}\) (study guide formula sheet). Only if the omnibus test rejects do you look for which pairs differ, with Tukey for all pairs, Dunnett against a baseline, or a correction. Friedman is the rank-based omnibus test when the data aren't normal (Comp p.28, study guide answer 25).

If you ran several tests on one comparison, report all of them and explain any disagreement. Quietly discussing only the one under 0.05 is the p-hacking the lecture warns about (study guide, answer 24).

Worked example

Lecture numbers (Comp p.25). With \(s_d = 0.0743\), \(\delta = 0.05\), \(\alpha = 0.05\) and 80% power, \(z[0.025] + z[0.2] = 1.960 + 0.842 = 2.802\). Then \(n = (2.802 \times 0.0743/0.05)^2 = (2.802 \times 1.486)^2 = 4.164^2 = 17.3\), rounded up to 18 pairs. Unpaired with \(s = 0.12\), \(n = 2(2.802 \times 0.12/0.05)^2 = 2(6.725)^2 = 2(45.2) = 90.4\), so 91 per group. Pairing cut the work by about a factor of five.

Five benchmarks again. \(s_d = 1.581\). To detect a 1-second difference at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with 80% power, \(n = (2.802 \times 1.581/1)^2 = 4.430^2 = 19.6\), so 20 benchmarks.

Effect size for the small Welch example. The raw difference is −5 ms, 95% CI (−9.61, −0.39). Relative to B's mean of 15 ms that's about a third faster, with a range from about 3% to 64%. Report that, not just "significant".

Easy to get wrong

  • The unpaired formula has the factor 2 and uses the raw s, not \(s_d\).
  • \(z[\beta]\) for 80% power is 0.842, the upper 20% point. It's not 1.282 (that's 90% power).
  • "We ran it six times" isn't a sample size, it's the absence of one (Comp p.25). The six-workload example had about a one-in-five chance of detecting a 0.05 difference.
  • Not significant with tiny n is not "equivalent" (Comp p.27).

Building pairing into the experiment, and papers in the wild

The idea

The study guide's design questions all ask the same thing. Since the design can't be fixed afterwards, how do you get pairing, or something close to it, before you collect data? The answer is to make the two measurements share as much of their noise as possible (Comp p.5-6). The Wild handout is a set of real papers that do this well or badly, and it's the source for HW6 Problem 3.

What to know

SituationHow to pair or recover \(\rho\)Source
Cloud VMs whose speed driftsIn space, run A and B at the same time on the same machine (duet benchmarking). In time, interleave one run of A and one of B per round with the order randomized each round (RMIT)Study guide answer 15, Wild entries 1 and 2
Production schedulers, each session sees oneUnpaired, but stratify or block on request rate, region, device or time of day, use each user's pre-period behaviour as a covariate, or switch the whole system between A and B in alternating windowsStudy guide answer 12
Two LLMs on the same 400 questionsThe unit is the question, so it's paired by construction. You need per-question outcomes from the harness, not the two summary scoresStudy guide answer 16, Wild entry 3
p99 latency on 20 shared workloadsKeep the pairing, but make the response the per-workload p99, then pick paired t, signed-rank or sign test from the shape of the 20 differencesStudy guide answer 14
Iterations inside one executionThey don't add units. Spend runs at the level with the most variance, and pair at that levelWild entry 11, Comp p.4

With 20 runs to spend, 10 paired units almost always beat 10 + 10 unpaired. The answer flips when \(\rho \le 0\), meaning the units share nothing, or when measuring under A changes the unit for B (cache pollution, learning effects, leftover state). Then pairing buys no variance and adds carry-over bias (study guide, answer 17).

Papers from the Wild handout worth one line each.

PaperWhat it shows
Bulej et al., Duet Benchmarking (ICPE 2020)Running both variants together manufactures \(\rho\). Accuracy improved 2.3× to 12.5× on ScalaBench and DaCapo and 23.8× to 82.4× on SPEC CPU 2017. It fails if the two variants interfere, since they share the cores
Abedi and Brecht, RMIT (ICPE 2017)Consecutive batches of A then B reported up to 37.8% differences between identical alternatives. Interleaving pairs in time
Miller, Adding Error Bars to Evals (2024)The paired eval uses \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B)\) with the question as the unit, and the covariance shrinks it
Dror et al. (ACL 2018)A test-choice decision tree for NLP, with the paired bootstrap as the usual default. F1 isn't a mean, so a t test on it is hard to justify
Arcuri and Briand (ICSE 2011)Randomized algorithms need many runs, a rank test and \(A_{12}\)
FuzzBench (FSE 2021)Mann-Whitney plus \(A_{12}\) per benchmark, Friedman with Nemenyi across benchmarks, which is a blocked design
Bouthillier et al. (MLSys 2021)Compare by \(P(A > B)\), which is \(A_{12}\), with a threshold of 0.75 fixed in advance. In their simulations comparing average scores had a false-negative rate near 90%, while their test kept false positives near 5% and false negatives around 30%. They recommend 29 training runs
Georges et al. (OOPSLA 2007)The JVM protocol that compares by interval overlap, the Comp p.15 pitfall in a widely cited paper
Belia et al. (2005), Cumming and Finch (2005)Published researchers in psychology, behavioural neuroscience and medicine, asked to place two error bars so the difference was just significant, mostly couldn't, for both CI and SE bars, and often confused the two. Cumming and Finch is the source of the half-margin overlap rule
Card et al. (EMNLP 2020)Asks the power question backwards, finding the smallest effect a published n could detect
Kalibera and Jones (ISMM 2013)Repetition hierarchy of builds, executions and iterations. Repeats at the wrong level buy nothing

The handout's seven pitfalls are unpaired analysis of paired data, paired analysis of unpaired data, "not significant" read as "equivalent", two intervals instead of one, pooled t on unequal groups, the test chosen after the data, and a mean compared when the claim is about the tail. They match Comp p.27 plus the pooled-t item from p.13.

Worked example

Study guide question 17 with numbers. Suppose each system's runtime has \(\sigma_A = \sigma_B = 20\) s across units and you have 20 runs.

Strong correlation, \(\rho = 0.99\). \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B) = 400 + 400 - 2(0.99)(20)(20) = 800 - 792 = 8\), so \(\sigma_d = 2.83\). Paired with 10 units, the standard error is \(2.83/\sqrt{10} = 0.894\) and the half-width is \(t_{[0.025,\,9]} \times 0.894 = 2.262 \times 0.894 = 2.02\) s. Unpaired 10 + 10, the standard error is \(\sqrt{400/10 + 400/10} = \sqrt{80} = 8.94\) and the half-width is \(t_{[0.025,\,18]} \times 8.94 = 2.101 \times 8.94 = 18.8\) s. Pairing is about nine times narrower.

No correlation, \(\rho = 0\). Now \(\mathrm{Var}(A-B) = 800\), \(\sigma_d = 28.3\), and the paired standard error is \(28.3/\sqrt{10} = 8.94\), the same as unpaired. But the paired t uses 9 df, so its half-width is \(2.262 \times 8.94 = 20.2\) s against 18.8 s unpaired. That's why "paired always wins" is false (study guide, answer 19b).

Easy to get wrong

  • The Wild handout cites slide numbers from an older version of the deck (it puts effect size on slide 22 and how many runs on 23). In the posted Comp PDF those are p.24 and p.25. Cite the deck pages.
  • Interleaving and duet runs don't fix an instance that's slow for the whole experiment. You still want several instances (study guide, answer 15).
  • A referee saying "only eight benchmarks" is usually worried about generality, not the statistics. Answer with the interval, the consistency across units, and a limitations sentence (study guide, answer 21).

Check yourself

1. A labmate runs compiler A on benchmarks 1 to 8 and compiler B on benchmarks 9 to 15 "to save time". What's wrong, and what should they do?

That turns a naturally paired comparison into an unpaired one and confounds the compiler with the benchmarks, since benchmarks differ far more than compilers do. Run both compilers on all 15 benchmarks and analyze the 15 differences with the paired interval. Pairing removes the between-benchmark spread from the standard error (Comp p.5-6).

2. Paired differences are 1, −1, 3, 2, 0. Build the 90% and 95% intervals for the mean difference.

\(\bar d = 5/5 = 1\). Deviations are 0, −2, 2, 1, −1, so \(s_d^2 = (0+4+4+1+1)/4 = 2.5\) and \(s_d = 1.581\). The standard error is \(1.581/\sqrt5 = 0.707\). At 90%, \(t_{[0.05,\,4]} = 2.132\) and the interval is \(1 \pm 1.508 = (-0.51,\ 2.51)\). At 95%, \(t_{[0.025,\,4]} = 2.776\) and it's \(1 \pm 1.963 = (-0.96,\ 2.96)\). Both contain 0, so no difference is established (Comp p.9).

3. Unpaired. System A has \(n_A = 10\), mean 20, \(s_A = 3\). System B has \(n_B = 8\), mean 16, \(s_B = 4\). Find s, the Welch \(\nu\) and the 95% interval.

\(a = 9/10 = 0.9\) and \(b = 16/8 = 2.0\), so \(s = \sqrt{2.9} = 1.703\). \(\nu = 2.9^2/(0.9^2/9 + 2.0^2/7) = 8.41/(0.09 + 0.571) = 8.41/0.661 = 12.7\), rounded down to 12. \(t_{[0.025,\,12]} = 2.179\), so the half-width is \(2.179 \times 1.703 = 3.71\) and the interval is \(4 \pm 3.71 = (0.29,\ 7.71)\). Zero is outside, so A's mean is higher (Comp p.12). Check that 12.7 sits between 7 and 16.

4. True or false. If two 95% confidence intervals overlap, the difference isn't significant at 0.05.

False. Non-overlap implies significance, but overlap implies nothing. Intervals can overlap by up to about half a margin of error and the difference can still be significant, because the standard error of the difference is smaller than the sum of the two standard errors (Comp p.15). The lecture's example has overlapping 90% intervals but a difference interval of (−0.241, −0.029) (Comp p.16).

5. A paper says "our latency was 42 ms, the baseline's was 45 ms, and since the error bars overlap the difference isn't significant." Name two problems.

First, overlap of two separate intervals isn't a test of the difference, so they should have built one interval on the 3 ms difference, paired if the workloads were shared and Welch otherwise (Comp p.15-16). Second, the caption doesn't say whether the bars are SD, SE or a confidence interval, and those differ by factors of about \(\sqrt n\) and 2, so the figure can't be read at all (comparing-alternatives study guide, answer 20).

6. Why should you pick Welch over the pooled t before seeing the data, and what's the pooled df?

Pooled uses \(n_A + n_B - 2\) df and assumes equal variances. Welch assumes nothing and is barely less powerful when variances are equal. On the lecture's example pooled gave p = 0.0501 and Welch p = 0.0404, opposite verdicts, so choosing afterwards is p-hacking (Comp p.13).

7. Mann-Whitney with \(n_1 = n_2 = 10\) and \(W_1 = 70\). Find \(U_1\), Z, the verdict at \(\alpha = 0.01\), and \(A_{12}\).

\(U_1 = 70 - 10(11)/2 = 70 - 55 = 15\). \(\mu_U = 100/2 = 50\). \(\sigma_U^2 = 10(10)(21)/12 = 2100/12 = 175\), so \(\sigma_U = 13.23\). \(Z = (15 - 50)/13.23 = -2.65\), beyond \(-2.576\), so reject at 0.01. \(A_{12} = 15/100 = 0.15\), meaning a random sample-1 value beats a random sample-2 value 15% of the time (Comp p.19).

8. Five paired workloads all favor B. Can a Wilcoxon signed-rank test reject at 0.05 two-sided?

No. The most extreme pattern, all five the same sign, has probability 1/32 in each tail, so the smallest two-sided p is \(2/32 = 0.0625\). With five or fewer pairs no outcome reaches 0.05, and the same holds for the sign test (Comp p.22-23).

9. Pilot data give \(s_d = 2\) for paired differences and a raw s = 6. How many runs are needed to detect \(\delta = 1\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with 80% power, paired and unpaired?

\(z[0.025] + z[0.2] = 1.960 + 0.842 = 2.802\). Paired, \(n = (2.802 \times 2/1)^2 = 5.604^2 = 31.4\), so 32 pairs. Unpaired, \(n = 2(2.802 \times 6/1)^2 = 2(16.81)^2 = 2(282.6) = 565.3\), so 566 per group (Comp p.25).

10. Failure rate is 80 of 400 runs (0.20) for A and 120 of 400 runs (0.30) for B, on independent runs. Give a 95% interval for \(p_A - p_B\).

Variance pieces are \(0.20(0.80)/400 = 0.16/400\) and \(0.30(0.70)/400 = 0.21/400\), summing to \(0.37/400 = 0.000925\). The square root is 0.0304 and the half-width is \(1.96 \times 0.0304 = 0.060\). The interval is \(-0.10 \pm 0.060 = (-0.160,\ -0.040)\), so A fails less often. This builds on the proportion interval in CI p.48, which the Comp deck doesn't cover itself.

11. HW6 Problem 1 asks if A is better at 95%. The solution used a two-sided interval (−10.77, 11.99). Would a one-sided bound change the answer?

"A better" means lower delay, so \(d = A - B < 0\). A one-sided 95% upper bound is \(\bar d + t_{[0.05,\,8]} \times 4.935 = 0.611 + 1.860 \times 4.935 = 0.611 + 9.18 = 9.79\). To claim A is better the upper bound would have to be below 0, and it isn't, so the verdict is the same. Comp works with two-sided intervals throughout (Comp p.9).

12. A student runs 30 iterations inside one run of each system, does a paired t test on the iterations, and gets p < 0.001. What's wrong?

The iterations share one process, one cache state and one machine condition, so they aren't independent units. The effective n is one run per system and the standard error is far too small. Make the run the unit, do many independent runs, summarize each by one number, and pair across runs if the same workload is used for both (Comp p.4).

13. Ten paired workloads give 9 positive differences and 1 negative. Run the sign test at \(\alpha = 0.05\) two-sided.

Under the null \(S \sim \text{Binomial}(10, 1/2)\). The chance of 9 or 10 positives is \((10 + 1)/2^{10} = 11/1024\), and doubling for two sides gives \(22/1024 = 0.021\). That's below 0.05, so reject. The sign test ignores the sizes of the differences, which is why it needs a lopsided split to reject (Comp p.23).

14. On the six-workload cache data, the paired t gives p = 0.54, signed-rank 0.44 and the sign test 1.00. Why do they differ, and what's the conclusion?

S = 3 of 6 is the most likely outcome under the null, so the sign test gives 1.00. The signed-rank also uses sizes, and the three positive differences are the three largest, so \(W^+ = 15\) of 21 pulls its p down to 0.44. All three agree that six workloads can't settle it, which is the honest conclusion (Comp p.22-23, study guide answer 9).

15. You must compare two systems on cloud VMs whose performance drifts during the day. Give one way to pair in space and one in time.

In space, run both systems at the same time on the same machine (duet benchmarking), so a noisy neighbour hits both. In time, interleave runs of A and B in rounds with the order randomized each round (RMIT), so drift hits both equally and each round is a pair. Running all of A and then all of B is unpaired in time, and that design reported up to 37.8% differences between identical systems (study guide answer 15, Wild handout entries 1 and 2).

16. Your pilot shows paired runtime differences with a long right tail. Which analysis do you plan, and why not the signed-rank test?

Signed-rank assumes the differences are symmetric about 0, so skew alone can make it reject. Runtime skew is usually multiplicative, so analyze \(\log(A_i/B_i)\), which is often near symmetric, with a paired t, and back-transform to a geometric-mean speedup. If the logs are still skewed, use the sign test or a bootstrap of the median difference. Decide this from the pilot, before the real data (Comp p.17-18, study guide answer 18).

Measurement

Slides: Meas (all 31 pages, lectured 10/19), the error and proportion slides in CI, and the tools agenda in Intro. Handouts: the measurement study guide and the "Measurement in the Wild" companion handout, which Setia replaced on 10/20 with a 7-page version that adds entries 12 to 17 for areas outside systems and asked everyone to read the entries for their own area. Homework: none yet (HW7 comes after the midterm), so the check questions follow the measurement study guide.

Events and the three ways to observe them

The idea

Every measurement tool watches for events. An event is any change in system state you decide to care about, such as a cache miss, a disk access, a packet, an interrupt or a request span (Meas p.6). The system doesn't hand you events. You pick them, and that choice shapes everything after it.

Once you've picked the event, three questions settle the design. How often does it happen? How much context do you need each time it happens? How much disturbance can you live with? The answers point you to counting, tracing or sampling (Meas p.6-8). Most bad setups come from grabbing a familiar tool instead of answering these three questions first.

When you can't observe the quantity at all, there's a fourth route called indirect measurement. You measure something you can see and derive the thing you can't. It gets its own topic below.

What to know

Counting (event-driven)TracingSampling
What it doesIncrement a counter on each eventRecord each event plus system stateLook at the state at fixed time intervals
ResultExact countComplete, ordered recordStatistical estimate
Overhead grows withEvent rateEvent rateSampling rate
ContextNone, how many but never whyAll of itWhatever the snapshot holds
Rare eventsCountedCapturedLikely missed
Fails whenEvents are very frequentOverhead or storage runs outYou need exact numbers

The one sentence to keep is that counting and tracing scale with the workload while sampling scales with your sampling rate (Meas p.7). That's why sampling is the only strategy whose cost you can fix before you run. The slide's summary is "exactness, context, overhead, you get two" (Meas p.8).

Modern tools are the same three ideas (Meas p.9). perf stat and hardware performance counters count. ftrace, eBPF and OpenTelemetry spans trace. perf record, async-profiler, py-spy and flame graphs sample. Name which of the three a tool is and its cost model follows.

A sampling profiler reports a proportion, the share of samples that landed in a function. That share has a standard error, so the proportion interval from the CI lecture applies unchanged (CI p.48).

\[ p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}}, \qquad n = \text{sampling rate} \times \text{run length} \]

To get a half-width of \(e\) in absolute terms you need \(n = z_{1-\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)/e^2\) samples, and the run length is \(n\) divided by the sampling rate (measurement study guide, Part B). The CI slides write the same formula with a relative error, \(n = z_{1-\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)/(e p)^2\) (CI p.54), and their worked case is the "how much time is spent in the OS" sampler (CI p.52-57). Check which kind of \(e\) a question gives you before you plug in.

Notation. The study guide calls this Lecture 6's Wald interval and writes the critical value as \(z_{\alpha/2}\), listing \(z_{0.025} = 1.960\) for 95%, \(z_{0.05} = 1.645\) for 90% and \(z_{0.005} = 2.576\) for 99% (measurement study guide, Part C). The CI slide defines its \(z\) as the \(1-\alpha/2\) quantile (CI p.48). Both are the same number, 1.96 at 95%, just named from opposite tails.

The CI slides' own case (CI p.52-57). Interrupt every 10 ms for one minute, so \(n = 6000\), and \(m = 658\) interrupts land in the OS. That's \(p = 0.1097\) and a 95% interval of 10.2% to 11.8%. To get \(\pm 0.5\%\) relative error the slides need about 1.25 million interrupts, which at 10 ms each is 3.46 hours. The same target read as 0.5 percentage points absolute needs only about 15,000 interrupts, or 2.5 minutes. That gap is why you must check which \(e\) you were given.

Raising the rate is not free (measurement study guide, question 2). Sampling ten times faster collects samples ten times faster, but sampling overhead is set by the rate, so you've multiplied perturbation by ten, which is exactly what sampling was chosen to control (Meas p.7). Samples taken very close together inside the same function call are also correlated, so ten of them aren't ten independent observations and the \(n\) in the formula overstates what you have.

Half-width scales as \(1/\sqrt{n}\) (measurement study guide, Part B). Four times the samples halves the interval, and a hundred times the samples cuts it to a tenth.

Worked example

You profile a 20 second run at 500 Hz, and function f gets 800 samples. What's the 95% interval for the share of time in f, and how long would you need to run to get it to within 0.2 percentage points?

Number of samples is \(n = 500 \times 20 = 10{,}000\). The sample proportion is \(p = 800/10{,}000 = 0.08\).

\[ p(1-p) = 0.08 \times 0.92 = 0.0736, \qquad \frac{0.0736}{10{,}000} = 0.00000736 \] \[ \sqrt{0.00000736} = \sqrt{7.36}\times 10^{-3} = 2.713 \times 10^{-3} = 0.002713 \] \[ \text{half-width} = 1.96 \times 0.002713 = 0.00532 \]

The interval is \(0.08 \pm 0.0053\), which is \((0.0747, 0.0853)\), or about 7.5% to 8.5% of run time. Check the normal approximation with \(np = 800\) and \(n(1-p) = 9200\), both far above 10, so it's fine.

For a half-width of \(e = 0.002\) (absolute),

\[ n = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.0736}{0.002^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.0736}{0.000004} = \frac{0.28274}{0.000004} = 70{,}685.4 \]

Round up to 70,686 samples. At 500 Hz that's \(70{,}686/500 = 141.4\) seconds of running. You could sample at 5 kHz to get there in 14 seconds, but then you've raised the overhead tenfold, which is exactly the thing sampling was supposed to keep fixed (Meas p.7).

Easy to get wrong

  • Treating profiler output as exact. "8.00%" from a sampler is an estimate with a standard error, and it can't see anything that happens between samples.
  • Saying sampling overhead depends on the workload. It depends only on the rate you chose. Counting and tracing are the ones whose cost rises with event frequency.
  • Choosing sampling for a rare event. A 1-in-a-million slow request will almost never be under a sample. Trace it, ideally only when a latency threshold is crossed.
  • Mixing up relative and absolute \(e\) in the sample size formula. The CI slides use \(e p\), the study guide uses \(e\) alone.

Clocks, interval timers and rollover

The idea

Time is the most measured quantity in CS and the least examined (Meas p.10). The word "time" hides three different quantities, and the clock you read has its own limits. If a paper doesn't say which time it measured and with which clock, you don't actually know its result (Meas p.11).

Underneath every software clock is an interval timer, which is just a counter that ticks at a fixed period. You read it before and after the event and multiply the difference by the tick length. That simple design gives you two built-in limits. The tick length sets the resolution, and the counter's width sets how long an interval you can time before it wraps around.

What to know

Three times (Meas p.11).

TimeWhat it includesWho cares
Wall timeElapsed real time, including waiting, I/O and other tenantsThe user
CPU timeOnly time your process held a core, excluding blocking and deschedulingWhoever claims an optimisation cut work
Steady-state timeTime after caches fill and the JIT finishesBenchmark reports

Under load these diverge sharply, so neither wall time nor CPU time is "more rigorous". They answer different questions.

Which clock (Meas p.12). Use clock_gettime(CLOCK_MONOTONIC) for elapsed time because it never runs backwards. Use clock_gettime(CLOCK_PROCESS_CPUTIME_ID) for CPU time. Both are cheap because the OS serves them without a system call. Don't use gettimeofday() in new code, since it's obsolescent and NTP can move it backwards mid-measurement. Report your clock and its resolution the way you'd report a microscope's magnification.

The cycle counter changed meaning (Meas p.13). The old recipe read the cycle counter, calibrated the MHz and divided. On current hardware the counter is invariant, meaning it ticks at a fixed rate regardless of frequency scaling and turbo. It already measures elapsed time, so dividing by a rated MHz is now wrong. The read can also be reordered by out-of-order execution unless serialised, and the counter isn't guaranteed to agree across cores. The lesson is that an instrument's error model expires, so re-derive it instead of inheriting it.

Warm-up (Meas p.14). Caches, branch predictors, JIT compilers and connection pools make early iterations unrepresentative. Discarding the first N iterations is a decision, so say how N was chosen. Some small benchmarks never reach a steady state at all, which is why you plot the iterations before you summarise them.

A warm-up policy you can defend (measurement study guide, question 10). First, run far more iterations than you plan to keep and plot per-iteration time against iteration number for several process invocations, not one, because one invocation can't show you what varies between invocations. Second, pick the cut point with a rule stated in advance, such as "discard until the running mean of the rest changes by less than some threshold", instead of eyeballing it. Third, check that the series actually flattens, and if it doesn't, report that as a finding. In the methods section you report the plot or its summary, the rule, the resulting N, and that N was fixed before the comparison was run.

The handout's warm-up papers are why this matters. Georges et al. (OOPSLA 2007) built a whole protocol for Java around the question "has it settled yet", since JIT compilation, class loading and GC keep changing the system while you measure it. Barrett et al. (OOPSLA 2017) found that small, deterministic, well-studied benchmarks often slow down, oscillate between levels or never settle (Meas p.14, measurement-in-practice handout, entries 3 and 5). If a series oscillates between two levels, "mean of the last 20 iterations" reports whatever mix of the two levels happened to land in that window, and nothing in the usual workflow warns you unless you plotted it.

Interval timer arithmetic (textbook, Lilja ch. 6). The Meas slides don't do this arithmetic, but the course agenda lists interval timers and cycle counters as tools you should know (Intro p.18), and the handout's reference table says a paper that reports sub-microsecond timings without naming its clock has told you something by leaving it out (measurement-in-practice handout). With tick period \(T_c\) and an \(n\)-bit counter,

\[ T_e \approx (\text{end} - \text{start})\, T_c, \qquad T_{\max} = (2^n - 1)\, T_c \]

If the counter wraps once during the event, the end reading is smaller than the start reading, and the elapsed ticks are \(\text{end} + 2^n - \text{start}\). If it can wrap more than once, you can't tell how many times it did, so the reading is useless.

Worked example

(a) Rollover limits. Handy facts are \(2^{16} = 65{,}536\), \(2^{32} \approx 4.295 \times 10^9\) and \(2^{64} \approx 1.845 \times 10^{19}\).

A 16-bit counter with \(T_c = 10\) ns wraps after \((65{,}536 - 1) \times 10 = 655{,}350\) ns, about 655 µs.

A 32-bit counter with \(T_c = 1\) µs wraps after \(4.295 \times 10^9\) µs \(= 4295\) s, and \(4295 / 60 = 71.6\) minutes.

The same 32-bit counter ticking every 1 ns (a 1 GHz counter) wraps after \(4.295 \times 10^9\) ns \(= 4.29\) s. That's why a 32-bit cycle counter can't time anything longer than a few seconds.

A 64-bit counter at 1 ns wraps after \(1.845 \times 10^{19}\) ns \(= 1.845 \times 10^{10}\) s. A year is about \(3.15 \times 10^7\) s, so that's \(1.845 \times 10^{10} / 3.15 \times 10^{7} \approx 585\) years, which is why 64-bit counters never worry anyone.

(b) A reading across one wrap. The 16-bit, 10 ns counter reads 65,000 at the start and 1,200 at the end. The end is smaller, so it wrapped once.

\[ \text{ticks} = 1200 + 65{,}536 - 65{,}000 = 1736, \qquad T_e \approx 1736 \times 10 \text{ ns} = 17{,}360 \text{ ns} = 17.36 \text{ µs} \]

Naive subtraction would give \(1200 - 65{,}000 = -63{,}800\) ticks, a negative time, which is the giveaway.

Easy to get wrong

  • Using \(2^n\) where the formula wants \(2^n - 1\). For big \(n\) it doesn't matter numerically, but write the textbook form.
  • Dividing an invariant cycle count by the CPU's rated MHz. On current hardware the count already tracks elapsed time.
  • Calling CPU time the "correct" one. If your change removes blocking or overlaps I/O, CPU time won't show it.
  • Treating "we discarded the first 10 iterations" as a fact instead of a choice you have to justify.

Resolution, quantization error and timer overhead

The idea

A timer can only report whole ticks. An event that starts and stops between ticks gets rounded, and whether it rounds up or down depends on where the event happened to start relative to the clock. The same event can read 13 ticks one time and 14 the next (CI p.9). That rounding is quantization error. It's random in direction, which is why the CI lecture files it under random error (CI p.10).

The clock read itself also costs time. If the event is long that cost vanishes, but for very short events the reads can be most of what you measure.

What to know

Accuracy, precision, resolution (CI p.16-18, Meas p.20).

TermMeaningComes fromRepetition helps?
AccuracyHow close the mean of your measurements is to the true valueSystematic errorNo
PrecisionHow repeatable the measurements are, their spreadRandom errorYes, it narrows the interval
ResolutionSmallest increment the tool can distinguish, like one tickThe tool itselfNot for a single reading

Accuracy can only be judged against an outside standard, resolution is fixed by the tool, and precision is the part statistics can quantify (CI p.18).

Quantization bound. With resolution \(T_c\), one measurement is off by up to one tick, so \(T_{\text{true}} \in (T_m - T_c,\ T_m + T_c)\) and the relative error is at most about \(T_c / T_e\). The study guide's example is a 180 ns event on a 100 ns clock, which can only come back as 100 or 200 ns (measurement study guide, Part B). If you want quantization error under some fraction \(x\), the timed interval has to be at least \(T_c / x\) long.

Timer overhead. If one clock read costs \(c\) and you bracket an event of length \(T\) with two reads, the share of the measured time that is overhead is

\[ \frac{2c}{T + 2c} \]

(measurement study guide, Part B). The rule of thumb is that the event should be one to two orders of magnitude longer than a clock read, or you shouldn't time it on its own.

Worked example

(a) Quantization. A timer ticks every 1 µs and an event lasts about 40.3 µs. A single reading comes back as 40 or 41 µs, never 40.3. The worst-case error is one tick, so the relative error can be up to \(1/40 = 2.5\%\).

To get that under 0.5% the timed interval has to be at least \(1 \text{ µs} / 0.005 = 200\) µs. One event is 40.3 µs, so time a loop of \(k\) of them with \(k \times 40.3 \ge 200\), which gives \(k \ge 4.96\), so \(k = 5\). Five back-to-back events last about 201.5 µs, and one tick is \(1/201.5 \approx 0.5\%\) of that.

(b) Overhead. A clock read costs \(c = 20\) ns and the operation takes 40 ns. Two reads cost 40 ns.

\[ \frac{2c}{T + 2c} = \frac{40}{40 + 40} = 0.5 \]

Half of every reading is the timer. If you instead time 1000 operations between one pair of reads, the operations take 40,000 ns and the overhead is \(40/40{,}040 \approx 0.1\%\).

Easy to get wrong

  • Thinking more repetitions improve resolution. Averaging many single readings smooths random quantization, but each reading still has one-tick resolution. To actually shrink resolution you lengthen the timed interval.
  • Calling a tight interval "accurate". A narrow interval is precise. It says nothing about systematic error (Meas p.20).
  • Forgetting the second clock read. Bracketing costs \(2c\), not \(c\).
  • Reporting four significant figures from three runs. The measurement-in-practice handout's reference table says that paper is confusing accuracy with precision, because the extra digits claim a certainty that neither the spread nor the tool's resolution supports.

Timing short events, and how many repetitions it takes

The idea

When an event is close to or shorter than one tick, a single reading is useless. There are two fixes. If you can run the event back to back, time a loop and divide, which spreads one tick over many events. If the event can't be looped cleanly and it's shorter than a tick, you can still estimate it statistically. Each time you run it, check whether a tick fell inside it. The fraction of runs that saw a tick estimates \(T_e / T_c\), and from there it's a proportion problem.

What to know

Loop amortisation (measurement study guide, Part B). Time \(k\) operations in a loop, time the empty loop, and subtract.

\[ \text{per-op time} = \frac{T_{k\text{ ops}} - T_{\text{loop}}}{k}, \qquad \text{resolution} = \frac{T_c}{k} \]

This assumes each operation costs the same in a loop as it does alone. Often it doesn't, because the loop warms the cache and branch predictor, and the compiler may hoist or delete a body whose result you never use.

The tick-counting method (textbook, Lilja ch. 6). For \(T_e < T_c\), each run reads either 0 or 1 tick. If a tick boundary lands at a uniformly random point relative to the event, the chance one falls inside it is \(T_e / T_c\). Run the event \(n\) times and count the \(m\) runs that saw a tick.

\[ p = \frac{m}{n}, \qquad T_e \approx p\, T_c \]

Put the CI slides' proportion interval on \(p\) and multiply both ends by \(T_c\) (CI p.48). To get the estimate within a relative error \(e\), use the CI slides' sample size (CI p.54).

\[ n = \frac{z_{1-\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)}{(e p)^2} \]

For timing means rather than proportions, the matching rule is \(n = \left(z_{1-\alpha/2}\, s / (e \bar{x})\right)^2\) from a pilot run (CI p.44). Either way, halving the interval costs four times the repetitions.

Worked example

(a) Loop. On a 1 µs clock you time 10,000 iterations of an operation and get 520 µs. The empty loop takes 120 µs.

\[ \text{per-op} = \frac{520 - 120}{10{,}000} \text{ µs} = \frac{400}{10{,}000} \text{ µs} = 0.04 \text{ µs} = 40 \text{ ns} \]

The resolution of that per-op number is \(1000 \text{ ns}/10{,}000 = 0.1\) ns, so quantization is no longer the problem. Caching and compiler effects are.

(b) Tick counting. The clock ticks every \(T_c = 10\) µs and the event is too short to see directly. You run it \(n = 1000\) times and 230 runs saw a tick.

\[ p = \frac{230}{1000} = 0.23, \qquad T_e \approx 0.23 \times 10 = 2.3 \text{ µs} \] \[ p(1-p) = 0.23 \times 0.77 = 0.1771, \qquad \sqrt{\frac{0.1771}{1000}} = \sqrt{0.0001771} = 0.01331 \] \[ 1.96 \times 0.01331 = 0.0261, \qquad p \in (0.23 - 0.0261,\ 0.23 + 0.0261) = (0.2039,\ 0.2561) \]

Multiply by 10 µs and the 95% interval for the event length is about \((2.04,\ 2.56)\) µs. The normal approximation is fine since \(np = 230\) and \(n(1-p) = 770\).

(c) How many runs for ±5% at 95%? Use the pilot \(p = 0.23\) and \(e = 0.05\).

\[ e p = 0.05 \times 0.23 = 0.0115, \qquad (0.0115)^2 = 0.00013225 \] \[ n = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.1771}{0.00013225} = \frac{0.68035}{0.00013225} = 5144.4 \]

Round up to 5145 runs. You already did 1000, so you need about 4145 more.

Easy to get wrong

  • Rounding the sample size down. Always round up, since 5144 runs gives a half-width slightly over 5%.
  • Forgetting to subtract the empty loop. The loop counter and branch cost time too.
  • Assuming loop timing equals cold, one-off timing. The loop measures the hot-cache cost, which may not be what production sees.
  • Using the tick-counting method when the start of the event is synchronised with the clock. Then the "uniformly random phase" assumption fails and \(m/n\) is biased.

Perturbation and indirect measurement

The idea

You can't watch a running system without joining it (Meas p.15). Instrumentation uses the very cycles, cache lines, memory bandwidth and I/O it's trying to measure, and it can change behaviour you weren't even looking at. A concurrency bug can disappear because you traced it (Meas p.16). This is the observer effect. The finer you observe, the more of the system you occupy, so resolution always costs perturbation.

Sometimes you can't observe the quantity at all, such as the load on a machine you don't control. Then you measure something you can see and derive what you want, which is indirect measurement (Meas p.18).

What to know

Perturbation isn't additive (Meas p.17). Measuring each probe's cost and subtracting the sum fails. Two probes together can cost more than the sum, because their buffers evict the working set, or less, because the second probe's data is already in cache. So "overhead was negligible" needs evidence. Run with and without instrumentation and report both.

\[ \text{perturbation} = \frac{T_{\text{inst}} - T_{\text{base}}}{T_{\text{base}}} \]

If you can't run the uninstrumented system, say you can't bound the perturbation.

Indirect measurement (Meas p.18). To estimate competing load, time a known program on a known-idle machine, then time it now. The ratio tells you what share of the machine you're getting. The same shape gives energy from power draw, contention from latency, and capacity from response time. The derived number inherits the noise of both inputs, and for a ratio \(R = A/B\) of independent inputs the relative errors add in quadrature (measurement study guide, Part B).

\[ \left(\frac{\sigma_R}{R}\right)^2 \approx \left(\frac{\sigma_A}{A}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{\sigma_B}{B}\right)^2 \]

Speedup, efficiency and energy per operation are all derived like this. Hoefler and Belli (SC 2015) point out that speedup is a ratio of two noisy numbers, so it needs its own uncertainty, and their position is that an average should only be reported when the measurement has no variance, which in benchmarking is essentially never (measurement-in-practice handout, entry 6).

Energy from a power meter (measurement study guide, question 16). With only a whole-machine meter sampled at 1 Hz, you measure idle power long enough to see its own spread, then power with the workload running, integrate both over the same 20 s window, subtract, and repeat in randomised order. The result inherits three errors. It assumes idle draw is unchanged by your workload, which fails if the workload changes frequency, wakes a sleeping package or spins up fans. At 1 Hz you only get about 20 samples, so any phase shorter than a second is invisible. And it's a difference of two noisy numbers, so it's noisier than either.

"Overhead was negligible" as a reviewer (measurement study guide, questions 17c and 18). Overhead isn't one constant applied equally to both systems. Different probe counts or cache pressure can slow one configuration more than the other, so a 2% mean overhead can still hide a differential of the same size as your claimed effect. The evidence that would satisfy a reviewer is paired runs with and without the probes, in the configuration actually used for the results, in randomised order, with the overhead reported as a difference with an interval.

Worked example

(a) Perturbation. A benchmark takes 50.0 s without tracing and 53.0 s with it.

\[ \frac{53.0 - 50.0}{50.0} = \frac{3.0}{50.0} = 0.06 = 6\% \]

The tracer has two probes that cost 1.8 s and 1.5 s when each runs alone. That sums to 3.3 s, not 3.0 s. It isn't a contradiction, since the two probes probably share cached data or a log flush, so together they cost less than separately. Only the with-and-without number is trustworthy.

(b) Indirect load. A calibrated loop takes 2.0 s on an idle machine and 2.5 s now.

\[ \text{share you get} = \frac{2.0}{2.5} = 0.8, \qquad \text{competing load} \approx 1 - 0.8 = 0.2 \]

If each timing is uncertain by 2% (relative), the ratio is uncertain by about \(\sqrt{2^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{8} \approx 2.8\%\), which is worse than either input.

The inference can still be wrong with perfect timings. Your loop might be CPU-bound while the neighbour is memory-bound, or the slowdown might come from thermal throttling and not from load at all. Indirect measurement is only as good as the model linking the two quantities.

Easy to get wrong

  • Adding up per-probe costs to estimate total overhead. Perturbation is non-additive in both directions.
  • Assuming a small mean overhead can't explain your effect. Instrumentation can slow one configuration more than another, so measure both with and without.
  • Reporting a derived ratio (speedup, energy per op) without noticing it's noisier than either measurement it came from.

Errors repetition won't fix

The idea

The question for the whole lecture is which errors shrink when you repeat the experiment and which don't (Meas p.3). Random error goes in both directions and averages out, so more runs make the interval narrower. Systematic error pushes every run the same way, so more runs just make you more confident about a wrong number (Meas p.20). Repetition is cheap and feels rigorous, but it only ever buys you precision.

What to know

Random errorSystematic error
Unpredictable direction, cancels across runsSame direction every run, untouched by repetition
Sets precision, the spreadSets accuracy, where the cluster sits
Covered by the confidence intervalEntirely outside the interval

The CI slides give examples of each. Clock drift from temperature or forgetting to clear the cache is systematic. Tool limits and random processes in the system are random (CI p.7-8).

Measurement bias is ordinary (Meas p.21). Link order and the size of the UNIX environment shift run time enough to reverse a paper's conclusion, and every biased run is perfectly repeatable. None of the 133 surveyed ASPLOS, PACT, PLDI and CGO papers handled it adequately.

Randomise what you can't control (Meas p.22). You can't remove a nuisance factor you don't know about, but you can turn it from systematic into random. Re-randomise code, stack and heap layout between runs, randomise the order of conditions instead of all A then all B, and spread runs over hours and days. Then the nuisance shows up as spread, which repetition does fix.

The machine isn't a constant (Meas p.23). On shared cloud hardware there are noisy neighbours, migration and throttling, and a three-month AWS study found variation by hour of day and day of week. The time you ran the experiment is an experimental factor whether you recorded it or not.

Confounding (measurement study guide, question 23). Run system A on Monday night and baseline B on Wednesday afternoon and "system" and "time slot" become the same variable, so no analysis can pull them apart. Five hundred runs on Monday night only estimate Monday night more precisely. The fix is to interleave A and B in randomised order across the same hours and days.

A four-point randomisation plan (measurement study guide, question 12). (i) Interleave conditions run by run so drift hits both equally. (ii) Randomise the order within each round, so a periodic disturbance can't line up with a strict A, B, A, B pattern. (iii) Spread runs over all hours and days, so time of day becomes noise both systems share. (iv) Re-randomise layout, link order and environment size inside the machine. What this can't remove is an offset shared by every run, such as an instance type unlike your readers' hardware or a clock source that under-reports, because randomisation only turns differences between conditions into noise.

When the bias is as big as the effect (measurement study guide, question 13). If you claim 8% but layout alone moves the benchmark by about ±12%, re-randomise layout between runs so the ±12% becomes spread, use a paired design across the same randomised layouts, and run enough repetitions (from a pilot and the sample-size formula) that the interval on the difference is clearly narrower than 8%. If you can't get there, report that honestly, for example "our interval on the difference is (−2%, +14%)". The failure the lecture warns about is a narrow interval computed from one fixed layout.

Worked example

The true mean is 10.0 ms, but your setup adds a fixed +0.6 ms bias (say, an unlucky link order). Run-to-run spread is \(s = 2\) ms. Watch what more runs do.

With \(n = 100\), the standard error is \(2/\sqrt{100} = 0.2\) and the 95% half-width is \(1.96 \times 0.2 = 0.392\). Your sample mean sits near 10.6, so the interval is about \((10.21,\ 10.99)\). It already misses 10.0.

With \(n = 400\), the standard error is \(2/\sqrt{400} = 0.1\) and the half-width is \(0.196\). The interval is about \((10.40,\ 10.80)\). It's half as wide and further from the truth relative to its width.

Quadrupling the runs halved the interval and did nothing to the 0.6 ms bias. Re-randomising layout between runs would have turned that bias into extra spread, giving a wider interval that actually covers 10.0.

Easy to get wrong

  • Saying 1000 runs makes a result "more accurate". It makes it more precise. Accuracy depends on systematic error.
  • Running system A on Monday night and system B on Wednesday afternoon. System and time are now confounded, and no analysis can separate them.
  • Thinking randomisation removes a bias shared by every run. It only turns differences between conditions into noise.

From numbers to a claim

The idea

Even with a good instrument, how you repeat, summarise and report decides whether the number supports your claim. Repetitions come in nested levels, each with its own variance, and repeating the cheap level can be nearly worthless. The summary has to match the claim, and the report has to give enough detail that someone else could repeat it.

What to know

Levels of repetition (Meas p.25). Iterations sit inside runs, runs inside processes, processes inside rebuilds. Twenty iterations in one process aren't twenty measurements, because layout, JIT decisions and core assignment are fixed for all twenty. With \(p\) processes of \(k\) iterations each (measurement study guide, Part B),

\[ \operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = \frac{\sigma_p^2}{p} + \frac{\sigma_i^2}{p\,k} \]

Only the first term responds to more processes, and no number of iterations can shrink it. Spend the budget where the variance lives. Kalibera and Jones found 71 papers with no measure of variation at all, and van der Kouwe et al. found preventable flaws in 96% of 50 security papers (Meas p.25). This matters because reported improvements in systems work are often around 10%, which is inside the range a careless setup can manufacture (Meas p.2).

Pseudo-replication (measurement study guide, question 20). A mean over 20 iterations in one process with a ±0.3% interval describes how much that one invocation varied internally, which is the smallest source of variation in the experiment. Readers take it to mean "rerun this and you'll land within 0.3%", which it doesn't say. The interval they want comes from many independent process invocations, ideally with layout re-randomised, each reduced to one number (its post-warm-up mean), with the interval computed across invocations.

Spending a budget that has costs (measurement study guide, question 11). If a process costs \(c_p\) to start and an iteration costs \(c_i\), the budget constraint is \(c_p\, p + c_i\, p k \le B\). You minimise \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x})\) under that constraint by trying a few allocations. When \(\sigma_p\) is much larger than \(\sigma_i\), the answer pushes \(p\) as high as the budget allows with \(k\) near 1.

Which summary (Meas p.26). The mean is pulled by outliers and assumes symmetric error, so it's rarely right for latency. The median and quantiles ignore a few slow outliers but need different interval machinery. The fastest run is the least polluted, because interference only slows code down, but it drifts lower the more runs you do, so it's an unstable estimate.

The slide's key sentence is that timing noise is one-sided, since nothing makes your code run faster than it can (Meas p.26). The study guide turns that into the strongest case for the minimum. What you observe is the true cost plus non-negative contamination, so the mean estimates cost plus average contamination while the fast end is the cleanest view. The case against has two parts. The minimum is an extreme order statistic, so it's noisy, has no standard interval, and keeps falling as \(n\) grows, which makes two papers with different \(n\) incomparable. And it answers a best-case question, while most claims are about typical or tail behaviour, where the contamination is part of what users feel. Report the full distribution and the median with a quantile or bootstrap interval, and give the minimum alongside with \(n\) stated, never in its place (measurement study guide, question 21).

Dropping outliers is a claim about the world (measurement study guide, question 6). Removing points says they came from something other than the operation, like an interrupt or a migration. That may be true, but you have to state the rule, say how many points it removed and report both numbers. Removing outliers after seeing which way it moves the answer is the timing version of p-hacking.

A number isn't a measurement yet (Meas p.27). Report the spread. Compare two systems with an interval on the difference, never by eyeballing two intervals. Non-overlap implies a difference, overlap implies nothing. For unpaired results the standard error of the difference is \(\sqrt{SE_A^2 + SE_B^2}\), not the sum of half-widths. If both systems ran on the same workloads, pair them and use Lecture 10's paired interval instead. The slide also asks whether your effect is larger than the artefacts you didn't control, and says that if you don't know, that's the finding to report. The interval covers sampling error only, so everything in Part 4 stays outside it (Meas p.27).

Measure the right thing (Meas p.28). An interval on mean latency says nothing about the 99th percentile. Closed-loop benchmarks, where a fixed set of clients each wait for a reply, can never overload the system, because the load slows down when the system does. Real arrivals keep coming, so tail latency under overload needs an open-loop generator (Meas p.28). An open-loop generator issues requests from an arrival process, such as Poisson at a chosen rate or a replayed trace, regardless of when replies come back, so a slow system builds a queue the way production would. The generator must not be the bottleneck itself, it should record queueing delay at the client, and you report the offered rate, not the achieved throughput (measurement study guide, question 14). Schroeder, Wierman and Harchol-Balter showed that switching between open and closed models can change which scheduling policy looks best (measurement-in-practice handout, entry 9).

Mean versus p99 (measurement study guide, question 24). A mean of 41 ms with ±0.2 ms over 100,000 requests says nothing about a 50 ms SLO written at the 99th percentile, because right-skewed latency can easily have a p99 ten times its mean. You should measure p99 itself with an order-statistic or bootstrap interval, across repeated runs. Picking the mean and its t interval is the statistics half of the problem and the easy one. The measurement half is bigger. One run's 100,000 requests show within-run variation only, and a closed-loop harness would give the tail of a self-throttling system anyway.

What to record (Meas p.29). Hardware (CPU model and count, governor, turbo, SMT). Software (OS and kernel, VM or shared host, compiler, flags, libraries). Experiment (workload and seeds, warm-up policy and how it was chosen, repetitions at each level). Analysis (which summary and why). Publish scripts and raw results. A measurement nobody can repeat is an anecdote.

Fixing a weak methods paragraph (measurement study guide, question 15). "Run on a 32-core server, 10 times, we report the average, overhead is negligible" is missing hardware detail (CPU model, governor, turbo, SMT, shared host or not), software detail (OS, kernel, compiler, flags, libraries), experiment detail (workload, seeds, warm-up rule, what "10 times" means and how runs were ordered in time) and analysis detail (which average, and no spread at all). The single most valuable addition is a measure of variability, because without it every other number is uninterpretable. Next is saying what the 10 repetitions are repetitions of, and "negligible" has to become a measured overhead.

The seven questions (Meas p.30). Use these on any measurement section, yours or one you review.

  1. Which clock or counter, at what resolution?
  2. Wall time, CPU time or steady state?
  3. How was the warm-up policy chosen?
  4. How many repetitions, at which level?
  5. Is perturbation measured, or merely asserted to be small?
  6. What here is systematic, and therefore immune to repetition?
  7. Is the summary the quantity the claim is about?

The wrap-up slide compresses the lecture into five lines (Meas p.31). Exactness, context, overhead, you get two. Say which time you measured, and remember error models expire. Perturbation is not additive and never zero. Repetition narrows the interval, never the bias. Report spread, not just centre, with enough detail to repeat it.

Worked example

Pilot data say \(\sigma_p = 6\) ms between processes and \(\sigma_i = 3\) ms between iterations, so \(\sigma_p^2 = 36\) and \(\sigma_i^2 = 9\). You can afford 36 measurements. Compare three ways to spend them.

Allocation\(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x})\) in ms²SE in ms
1 process × 36 iterations\(36/1 + 9/36 = 36 + 0.25 = 36.25\)\(\sqrt{36.25} = 6.02\)
6 processes × 6 iterations\(36/6 + 9/36 = 6 + 0.25 = 6.25\)\(\sqrt{6.25} = 2.50\)
36 processes × 1 iteration\(36/36 + 9/36 = 1 + 0.25 = 1.25\)\(\sqrt{1.25} = 1.12\)

Same 36 numbers, more than a fivefold difference in standard error. The second term is always \(9/36\) because \(p k = 36\) in every row, so all the difference comes from dividing \(\sigma_p^2\) by \(p\). One process with many iterations, the common habit, is the worst choice. In practice processes cost more to start than iterations, so the real optimum trades this against cost, which is study guide question 11.

With costs (study guide setting N). A process costs 8 s to start and an iteration 0.2 s, the budget is 600 s, \(\sigma_p = 8\) ms and \(\sigma_i = 2\) ms, so \(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x}) = 64/p + 4/(pk)\) and the cost is \(8p + 0.2pk \le 600\).

\(p \times k\)Cost in s\(\operatorname{Var}(\bar{x})\)SE in ms
73 × 1\(584 + 14.6 = 598.6\)\(0.877 + 0.055 = 0.932\)0.97
60 × 5\(480 + 60 = 540\)\(1.067 + 0.013 = 1.080\)1.04
30 × 25\(240 + 150 = 390\)\(2.133 + 0.005 = 2.139\)1.46
10 × 250\(80 + 500 = 580\)\(6.4 + 0.0016 = 6.402\)2.53

The study guide's answer is 73 × 1 with SE 0.97 ms. If you search every whole-number allocation, 71 × 2 (cost 596.4 s, Var \(0.901 + 0.028 = 0.930\)) is better only in the third decimal, so either answer is fine on the exam. The rule is the point. \(\sigma_p\) is four times \(\sigma_i\), so each extra process is worth more than any number of extra iterations.

Data set T and outliers (study guide question 6). Thirty timings have mean 13.85 ms, \(s = 5.26\), median 13.00. The standard error is \(5.26/\sqrt{30} = 5.26/5.477 = 0.960\), and with \(t_{0.025,29} = 2.045\) the half-width is \(2.045 \times 0.960 = 1.96\), giving \((11.89,\ 15.81)\) ms. Dropping the two large values 26.1 and 35.0 leaves \(n = 28\), mean 12.66, \(s = 2.46\). Now the standard error is \(2.46/\sqrt{28} = 2.46/5.292 = 0.465\), and \(2.052 \times 0.465 = 0.95\), giving \((11.71,\ 13.61)\) ms. The half-width shrank by \(1.96/0.95 \approx 2.1\) times and the centre moved \(13.85 - 12.66 = 1.19\) ms, which is more than the new half-width of 0.95. (The study guide says "more than the entire width of the second interval", but that width is \(13.61 - 11.71 = 1.90\) ms, so half-width is the comparison that holds.) That's why removing points needs a stated rule and both results reported. The study guide prints the first interval's upper end as 15.82 because it uses the unrounded mean 13.853, so either is fine.

Easy to get wrong

  • Computing a t interval over iterations from one process and calling it the run-to-run uncertainty. That's pseudo-replication and it can understate the real error several times over.
  • Reporting the minimum of n runs without n. It keeps falling as n grows, so two papers with different n aren't comparable.
  • Concluding "no difference" because two intervals overlap.
  • Supporting a p99 claim with an interval on the mean.
  • Dropping outliers quietly, or only after seeing which way it moves the answer.
  • Measuring tail latency with a closed-loop harness, which can never overload the system.

Measurement in the wild, the companion handout

The idea

Setia said on 10/20 that this handout matters a lot, because the lecture can't cover every subfield. The lecture hands you instruments. The handout is about what goes wrong when those instruments meet a real experiment and a deadline. Several entries are surveys where a community audited its own papers and didn't like what it found, which is useful precisely because the failures are ordinary, not exotic (measurement-in-practice handout).

The question to carry through every entry is the lecture's question, which errors shrink when you repeat and which don't (Meas p.3). The entries marked "also L6" also appear on the confidence-intervals handout. There they show what an interval does and doesn't cover, and here they show how the measurement was made in the first place. The handout's intro says four entries are shared, but it actually marks five (1, 2, 3, 8 and 11), so go by the marks.

The 10/20 version splits the papers in two. Entries 1 to 11 are computer systems papers, where the lecture's instruments are native. Entries 12 to 17 carry the same question into networking, wireless, software engineering, HCI, machine learning and robotics. The instrument changes to a packet trace, a simulator, a participant, a random seed or a physical robot, but each failure rhymes with a systems one and the entry names which. Setia's advice is to read the entry for your own area first and then the systems entry it points back to, because the systems entry shows the same failure where it was first documented. For networking, streaming and VLM work that means entries 12, 13 and 16 first, then 1, 9, 2 and 4.

What to know

Reading a paper with lecture eyes. The handout opens with a table translating lecture concepts into what you'll see in a paper.

Lecture conceptWhat it looks like in a paper
Event counting"We collected PMU counters", perf stat, cache-miss and instruction counts
TracingFull request traces, ftrace or eBPF output, distributed spans
SamplingProfiles, flame graphs, "we sampled at 99 Hz"
Perturbation"Overhead was under X%", so check how they know
Interval counting vs. cycle countersRarely stated, so sub-microsecond timings with no clock named is itself a finding
Warm-up"The first N iterations were discarded", so check how N was chosen
Indirect measurementAny derived metric, such as speedup, efficiency or energy per operation
Accuracy vs. precisionFour significant figures from three runs confuses the two
Systematic errorConfiguration, layout, link order, environment, time of day

Part I, entries 1 to 11, the systems papers. The ones closest to networking and streaming work are 4, 6, 9, 10 and 11, and entries 1, 2, 4 and 9 are the ones the beyond-systems entries point back to most.

#PaperFindingLecture link
1Mytkowicz et al., Producing Wrong Data, ASPLOS 2009 (also L6)Link order and UNIX environment size can reverse a conclusion. None of 133 papers handled it.Accuracy vs. precision and measurement bias (Meas p.20-21)
2Curtsinger and Berger, STABILIZER, ASPLOS 2013 (also L6)Re-randomises code, stack and heap layout during execution so layout becomes random error, and argues the CLT then makes times roughly Gaussian.Randomise what you can't control (Meas p.22)
3Georges et al., Statistically Rigorous Java Performance Evaluation, OOPSLA 2007 (also L6)Protocol separating startup from steady state, intervals on means, ANOVA for several factors.Warm-up (Meas p.14)
4Kalibera and Jones, Rigorous Benchmarking in Reasonable Time, ISMM 2013Find which nested level holds the variance and spend a fixed budget there. 71 of the ISMM, TOPLAS and TACO papers they surveyed gave no variation at all, and typical claimed gains of about 10% sit inside what sloppy measurement can produce.Levels of repetition, sample size (Meas p.25)
5Barrett et al., VM Warmup Blows Hot and Cold, OOPSLA 2017, and Traini et al., EMSE 2023Small, deterministic microbenchmarks often slow down, oscillate or never settle.Warm-up assumes a steady state (Meas p.14)

8Raasveldt et al., Fair Benchmarking Considered Difficult, DBTest 2018 (also L6)Eight database pitfalls, among them unoptimised baselines, apples vs. oranges, over-tuning and cold/hot/warm confusion. Part I continued, entries 12 to 17, beyond systems. These are new in the 10/20 version. There's one entry per area, picked as the paper a student in that area should read before designing an experiment, and each one names the systems entry it rhymes with.

#PaperFindingRhymes withLecture linkDiscussion question, in short
12Networking. Paxson, Strategies for Sound Internet Measurement, IMC 2004Strategies from years of Internet measurement for imperfect devices, calibration, tracking precision and accuracy, huge datasets and reproducibility. Many popular tools never assess their own precision, and a sound study should say whether its precision is good enough for its claims.Entry 1, and Part II item 1Report the clock and its resolution (Meas p.12), accuracy vs. precision (p.20)A packet trace looks like ground truth. Name two ways it could be wrong while looking complete, and which one more repetition would reveal.
13Wireless. Kurkowski, Camp and Colagrosso, MANET Simulation Studies: The Incredibles, ACM SIGMOBILE MC2R 9(4), 2005Survey of simulation papers in MobiHoc. As simulation use grew, the credibility of results fell. Catalogues setup, execution and reporting pitfalls and asks papers to report average shortest-path hop count and average partitioning so readers can tell if the protocol was exercised at all.Entries 7 and 9Measuring the right thing (Meas p.28), record the workload and seeds (p.29)Which simulator parameters came from the default config file rather than from you, and would the conclusion survive a reviewer changing one?
14Software engineering. Arcuri and Briand, A Practical Guide for Using Statistical Tests to Assess Randomized Algorithms in SE, ICSE 2011A review of 2009 SE venues finds randomized algorithms like search-based and random testing in a large share of papers, with the randomness rarely accounted for. Gives guidance on how many runs, which tests, and reporting an effect size (Vargha-Delaney \(\hat{A}_{12}\)) next to the p-value.Entries 4, 3 and 6How many repetitions at which level (Meas p.25), compare with an interval on the difference (p.27)If a tool finds a bug in 3 of 30 runs and a competitor in 1 of 30, is the first better, and what must you report for a reader to decide?
15HCI. Cockburn, Gutwin and Dix, HARK No More: On the Preregistration of CHI Experiments, CHI 2018Reviews why and how other sciences adopted preregistration, what it changed, and how it would have to be adapted for HCI studies.Entry 7's incentives question, and Part II item 7Systematic error (Meas p.20), say which summary and why (p.29)List the analysis choices in your last experiment that you made after seeing data. Which could have changed the conclusion?
16Machine learning. Bouthillier et al., Accounting for Variance in Machine Learning Benchmarks, MLSys 2021Measures how much results move with data sampling, augmentation, initialization and hyperparameter choice, then estimates how often common comparison practice detects an improvement that isn't there, across five deep-learning tasks. Randomizing more sources of variation per run gets closer to the ideal estimator at about 51 times less compute than retraining exhaustively.Entries 2 and 4Randomise what you can't control (Meas p.22), levels of repetition (p.25)Most ML papers report a mean over 3 to 5 seeds with everything else fixed. Which variance sources does that sample, and which does it hold constant by accident?
17Robotics. Kress-Gazit et al., Robot Learning as an Empirical Science: Best Practices for Policy Evaluation, arXiv:2409.09491, 2024Success rate over a few physical trials hides most of what matters. Report experimental conditions, add metrics beyond success rate, run a statistical analysis and describe failure modes. Policies are very sensitive to lighting, object placement and initial pose, which papers rarely control or report. Scope is robot learning and manipulation, not planning or SLAM.Entries 1, 11 and 10The machine isn't constant (Meas p.23), report the spread (p.27), record conditions (p.29)A policy succeeds in 18 of 20 trials. What's the interval on that rate, and what do you need to know about trial order and resets before believing it?

The beyond-systems entries in more detail. Entries 12, 13 and 16 are the ones for networking, streaming and VLM work, so they get the most space here.

Entry 12 is about checking the instrument before believing the network. Paxson's point is that a measurement tool has its own precision and accuracy, and most tools never report them, so a study has to establish them itself. Much of the paper is consistency checks and redundant measurements that test the instrument against itself before any conclusion about the network is drawn. That's the lecture's advice to report your clock and resolution like a microscope's magnification (Meas p.12), applied to packet captures, probes and traceroutes. For the discussion question, a capture can silently drop packets when the capture host falls behind during a burst, and its timestamps come from the capture host's clock, which has a resolution and can be offset or stepped (p.12). A capture taken at the wrong vantage point also looks complete while missing a path. Repetition can reveal the drops if they vary from run to run, since the counts then disagree across runs. It can't reveal a clock offset or a bad vantage point, because those repeat identically every time, so they're systematic error (p.20).

Entry 13 is entry 9 moved to wireless. The scenario, not the protocol, can decide the winner. A routing protocol isn't really tested when the scenario has a low average hop count, since most packets go one hop, or when the network is heavily partitioned, since most packets can't be delivered at all. That's why Kurkowski et al. ask papers to report average shortest-path hop count and average partitioning. The general lesson is that every simulator or emulator default you didn't choose is still a choice, and it belongs in what you record (Meas p.29).

Entry 16 is STABILIZER's argument arriving in ML. A seed controls initialization, but data sampling, augmentation, and hyperparameter choice also move the result. Fixing all of those and varying only the seed gives a precise number about one particular setup, not an accurate one about the method, which is the precision vs. accuracy split on Meas p.20. Their counter-intuitive result is that randomizing more sources of variation in every run gets closer to the ideal estimator and costs about 51 times less compute than retraining exhaustively, because you sample the nuisance factors you'd otherwise hold fixed (p.22). It's also entry 4's question, which level of repetition actually holds the variance (p.25).

Entry 14 says a randomized tool needs statistics, not anecdotes. With enough runs any tiny difference becomes significant, so a p-value alone can't say whether one tool is worth using (HT p.17). The fix is an effect size, and their choice \(\hat{A}_{12}\) is the probability that a random run of A beats a random run of B, with 0.5 meaning no difference (Comp p.24, HT p.18). Entry 15 says that in a user study the analysis, not the instrument, is the weak point. Choosing the dependent measure, the outlier rule or the subgroup after seeing results is a systematic error, so adding participants doesn't remove it, and preregistering the analysis does. HARKing also shows up on HT p.30. Entry 17 says success rate over a few trials isn't a measurement protocol. Each physical trial is expensive, so the pull to under-repeat is stronger than anywhere else, and uncontrolled lighting or initial pose is the same nuisance factor problem as link order in entry 1 or time of day in entry 11.

Points the handout adds beyond the slides.

Entry 1 separates two things the lecture keeps close. Perturbation is your instrument disturbing the system. Measurement bias is your chosen configuration disturbing the result while the instrument reports it faithfully and repeatably. Both have unpredictable sign. The practical difference is the fix. You can bound perturbation by running with and without the instrument, but there's no instrument to remove for bias, so the only fix is to vary the configuration, which is what STABILIZER does.

Entry 2's move is to randomise a nuisance factor on purpose instead of holding it fixed. That's standard experimental design from other fields, and it arrived in systems measurement about thirty years late (Meas p.22).

Entry 8 justifies the median with quantile-based intervals for query times. The reason isn't convenience. Interference such as a checkpoint or a background process can only slow a query, so the slow tail is the polluted end and a summary that ignores it is the more honest one. This is the same one-sided noise argument as Meas p.26.

Entries 9 and 10 cover what the lecture mostly takes as given, the workload model and the choice of summary. Rigour in measuring the wrong workload or the wrong statistic doesn't rescue the claim, because a perfectly accurate mean latency is the wrong answer to a question about responsiveness.

Entry 11 asks whether cloud time-of-day variation is random or systematic. The answer depends on your schedule. Interleave runs across hours and days and it becomes random spread you can average. Run one overnight session and it's a fixed offset baked into every run.

Seven things to check (measurement-in-practice handout, Part II). These are the same seven questions as Meas p.30, each pointed at a paper, and the 10/20 version points four of them at the new entries too. Microsecond differences with no timing method named are unverified, and entry 12 makes the same point for network instruments. Wall, CPU and steady-state time answer different questions and diverge under load (p.11). Warm-up rules rest on an assumption entry 5 shows is often false. Twenty iterations in one process aren't twenty independent measurements (entry 4), and entries 14 and 16 say the same for randomized tools and ML training runs. Perturbation can't be estimated by summing components (p.17). Configuration, layout, link order, time of day and the machine's neighbours are systematic, and so are the simulation scenario, the analysis choices and a robot's initial conditions, so repetition touches none of them (entries 1 and 11, then 13, 15 and 17). Mean, median, minimum and p99 describe different things, and usually only one is what the claim is about (entries 8 and 10). Entry 14 adds effect size to that list and entry 17 adds success rate.

The homework exercise (Part III, now titled "Homework Exercise", no submission required). Part A audits a recent paper from your own area. Say why you picked it, then find the instrument and resolution, classify the strategy against event frequency, find the repetition level, find the warm-up policy, name one uncontrolled systematic error and say whether repetition would help, and check the summary against the claim. If your area is one of entries 12 to 17, use that entry as the standard you compare the paper against, so a networking or streaming paper gets judged against entry 12 and a VLM evaluation against entry 16. Part B times a small operation with two different clocks or profilers, plots all 30 runs instead of the mean, then changes something that shouldn't matter like function order, an environment variable or the time of day. If that moves the number by more than the run-to-run spread, you've reproduced entry 1 on your own machine.

Applying it to streaming work. Start from entry 12, because a video or VLM streaming study is a network measurement before it's anything else. End-to-end latency is wall time, so say which clock timed it and where (Meas p.11-12). If two machines timestamp the same frame, their clock offset goes straight into the one-way delay, so either sync and report the offset or time the round trip on one clock. A packet capture or a tc-shaped link is an instrument too, so check it against itself before trusting it, for example by confirming the shaped rate with a separate throughput test and checking the capture's drop count (entry 12). Entry 13 is the warning for emulated networks. A set of bandwidth traces that never falls below the lowest bitrate never exercises the adaptation logic, so report what the traces contain, such as their mean and minimum bandwidth and how often they dip, the way Kurkowski et al. ask for hop count and partitioning. A load test with a fixed set of clients that wait for each response is closed-loop and can't show the system under overload, which entry 9 warns about. Frame or response latency is right-skewed, so a deadline claim needs p99, not the mean (entry 10). A cloud model API timed over one evening carries that evening's load as a fixed offset, so interleave the configurations across hours and days (entry 11). For VLM accuracy, entry 16 applies directly. Rerunning the same clips with a new seed or temperature samples one source of variance while the clip set, the question set and the model version stay fixed by accident, so the number is precise about one setup rather than accurate about the task (p.20, p.22). Accuracy over N questions is a success rate, so give it a proportion interval (CI p.48) the way entry 17 asks for robot success rates. When two encoder settings run on the same clips, pair by clip (Comp p.6) and report an effect size with the p-value (entry 14). Pick the metric and the clip subset before looking at results, because choosing them afterwards is HARKing (entry 15). Metrics like accuracy per kilobit or speedup are ratios and carry the noise of both inputs (entry 6). Repeating requests within one session is the cheap level of repetition, and the variance may live across sessions or days (entry 4).

Worked example

(a) A warm-up rule on an oscillating benchmark (the entry 5 exercise). Say iterations 1 to 10 take 20 ms while the JIT runs. After that the benchmark alternates in blocks of 15 iterations, 10 ms for iterations 11 to 25, 14 ms for 26 to 40, and so on, so 71 to 85 are at 10 ms and 86 to 100 are at 14 ms. The policy is "discard warm-up and report the mean of the last 20 iterations".

If you stop at iteration 100, the last 20 are 81 to 100. That's 5 at 10 ms and 15 at 14 ms.

\[ \frac{5 \times 10 + 15 \times 14}{20} = \frac{50 + 210}{20} = \frac{260}{20} = 13.0 \text{ ms} \]

If you stop at iteration 90, the last 20 are 71 to 90. That's 15 at 10 ms and 5 at 14 ms.

\[ \frac{15 \times 10 + 5 \times 14}{20} = \frac{150 + 70}{20} = \frac{220}{20} = 11.0 \text{ ms} \]

Same benchmark, an 18% difference (\(2/11 \approx 0.18\)) that depends only on when you stopped. The run-to-run spread inside each window looks small, so nothing in the numbers warns you. Plotting the iterations would show the two levels at once.

(b) Uncertainty of a speedup (entry 6). The baseline takes 10.0 s and your system 8.0 s, each with a 3% relative standard error. The speedup is \(10.0/8.0 = 1.25\).

\[ \frac{\sigma_R}{R} \approx \sqrt{0.03^2 + 0.03^2} = \sqrt{0.0009 + 0.0009} = \sqrt{0.0018} = 0.0424 \]

So \(\sigma_R \approx 0.0424 \times 1.25 = 0.053\). A rough 95% range is \(1.25 \pm 1.96 \times 0.053 = 1.25 \pm 0.10\), or about 1.15 to 1.35. The headline "25% faster" is really "somewhere between 15% and 35% faster", and that's before any systematic error.

(c) An interval on a success rate (the entry 17 discussion question). A policy succeeds in 18 of 20 trials. The same arithmetic applies to a VLM that answers 18 of 20 questions correctly. The proportion is \(p = 18/20 = 0.9\), and the slide interval is \(p \pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\) (CI p.48).

\[ \sqrt{\frac{0.9 \times 0.1}{20}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.09}{20}} = \sqrt{0.0045} = 0.0671, \qquad 1.96 \times 0.0671 = 0.1315 \]

That gives \(0.9 \pm 0.13\), or 0.769 to 1.031. The top end is above 1, which no success rate can be, and that's the sign this interval is failing. It's the Wald interval, and CI p.49 warns it's problematic and points to the Wilson interval for \(n < 40\). There are only 2 failures behind it, so the normal approximation to the binomial is poor.
