Part A. Concepts (17 short questions, about 30 minutes)

Answer each in two or three sentences. For true or false items, say which and give the reason, since the reason is where the marks are.

A1. True or false, with a reason. (a) If MIPS(A) > MIPS(B), machine A runs your program faster than machine B. (b) Linearity is one of the properties a good metric must have. (c) Is MFLOPS a means-based or an ends-based metric?

(a) False. MIPS counts instructions per second, and machines differ in how much work one instruction does, so a machine with more MIPS can still take longer on your program. That's exactly the slides' example of an unreliable metric (Metrics p.8, Metrics p.17).

(b) False. Linearity and ease of use are nice but not necessary. The required four are reliable, repeatable, consistent and independent (Metrics p.13).

(c) Means-based. It counts floating-point operations whether or not they did useful work, and it reports 0 for an integer program that still sorts or searches. On the slides' spectrum MFLOPS sits near the means-based end with clock rate and MIPS, while execution time is the ends-based metric because it only counts what actually got done (Metrics p.19, Metrics p.27-28).

A2. A web search returns 25 pages. 15 of them are relevant, and it misses 35 other relevant pages. What are its precision and recall?

Precision is the share of returned pages that are relevant, so it's \(15/25 = 0.6\). Recall is the share of all relevant pages that were returned. There are \(15 + 35 = 50\) relevant pages in total, so recall is \(15/50 = 0.3\). This is the same setup as the slides' example with 30 returned, 20 relevant and 40 missed (Metrics p.32).

A3. Feitelson gives three meanings of "experimental computer science". Name them and say which one CS 700 is about.

The three are building systems as the counterpart to theory, experimentation as the feedback step in an engineering loop, and evaluating computer systems with the rigorous methods of the natural sciences. CS 700 is about the third one, which is why the course spends its time on measurement, error and statistics (Intro p.10).

A4. A first-year student says, "The advisor form isn't due until I've taken 24 credits, so I'll start looking that semester, and I'll open my first meeting by asking the professor to be my advisor." Give two reasons this plan is bad.

First, the 24-credit point is a hard deadline, and most faculty want an informal preliminary research co-operation phase before they agree to advise anyone. That phase can take a semester or more, so starting in the last semester leaves no room for it (Advisor p.3, Advisor p.7-8).

Second, the slides say not to open with "can you be my PhD advisor?". You should explain your background and interests and ask how you can get involved in their research, because the faculty member has to see you work first and may already be over-committed (Advisor p.11-12).

A5. True or false, with a reason. (a) Your CS 701 supervisor automatically becomes your PhD advisor. (b) If you can't find a CS 701 project this spring, you can take it next year. (c) If no faculty member agrees, the department will assign you one. (d) You should wait for one professor's answer before contacting a second.

(a) False. Declaring the PhD advisor is a separate form and a separate process within the first 24 credits, and many students end up with a different advisor and area than their 701 project (CS701 p.16, CS701 p.18).

(b) False. The catalog rule doesn't allow it, and 701 is only offered in Spring (CS701 p.25, CS701 p.2).

(c) False. A faculty member has to agree to supervise, the department doesn't assign one (CS701 p.26).

(d) False. You should contact several faculty at once, because answers take time and the form is due Oct 31 (CS701 p.21, CS701 p.7).

A6. Which of these is acceptable for CS 701, and why? (i) A survey of 30 papers on federated learning. (ii) Reimplementing three published cache policies and evaluating them with a new metric. (iii) A deliverable that reads "paper accepted at a conference by May". (iv) A deliverable that reads "a draft paper to be revised and submitted for publication later".

(i) Not acceptable on its own. A literature review is often part of a 701 project, but the project can't consist only of one, because it must have a hands-on component such as an implementation or experiments (CS701 p.10-11).

(ii) Acceptable. Implementing techniques from several papers and evaluating them, possibly with new metrics, is one of the slides' examples (CS701 p.12).

(iii) Not acceptable, because a conference or journal submission completed by the end of Spring can't be a deliverable (CS701 p.15).

(iv) Acceptable. The slides give this exact wording as the allowed alternative, and the report can then take the form of the draft paper (CS701 p.15).

A7. An author's papers have 15, 12, 7, 5, 5, 3 and 1 citations. What is the h-index?

The h-index is the largest \(h\) such that \(h\) papers each have at least \(h\) citations. Five papers have 5 or more citations (15, 12, 7, 5, 5), so \(h \ge 5\). Only three papers have 6 or more, so \(h = 6\) fails. The h-index is 5 (Pub p.6).

A8. (a) A conference uses double-blind review. What must your submission do, and what happens if it doesn't? (b) True or false. It's fine to send the same paper to two conferences at once as long as you withdraw one when the other accepts.

(a) You must leave out your names and affiliations and avoid indirect hints about who you are. A submission that breaks the double-blind rules is rejected right away, without review. The point is that reviewers judge the paper and not the authors' reputation (Pub p.21).

(b) False. A paper under review somewhere can't be submitted elsewhere, and the same holds for papers with substantial overlap. Dual submissions are often caught and draw severe sanctions (Pub p.20, Ethics p.14).

A9. Classify each as fabrication, falsification, plagiarism or none. (a) You drop two runs that went against your trend and don't mention it. (b) You run out of time and type in plausible numbers for one benchmark. (c) You paste a sentence from a survey into your related work with no quotation marks or citation. (d) After publishing, you find a timer bug that inflated your speedup by 3%.

(a) Falsification, because omitting data so the record misrepresents the research is the definition (Ethics p.13, Ethics p.20).

(b) Fabrication, because it means making up results and reporting them (Ethics p.20).

(c) Plagiarism. Verbatim text needs quotation marks and a citation, and even a paraphrase needs the citation (Ethics p.8-10).

(d) None. Research misconduct excludes honest error, though you should still correct the record (Ethics p.18).

A10. (a) You used ChatGPT to draft part of the related work in a paper for an ACM venue. Can you list it as a co-author, and what must you do? (b) You're extending your IEEE conference paper into a journal paper. What does the journal version need?

(a) No. ACM doesn't allow generative AI tools as authors. Using them to create content is allowed but must be fully disclosed, for example in the acknowledgements. Plain spelling, grammar and translation tools are the exception and don't need disclosure (Ethics p.24). You also take full responsibility for anything the tool produced, including plagiarism or fabricated facts (Ethics p.22).

(b) It needs substantially more material, typically 30% more for IEEE, and it must cite and attach the conference version with an explanation of the differences (Ethics p.14). Outside that case, copying your own published text counts as self-plagiarism (Ethics p.12).

A11. Which index of dispersion would you report for (a) CPU utilization in percent, (b) an unbounded, unimodal, symmetric quantity, (c) right-skewed request latencies with a few huge outliers?

(a) The range, because the quantity is bounded between 0 and 100 (Summ p.49).

(b) The coefficient of variation \(s/\bar x\), because for a unimodal symmetric distribution the standard deviation describes the spread well and dividing by the mean makes it unit-free (Summ p.41, Summ p.49).

(c) Percentiles or the SIQR. Outliers inflate the standard deviation the same way they drag the mean, while quartiles depend only on order (Summ p.45, Summ p.49). For the same reason you'd pair it with the median instead of the mean (Summ p.18).

A12. From one sample you get a 95% confidence interval of (47.6, 52.4) ms for the mean run time. Which statements are correct? (i) There's a 95% probability that \(\mu\) lies in (47.6, 52.4). (ii) If you repeated the whole experiment many times, about 95% of the intervals built this way would contain \(\mu\). (iii) About 95% of individual runs take between 47.6 and 52.4 ms.

Only (ii) is correct. The 95% belongs to the procedure. Out of 100 samples, about 95 of the intervals contain \(\mu\) (CI p.33).

(i) is wrong because \(\mu\) is a fixed number and this interval is fixed once computed, so it either contains \(\mu\) or it doesn't (confidence-intervals study guide).

(iii) is wrong because the interval is for the mean, not for single runs. Its width shrinks like \(1/\sqrt n\), so with enough runs it gets far narrower than the spread of individual runs (CI p.39).

A13. True or false. Running a benchmark 1000 times instead of 10 makes the result more accurate.

False. More runs narrow the confidence interval, and that's precision, which comes from random error. Accuracy depends on systematic error, such as clock drift or forgetting to clear the cache, and repetition doesn't touch it (CI p.7, CI p.17). You just get a tighter interval around a possibly wrong number (Meas p.20).

A14. A paper compares two schedulers with 5 runs each, gets p = 0.30, and concludes that "the two schedulers perform the same." (a) What's wrong? (b) If the schedulers really differ, what kind of error did the paper make, and what is its probability called? (c) At a fixed n, what happens to that probability if you lower \(\alpha\)?

(a) Failing to reject \(H_0\) isn't evidence for \(H_0\). With n = 5 the test had little chance of seeing a difference, so absence of evidence isn't evidence of absence (HT p.4, HT p.14). The paper should report the interval for the difference, which would show how wide the plausible range is (HT p.16).

(b) A Type II error, failing to reject a false \(H_0\). Its probability is \(\beta\), and \(1-\beta\) is the power (HT p.6).

(c) \(\beta\) goes up. At a fixed n, lowering \(\alpha\) raises \(\beta\), and the only way to lower both is to collect more data (HT p.6).

A15. You test 20 benchmarks, each at \(\alpha = 0.05\), and no real effect exists on any of them. (a) How many "significant" results do you expect? (b) What's the formula for the chance of at least one, and its value from the slides? (c) What per-test level would Bonferroni use?

(a) Each test has a 0.05 chance of a false positive, so you expect \(20 \times 0.05 = 1\) of them.

(b) For independent tests the family-wise error rate is \(1-(1-\alpha)^m = 1 - 0.95^{20}\), which the slides give as 64% (HT p.27).

(c) Test each at \(\alpha/m = 0.05/20 = 0.0025\), which keeps the family-wise rate at or below 0.05 under any dependence (HT p.28).

A16. True or false. The 95% intervals for systems A and B overlap a little, so there's no significant difference between them.

False. Non-overlap implies a difference, but overlap implies nothing. For 95% intervals, an overlap of up to about half a margin of error can still be significant at 0.05, because the standard error of the difference is smaller than the sum of the two half-widths. The right move is an interval on the difference (Comp p.15-16).

A17. (a) Pick counting, tracing or sampling for each, with a reason. (i) Total L2 misses in a 60-second run. (ii) The code path behind the one-in-a-million request that takes 100 times the median. (iii) A two-hour job where you have no idea where the time goes. (b) True or false. 20 iterations inside one process give you n = 20 independent measurements.

(a)(i) Counting. You need only an exact total, and a counter gives it with tiny overhead (Meas p.7-8).

(a)(ii) Tracing. You need context about a rare event, and sampling would almost surely miss it (Meas p.8).

(a)(iii) Sampling. Its overhead is set by your sampling rate and not by the workload, which matters for a long run you can't afford to slow down (Meas p.7).

(b) False. Repetition has nested levels, and everything that varies between processes, like memory layout or JIT decisions, is frozen across those 20 iterations. n counts independent experimental units, here processes, not measurements (Meas p.25, HT p.20).

Part B. Problems (8 problems, about 75 minutes)

Show every step, since that's how the homework was graded. State which formula you use and why before you plug in.

B1. Choosing a mean. For each part, say which mean (arithmetic, harmonic or geometric) you'd use and why, then compute it. (a) Five runs of a build take 120, 80, 150, 90 and 160 ms. What's the mean build time? (b) The same 120 MB file is sent over three paths at 40, 60 and 120 MB/s. What's the mean transfer rate? (c) Three features are added one on top of the other, and each improves solution quality over the previous version by 8%, 25% and 28%. What's the mean improvement per feature? You may use \(1.2^3 = 1.728\).

Show solution

(a) Arithmetic mean, because these are times and the sum of the times is the total time, so the mean should be directly proportional to it (Summ p.25, Summ p.27).

\[ \bar x_A = \frac{120 + 80 + 150 + 90 + 160}{5} = \frac{600}{5} = 120 \text{ ms}. \]

(b) Harmonic mean, because these are rates for the same amount of work, and a mean rate has to be inversely proportional to the total time (Summ p.28, Summ p.32).

\[ \bar x_H = \frac{3}{\frac{1}{40} + \frac{1}{60} + \frac{1}{120}} = \frac{3}{\frac{3 + 2 + 1}{120}} = \frac{3}{6/120} = \frac{3 \times 120}{6} = 60 \text{ MB/s}. \]

Check it the long way. The transfers take \(120/40 = 3\) s, \(120/60 = 2\) s and \(120/120 = 1\) s, so 360 MB move in 6 s, which is \(360/6 = 60\) MB/s. The arithmetic mean \((40+60+120)/3 = 73.3\) MB/s would overstate the real rate because it ignores that the slow path takes the most time.

(c) Geometric mean, because each feature builds on the previous one, so the improvements multiply (Summ p.30). Turn each percentage into a ratio and multiply.

\[ 1.08 \times 1.25 = 1.35, \qquad 1.35 \times 1.28 = 1.728, \qquad \bar x_G = (1.728)^{1/3} = 1.2. \]

So the mean improvement is 20% per feature. Check it by compounding, \(1.2^3 = 1.728\), which is the real total gain of 72.8%. The arithmetic mean of 8, 25 and 28 is 20.33%, and compounding that gives \(1.2033^3 \approx 1.742\), which overshoots the true total. This is HW3 Problem 1 with smaller numbers.

B2. Quartiles. Twelve request latencies in ms, in the order measured.

231560183112252014282217

Using the course convention \(f_i = (i - 0.5)/n\) with linear interpolation, find (a) the five-number summary, (b) the IQR and SIQR, (c) the 90th percentile. (d) Which center and which index of dispersion would you report, and why?

Show solution

Sort first. The ordered values \(x_{(1)}, \dots, x_{(12)}\) are

\(i\)123456789101112
\(x_{(i)}\)121415171820222325283160

The quantile function puts \(x_{(i)}\) at \(p = (i-0.5)/n\) and interpolates in between (Summ p.59). Solving \((i - 0.5)/n = p\) gives the position \(r = np + 0.5\). With \(n = 12\), take the whole part \(j\) of \(r\) and the fraction \(r - j\), then \(Q(p) = x_{(j)} + (r - j)\big(x_{(j+1)} - x_{(j)}\big)\).

(a) The quartiles.

\[ Q_1:\ r = 12(0.25) + 0.5 = 3.5, \quad Q_1 = 15 + 0.5(17 - 15) = 16. \] \[ Q_2:\ r = 12(0.5) + 0.5 = 6.5, \quad Q_2 = 20 + 0.5(22 - 20) = 21. \] \[ Q_3:\ r = 12(0.75) + 0.5 = 9.5, \quad Q_3 = 25 + 0.5(28 - 25) = 26.5. \]

The five-number summary is min 12, \(Q_1 = 16\), median 21, \(Q_3 = 26.5\), max 60 (Summ p.50).

(b) \(\text{IQR} = Q_3 - Q_1 = 26.5 - 16 = 10.5\) ms and \(\text{SIQR} = 10.5/2 = 5.25\) ms (Summ p.45).

(c) \(r = 12(0.9) + 0.5 = 11.3\), so \(Q(0.9) = x_{(11)} + 0.3\big(x_{(12)} - x_{(11)}\big) = 31 + 0.3(29) = 31 + 8.7 = 39.7\) ms.

(d) The sum is 285, so the mean is \(285/12 = 23.75\) ms, which sits above 8 of the 12 values because the single 60 pulls it up. Report the median of 21 ms and the SIQR of 5.25 ms, because the data are skewed with an outlier and both are based on order, so the outlier can't move them (Summ p.18, Summ p.45, Summ p.49).

Excel's PERCENTILE interpolates at position \(1 + (n-1)p\) instead and would give \(Q_1 = 16.5\) and \(Q_3 = 25.75\). Use the course convention on the exam, since that's what HW3 Problem 2 used.

B3. Confidence intervals for a mean. (a) Eight independent runs of a compile job take these times in seconds.

5246505450465250

Find the mean, the sample standard deviation, and the 90%, 95% and 99% confidence intervals for the mean. (b) Treating these 8 runs as a pilot, how many runs would you need to be 95% confident the mean is within ±2% of the true mean? (c) A different job is run 64 times, with \(\bar x = 120\) ms and \(s = 16\) ms. Give the 95% and 99% intervals, then test \(H_0: \mu = 125\) against a two-sided alternative at \(\alpha = 0.05\) and \(\alpha = 0.01\), with the p-value. (d) If that job had been run only 16 times with the same \(\bar x\) and \(s\), what would the 95% half-width be, and why is it so much wider?

Show solution

(a) The sum is \(52+46+50+54+50+46+52+50 = 400\), so \(\bar x = 400/8 = 50\) s. The deviations from 50 are 2, −4, 0, 4, 0, −4, 2, 0 and their squares are 4, 16, 0, 16, 0, 16, 4, 0.

\[ s^2 = \frac{\sum (x_i - \bar x)^2}{n - 1} = \frac{56}{7} = 8, \qquad s = \sqrt 8 \approx 2.828 \text{ s}, \qquad \frac{s}{\sqrt n} = \sqrt{\frac{8}{8}} = 1. \]

With \(n = 8\) and \(\sigma\) unknown you need the t distribution with \(n - 1 = 7\) degrees of freedom, assuming the run times are roughly normal (CI p.27, CI p.37). The interval is \(\bar x \pm t[\alpha/2,\,7]\, s/\sqrt n\).

\[ 90\%:\ 50 \pm 1.895(1) = (48.105,\ 51.895) \text{ s} \] \[ 95\%:\ 50 \pm 2.365(1) = (47.635,\ 52.365) \text{ s} \] \[ 99\%:\ 50 \pm 3.499(1) = (46.501,\ 53.499) \text{ s} \]

Higher confidence means a wider interval, because you need to cover more of the t distribution (CI p.32).

(b) The slides set the half-width equal to a fraction \(e\) of the mean and solve for \(n\), using \(z\) (CI p.40).

\[ n = \left(\frac{z[0.025]\; s}{\bar x\, e}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{1.96 \times 2.828}{50 \times 0.02}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{5.543}{1}\right)^2 \approx 30.73. \]

A cleaner way by hand is \(n = 1.96^2 \times s^2 / (\bar x e)^2 = 3.8416 \times 8 / 1 = 30.73\). Round up to 31 runs. The pilot gives \(s\), which is the slides' way around needing \(s\) before you have data (CI p.41). Read the wording carefully, though. On CI p.42-43 "within 7%" was turned into ±3.5%, so check whether a question gives the total width or the half-width.

(c) With \(n = 64 > 30\), \(s\) is a good stand-in for \(\sigma\) and the normal distribution is fine (CI p.27, CI p.31). The standard error is \(16/\sqrt{64} = 16/8 = 2\) ms.

\[ 95\%:\ 120 \pm 1.960(2) = 120 \pm 3.92 = (116.08,\ 123.92) \text{ ms} \] \[ 99\%:\ 120 \pm 2.576(2) = 120 \pm 5.152 = (114.848,\ 125.152) \text{ ms} \]

For the test, \(H_0: \mu = 125\) and \(H_1: \mu \ne 125\).

\[ Z = \frac{\bar x - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt n} = \frac{120 - 125}{2} = -2.5. \]

At \(\alpha = 0.05\), \(|Z| = 2.5 > 1.960\), so reject \(H_0\). At \(\alpha = 0.01\), \(|Z| = 2.5 < 2.576\), so don't reject. The two-sided p-value is \(2\big(1 - \Phi(2.5)\big) = 2(1 - 0.9938) = 2(0.0062) = 0.0124\), which sits between 0.01 and 0.05 and gives the same two verdicts. The intervals agree too, since 125 is outside the 95% interval and inside the 99% one. That's the slides' point that the non-rejection region is the confidence interval (HT p.8, HT p.13).

(d) Now \(n = 16 < 30\), so use \(t[0.025,\,15] = 2.131\), and the standard error is \(16/\sqrt{16} = 4\) ms. The half-width is \(2.131 \times 4 = 8.524\) ms, against 3.92 ms before, about 2.2 times wider. Two things did it. The standard error doubled because \(\sqrt{64}/\sqrt{16} = 2\), and the critical value grew from 1.960 to 2.131 because t has heavier tails to pay for estimating \(\sigma\) from a small sample (CI p.35, CI p.39). This is HW4 Problem 1 part 4.

B4. A proportion from a sampling profiler. A profiler interrupts every 10 ms during a 4-second run, which gives 400 samples. In 80 of them the program counter is inside parse(). (a) Find 90% and 95% confidence intervals for the fraction of time spent in parse(), and check that the normal approximation is reasonable. (b) How many samples do you need to be 95% confident that the estimate is within ±5% of the true fraction (relative error, as on the slides), and how long must the run be? (c) How would the answer to (b) change if the question asked for ±5 percentage points instead?

Show solution

(a) A sampling profiler reports a proportion, so the proportion interval applies (CI p.51-53, measurement study guide).

\[ p = \frac{80}{400} = 0.2, \qquad \sqrt{\frac{p(1-p)}{n}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.2 \times 0.8}{400}} = \sqrt{\frac{0.16}{400}} = \sqrt{0.0004} = 0.02. \]

The interval is \(p \pm z_{\alpha/2}\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}\) (CI p.48).

\[ 90\%:\ 0.2 \pm 1.645(0.02) = 0.2 \pm 0.0329 = (0.1671,\ 0.2329) \] \[ 95\%:\ 0.2 \pm 1.960(0.02) = 0.2 \pm 0.0392 = (0.1608,\ 0.2392) \]

The check is that \(np = 80\) and \(n(1-p) = 320\) are both well above 10, so the normal approximation to the binomial is fine (CI p.47, confidence-intervals study guide). If either were small, the Wald interval would be unreliable and you'd switch to Wilson or Agresti-Coull (CI p.49). So the profiler's "20%" really means somewhere between about 16% and 24%.

(b) The slides write the half-width as a fraction \(e\) of \(p\), so \(e p = 0.05 \times 0.2 = 0.01\) (CI p.54).

\[ n = \frac{z_{\alpha/2}^2\, p(1-p)}{(e p)^2} = \frac{1.96^2 \times 0.2 \times 0.8}{(0.01)^2} = \frac{3.8416 \times 0.16}{0.0001} = \frac{0.614656}{0.0001} = 6146.56. \]

Round up to 6147 samples. At one sample every 10 ms the run must last \(6147 \times 0.01 = 61.47\) s, about a minute. This is the same calculation as the slides' OS-time example, which needed 3.46 hours for ±0.5% relative error (CI p.55-57).

(c) "±5 percentage points" is an absolute half-width of 0.05, so the denominator becomes \(0.05^2 = 0.0025\) and \(n = 0.614656/0.0025 = 245.9\), which rounds up to 246. The relative version needs 25 times more samples here, because \(0.05/0.01 = 5\) and \(n\) grows with the square. The measurement study guide uses the absolute form, so always check which one the question means.

B5. Hypothesis test for a mean. A team believes its new memory allocator brings the mean allocation latency below the old allocator's long-run mean of 40 µs. They measure 16 independent runs, each in a fresh process, and get \(\bar x = 37\) µs with \(s = 6\) µs. Assume the run means are roughly normal. (a) State \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) and justify the choice. (b) Test at \(\alpha = 0.05\). (c) Bracket the p-value using the table. (d) What's the verdict at \(\alpha = 0.01\)? (e) At \(\alpha = 0.01\), what is the largest sample mean that would let you reject \(H_0\)? (f) A colleague reruns the analysis, gets p = 0.064 and calls the result not significant. What did they do differently, and who is right?

Show solution

(a) The team wants to establish that the mean is below 40, so its negation goes in the null. \(H_0: \mu = 40\) (meaning \(\mu \ge 40\)) and \(H_1: \mu < 40\). If the test rejects \(H_0\), that's evidence for the claim, while failing to reject would prove nothing (HT p.4). The direction was stated before the data were collected, so a one-sided test is legitimate (HT p.5). This mirrors HW5 Problem 2.

(b) \(\sigma\) is unknown and \(n = 16\) is small, so use t with \(n - 1 = 15\) degrees of freedom (HT p.11).

\[ \frac{s}{\sqrt n} = \frac{6}{\sqrt{16}} = \frac{6}{4} = 1.5, \qquad t = \frac{\bar x - \mu_0}{s/\sqrt n} = \frac{37 - 40}{1.5} = -2.0. \]

For a one-sided test in the lower direction, reject if \(t < -t[0.05,\,15] = -1.753\). Since \(-2.0 < -1.753\), reject \(H_0\). At the 5% level the data support a mean below 40 µs.

(c) On the 15 df row, 2.0 falls between \(t[0.05,\,15] = 1.753\) and \(t[0.025,\,15] = 2.131\). So the one-sided p-value is between 0.025 and 0.05 (software gives 0.032). The p-value is the chance of a statistic at least this extreme if \(H_0\) is true (HT p.13).

(d) Now the cutoff is \(-t[0.01,\,15] = -2.602\), and \(-2.0\) isn't beyond it, so don't reject. That matches (c), since \(p > 0.025 > 0.01\).

(e) Rejection needs \((\bar x - 40)/1.5 \le -2.602\), so

\[ \bar x \le 40 - 2.602 \times 1.5 = 40 - 3.903 = 36.097 \text{ µs}. \]

Any sample mean of about 36.1 µs or less would reject at the 1% level. This is HW5 Problem 1 part 3 with t instead of z.

(f) The colleague used a two-sided test, which doubles the one-sided p-value, so their p is between 0.05 and 0.10 (software gives 0.064). The team is right here, because the direction was fixed by the claim before any data existed. The trap runs the other way. Picking one-sided after seeing which way the data went is a two-sided test at double the \(\alpha\) in disguise (HT p.5). Whichever test you use, also report the effect, a drop of about 3 µs, with its interval (HT p.16, HT p.18). The one-sided 95% upper bound is \(37 + 1.753 \times 1.5 = 39.63\) µs, which is below 40, matching the rejection in (b).

B6. Paired comparison. Two routing algorithms are run on the same six injected workloads, and the average end-to-end delay (ms) is recorded for each. Can you say with 95% confidence that algorithm A gives lower delay than B?

Workload123456
A423551286047
B413754316553
Show solution

Each workload is measured under both algorithms, so the data are paired and you analyze the six differences (Comp p.5). Take \(d_i = B_i - A_i\), so a positive \(d\) favors A.

Workload123456
\(d = B - A\)−123356
\(d - \bar d\)−4−10023
\((d - \bar d)^2\)1610049
\[ \bar d = \frac{-1 + 2 + 3 + 3 + 5 + 6}{6} = \frac{18}{6} = 3, \qquad s_d^2 = \frac{30}{5} = 6, \qquad \frac{s_d}{\sqrt n} = \sqrt{\frac{6}{6}} = 1. \]

The paired interval is a one-sample t interval on the differences with \(n - 1 = 5\) degrees of freedom, since df counts pairs (Comp p.9).

\[ \bar d \pm t[0.025,\,5]\,\frac{s_d}{\sqrt n} = 3 \pm 2.571(1) = (0.429,\ 5.571) \text{ ms}. \]

The interval excludes 0 and lies entirely above it, so yes, with 95% confidence A has lower delay than B, by somewhere between 0.43 and 5.57 ms. The mean delay under A is \(263/6 \approx 43.8\) ms, so that's roughly a 7% improvement with a wide range around it.

As a test, \(H_0: \mu_d = 0\) and \(H_1: \mu_d > 0\), with \(t = \bar d/(s_d/\sqrt n) = 3/1 = 3.0\). On the 5 df row, 3.0 lies between 2.571 and 3.365, so the one-sided p-value is between 0.01 and 0.025 and the two-sided one is between 0.02 and 0.05 (software gives 0.030 two-sided). Both reject at 0.05.

Pairing matters here. Delays range from 28 to 65 ms across workloads, far more than the 3 ms effect. An unpaired Welch test on these same 12 numbers would leave that workload spread in the standard error and give \(t \approx 0.43\) and \(p \approx 0.68\) (computed by software, just to show the contrast), which misses the effect completely (Comp p.6-8). Note that workload 1 favors B, and that's fine, since the claim is about the mean difference. This is HW6 Problem 1 with six workloads.

B7. Unpaired comparison with Welch. CPU time (ms) is measured for different random transactions on two systems, 6 on A and 8 on B. Is System B faster than System A at the 90% confidence level?

A525746544855
B4541474450424645
Show solution

The transactions differ between systems and the counts differ, so no value of A goes with a value of B. The data are unpaired, and Welch's t is the default (Comp p.5, Comp p.12-13).

System A. The sum is 312, so \(\bar x_A = 312/6 = 52\). Deviations are 0, 5, −6, 2, −4, 3, with squares 0, 25, 36, 4, 16, 9, summing to 90.

\[ s_A^2 = \frac{90}{5} = 18, \qquad \frac{s_A^2}{n_A} = \frac{18}{6} = 3. \]

System B. The sum is 360, so \(\bar x_B = 360/8 = 45\). Deviations are 0, −4, 2, −1, 5, −3, 1, 0, with squares 0, 16, 4, 1, 25, 9, 1, 0, summing to 56.

\[ s_B^2 = \frac{56}{7} = 8, \qquad \frac{s_B^2}{n_B} = \frac{8}{8} = 1. \]

The standard error of the difference is

\[ s = \sqrt{\frac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{n_B}} = \sqrt{3 + 1} = 2. \]

The Welch degrees of freedom are (Comp p.12)

\[ \nu = \frac{\left(\frac{s_A^2}{n_A} + \frac{s_B^2}{n_B}\right)^2}{\frac{(s_A^2/n_A)^2}{n_A - 1} + \frac{(s_B^2/n_B)^2}{n_B - 1}} = \frac{(3 + 1)^2}{\frac{3^2}{5} + \frac{1^2}{7}} = \frac{16}{1.8 + 0.1429} = \frac{16}{1.9429} \approx 8.24. \]

Round down to 8, since the table has whole rows and rounding down is the safe direction. As a sanity check, \(\nu\) must land between \(\min(n_A, n_B) - 1 = 5\) and \(n_A + n_B - 2 = 12\), and 8.24 does (Comp p.12).

The 90% interval for \(\mu_A - \mu_B\) uses \(t[0.05,\,8] = 1.860\).

\[ (\bar x_A - \bar x_B) \pm t[0.05,\,8]\, s = 7 \pm 1.860(2) = 7 \pm 3.72 = (3.28,\ 10.72) \text{ ms}. \]

The interval excludes 0 and is positive, so at the 90% level A takes longer and B is faster, by somewhere between 3.3 and 10.7 ms of CPU time. As a test, \(t = 7/2 = 3.5\), which is above \(t[0.005,\,8] = 3.355\), so the one-sided p-value is below 0.005.

The variance ratio is \(18/8 = 2.25\), under 4, so the old rule of thumb would have pooled. The current advice is Welch by default, chosen before you see any p-value, because picking whichever version clears the cutoff is p-hacking (Comp p.13). This is HW6 Problem 2 with small samples.

B8. Measurement. (a) Your clock has a resolution of 1 µs and the operation you want to time takes about 2.6 µs. What can one reading return, and how big is the error? (b) Instead you time 10,000 operations in a loop and get 27,412 µs, and an empty loop of 10,000 iterations takes 1,412 µs. What's the time per operation, what's the resolution of that figure, and what assumption does it need? (c) A clock read costs 20 ns, and you bracket each operation with two reads. What fraction of the measured time is timer overhead if the operation takes 60 ns? If it takes 4 µs? (d) A benchmark runs in 80 s untraced and 86 s with two trace probes on. Alone, the probes cost 2.5 s and 2.0 s. Report the perturbation and explain why 2.5 + 2.0 isn't 6. (e) The variance between processes is \(\sigma_p^2 = 48\) ms² and between iterations inside a process is \(\sigma_i^2 = 16\) ms². With a budget of 16 measurements, find the standard error of the overall mean for 1 process × 16 iterations, 2 × 8, and 16 × 1.

Show solution

(a) One reading counts whole ticks, so it returns either 2 µs or 3 µs depending on where the operation starts relative to a tick, and never 2.6. The quantization error is up to one tick, 1 µs, which is about 38% of the thing you're measuring. That's the slides' \(X \pm \Delta\) quantization error, and it's random (CI p.9-10).

(b) Subtract the loop's own cost, then divide by the count (measurement study guide, Part B).

\[ \frac{27{,}412 - 1{,}412}{10{,}000} = \frac{26{,}000}{10{,}000} = 2.6 \text{ µs per operation}. \]

The one-tick error is now spread over 10,000 operations, so the resolution is \(1/10{,}000 = 0.0001\) µs, or 0.1 ns. The assumption is that the operation behaves the same when repeated. It often doesn't, because the loop warms the cache and the branch predictor, and the compiler may even drop a loop body whose result is unused (measurement study guide, question 3).

(c) Two reads add \(2c = 40\) ns, so the overhead fraction is \(2c/(T + 2c)\).

\[ T = 60 \text{ ns}:\ \frac{40}{60 + 40} = \frac{40}{100} = 40\%, \qquad T = 4000 \text{ ns}:\ \frac{40}{4000 + 40} = \frac{40}{4040} \approx 1\%. \]

The rule is that if an operation isn't one or two orders of magnitude slower than a clock read, you time a loop of them as in (b) instead (measurement study guide, question 4).

(d) The perturbation is \((86 - 80)/80 = 6/80 = 7.5\%\). The probes measured alone sum to 4.5 s, but together they cost 6 s, and that's not a contradiction, because perturbation isn't additive. Two probes' buffers can evict the program's working set and slow it down beyond their own cost, or the second probe can find data already cached and cost less. So you have to measure the configuration you actually ran, with and without instrumentation (Meas p.16-17).

(e) The variance of the overall mean with \(p\) processes of \(k\) iterations is \(\sigma_p^2/p + \sigma_i^2/(pk)\) (measurement study guide, Part B).

\[ 1 \times 16:\ \frac{48}{1} + \frac{16}{16} = 49, \quad \text{SE} = 7 \text{ ms} \] \[ 2 \times 8:\ \frac{48}{2} + \frac{16}{16} = 25, \quad \text{SE} = 5 \text{ ms} \] \[ 16 \times 1:\ \frac{48}{16} + \frac{16}{16} = 4, \quad \text{SE} = 2 \text{ ms} \]

Same 16 measurements, but the standard error drops from 7 ms to 2 ms. Only more processes shrink the first term, so extra iterations inside one process can't buy down the bigger source of variance. Spend the budget at the level where the variance is (Meas p.25).

Part C. Paper audit (1 question, about 15 minutes)

This is the in-class version of Problem 3 from HW4 to HW6. Read the made-up results paragraph and answer the questions under it, two or three sentences each, except C3, which needs arithmetic.

"We compare FastKV with RocksDB on a single 16-core server. For each system we ran YCSB workload A five times and report the average throughput. The first 30 seconds of each run were discarded as warm-up, and throughput was timed with gettimeofday(). All FastKV runs were done on Monday, and the RocksDB runs on Tuesday after a kernel update. FastKV reaches 118.2 Kops/s against 104.6 Kops/s for RocksDB, a 13% improvement. Figure 4 shows both means with error bars of ±1 standard deviation (2.0 for FastKV and 4.0 for RocksDB). Because the bars do not overlap, the improvement is statistically significant. We repeated the comparison on 12 more workloads, and FastKV was significantly faster (p < 0.05) on 4 of them. Averaged over all 13 workloads, FastKV's arithmetic mean throughput is 9% higher. Finally, the two systems have equivalent p99 latency (t test, p = 0.41)."

C1. What is the experimental unit, what is n, and is the headline design paired or unpaired? Could any part of it have been paired? C2. Is the error-bar argument valid? C3. Write \(H_0\) and \(H_1\) for the headline claim, then rewrite the claim as a 95% confidence interval for the difference using Welch's method. C4. Is the arithmetic mean the right summary across workloads? C5. What's wrong with "significantly faster on 4 of 12 more workloads"? C6. Name two problems with the measurement setup that no interval can catch. C7. What's wrong with the p99 sentence?

Show solution

C1. The unit is one run, so n = 5 per system, and a "run" here is a separate measurement of one system, not a matched pair (HT p.20, Comp p.4). The headline comparison is unpaired, because run \(i\) of FastKV has nothing in common with run \(i\) of RocksDB, and they weren't even on the same day. The 13-workload comparison could have been paired by workload, since both systems can run the same workload, and pairing would cancel the large differences between workloads (Comp p.5-6).

C2. No. The bars are ±1 standard deviation, which describes the spread of single runs and doesn't shrink with n, so it says nothing direct about how precisely the means are known. The overlap rule on the slides is for confidence intervals, and even then non-overlap is the only safe direction (Comp p.15). The claim needs an interval on the difference, which C3 builds. The bars are 116.2 to 120.2 and 100.6 to 108.6, so they don't overlap, but that isn't the evidence.

C3. The claim is that FastKV has higher mean throughput, so \(H_0: \mu_F = \mu_R\) and \(H_1: \mu_F > \mu_R\), one-sided (HT p.4-5). For the interval, use Welch with \(n = 5\) each (Comp p.12).

\[ \frac{s_F^2}{n_F} = \frac{2.0^2}{5} = 0.8, \qquad \frac{s_R^2}{n_R} = \frac{4.0^2}{5} = 3.2, \qquad s = \sqrt{0.8 + 3.2} = \sqrt 4 = 2. \] \[ \nu = \frac{(0.8 + 3.2)^2}{\frac{0.8^2}{4} + \frac{3.2^2}{4}} = \frac{16}{0.16 + 2.56} = \frac{16}{2.72} \approx 5.88, \text{ round down to } 5. \] \[ 13.6 \pm t[0.025,\,5]\,(2) = 13.6 \pm 2.571(2) = 13.6 \pm 5.142 = (8.46,\ 18.74) \text{ Kops/s}. \]

The rewrite reads "FastKV's mean throughput on workload A was 13.6 Kops/s higher than RocksDB's (95% CI 8.5 to 18.7 Kops/s, Welch t, 5 runs each), which is roughly 8% to 18% of RocksDB's 104.6." The interval excludes 0, so the conclusion happens to survive, but by correct reasoning instead of the bar picture (Comp p.24, Comp p.26). It's still only a statement about sampling error, and C6 is why that matters.

C4. No. Throughput is a rate, and the mean of rates for equal work should be the harmonic mean, because it equals total work over total time (Summ p.28, Summ p.32). An arithmetic mean of rates gives too much weight to the fast workloads. Better still, report the per-workload numbers so readers can pick their own summary.

C5. That's 13 tests at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with no correction. Even if FastKV made no difference anywhere you'd expect \(13 \times 0.05 = 0.65\) false positives, and the chance of at least one is \(1 - 0.95^{13}\), roughly a coin flip (HT p.26-27). Four out of 13 is more than chance alone would give, but you can't say which four are real without a correction such as Bonferroni at \(0.05/13 \approx 0.0038\) per test, or Holm (HT p.28). The paper should also report all 13 results, not just the ones that cleared 0.05 (Comp p.26).

C6. Any two of these. System and day are confounded, since every FastKV run was on Monday and every RocksDB run was on Tuesday after a kernel update. That's a systematic error that sits entirely outside the interval, and more runs would only tighten the interval around the biased difference. The fix is to interleave the two systems in randomized order on the same kernel (Meas p.20, Meas p.22-23). gettimeofday() isn't monotonic, so NTP can move it mid-run, and CLOCK_MONOTONIC is the right clock (Meas p.12). The 30-second warm-up cut is asserted, not justified, so you don't know how it was chosen or whether a steady state exists (Meas p.14).

C7. Two things. A large p-value isn't evidence of equivalence, especially with 5 runs, since the test may have had almost no power (HT p.14). And a t test compares means, while p99 is a quantile, so if the claim is about the tail the paper should compare p99s directly, for instance with a bootstrap interval on their difference (Comp p.18). An honest version would give the p99 difference with its interval and say how large a difference the experiment could have detected.

Printable notes

The exam is open notes, so there's no page limit like a cheatsheet would have. Print CS700_Midterm_Notes.pdf one-sided at 100% scale. The first pages hold every formula and decision rule from lectures 2 to 11, in the order the guide covers them. The last page holds the tables you'd normally get from a calculator, which are t critical values, the standard normal table, chi-square quantiles and square roots from 2 to 100.

Bring your own homework printouts too, since the exam questions will look like them. Click a page to open it full size.
